Entropy plateaus, transitivity and bifurcation sets for the β \beta -transformation with a hole at 0 0
Pieter Allaart, Derong Kong

Abstract
. Given β ∈ ( 1 , 2 ] \beta\in(1,2] , let T β T_{\beta} be the β \beta -transformation on the unit circle [ 0 , 1 ) [0,1) such that T β ​ ( x ) = β ​ x ( mod 1 ) T_{\beta}(x)=\beta x\pmod{1} . For each t ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) t\in[0,1) let K β ​ ( t ) K_{\beta}(t) be the survivor set consisting of all x ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) x\in[0,1) whose orbit { T β n ​ ( x ) : n ≥ 0 } \{T^{n}_{\beta}(x):n\geq 0\} never enters the interval [ 0 , t ) [0,t) . Letting ℰ β \mathscr{E}_{\beta} denote the bifurcation set of the set-valued map t ↦ K β ​ ( t ) t\mapsto K_{\beta}(t) , Kalle et al. [ Ergodic Theory Dynam. Systems , 40 (9): 2482–2514, 2020] conjectured that dim H ( ℰ β ∩ [ t , 1 ] ) = dim H K β ​ ( t ) ∀ t ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) . \dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,1]\big)=\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1). The main purpose of this article is to prove this conjecture. We do so by investigating dynamical properties of the symbolic equivalent of the survivor set K β ​ ( t ) K_{\beta}(t) , in particular its entropy and topological transitivity. In addition, we compare ℰ β \mathscr{E}_{\beta} with the bifurcation set ℬ β \mathscr{B}_{\beta} of the map t ↦ dim H K β ​ ( t ) t\mapsto\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t) (which is a decreasing devil’s staircase by a theorem of Kalle et al.), and show that, for Lebesgue-almost every β ∈ ( 1 , 2 ] \beta\in(1,2] , the difference ℰ β \ ℬ β \mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta} has positive Hausdorff dimension, but for every k ∈ { 0 , 1 , 2 , … } ∪ { ℵ 0 } k\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}\cup\{\aleph_{0}\} , there are infinitely many values of β \beta such that the cardinality of ℰ β \ ℬ β \mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta} is exactly k k . For a countable but dense subset of β \beta ’s, we also determine the intervals of constancy of the function t ↦ dim H K β ​ ( t ) t\mapsto\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t) . Some connections with other topics in dynamics, such as kneading invariants of Lorenz maps and the doubling map with an arbitrary hole, are also discussed.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 37B10, 28A78; Secondary: 68R15, 26A30, 37E05, 37B40

1.
Introduction

Let
1<\beta\leq 2
and define the map
T_{\beta}:[0,1)\to[0,1)
by
T_{\beta}(x):=\beta x\!\!\mod 1
. Given a number
t\in(0,1)
, let

K_{\beta}(t):=\{x\in[0,1):T_{\beta}^{n}(x)\geq t\ \forall n\geq 0\}.

Up to countably many points,
K_{\beta}(t)
is the
survivor set
of the open dynamical system
(T_{\beta},O)
with the
hole

O=(0,t)
. Such open dynamical systems were considered first by Urbański
[
33
,
34
]
, who proved, among many other things, that the map
\eta_{2}:t\mapsto\dim_{H}K_{2}(t)
is a decreasing devil’s staircase. This result was extended more recently by Kalle
et al.

[
22
]
to all
\beta\in(1,2]
.

Other authors have studied the
\beta
-transformation with a hole. For instance, Glendinning and Sidorov
[
19
]
considered the doubing map
T_{2}
with an arbitrary hole
(a,b)
and proved several results about the size of the survivor set. Their work was partially extended by Clark
[
12
]
, who characterized for the general
\beta
-transformation with
\beta\in(1,2]
the holes
(a,b)
with an uncountable survivor set. Carminati and Tiozzo
[
10
]
showed that the local Hölder exponent of
\eta_{2}
at any bifurcation point
t
is precisely
\eta_{2}(t)
. In a recent paper
[
4
]
, the present authors determined for each
\beta\in(1,2]
the critical value

(1.1)

\tau(\beta):=\min\{t>0:\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)=0\}

and showed that the functon
\beta\mapsto\tau(\beta)
has an infinite set of discontinuities.

Note that the set-valued map
t\mapsto K_{\beta}(t)
is non-increasing. We define the
bifurcation set

(1.2)

\mathscr{E}_{\beta}:=\{t\in[0,1):K_{\beta}(t^{\prime})\neq K_{\beta}(t)\ \forall\,t^{\prime}>t\}.

It was shown by Urbański
[
34
]
that

\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{2}\cap[t,1]\big)=\dim_{H}K_{2}(t)\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1).

Kalle
et al.

[
22
]
investigated the topological structure of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
, and conjectured that a similar identity should hold for all
\beta\in(1,2]
, namely,

(1.3)

\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,1]\big)=\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1).

Baker and Kong
[
7
]
proved the conjecture for the special case when
\beta
is a
multinacci number
, i.e. the positive root of
x^{n+1}=x^{n}+x^{n-1}+\dots+x+1
, where
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The main purpose of this paper is to prove the conjecture for all
\beta\in(1,2]
.

Theorem 1.1
.

The equation (
1.3
) holds for all
\beta\in(1,2]
.

The identity (
1.3
) is an instance of the interplay between the “parameter space” (in this case,
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
) and the “dynamical space” (in our case
K_{\beta}(t)
) which was first observed by Douady
[
15
]
in the context of dynamics of real quadratic polynomials. Theorems of this type have frequently occurred in the literature in a variety of settings. For instance, Tiozzo
[
32
]
considers for
c\in\mathbb{R}
the set of angles of external rays which “land” on the real slice of the Mandelbrot set to the right of
c
(parameter space) and the set of external angles which land on the real slice of the Julia set of the map
z\mapsto z^{2}+c
(dynamical space), showing that these two sets have the same Hausdorff dimension. More recently, Carminati and Tiozzo
[
11
]
proved an identity analogous to (
1.3
) in the setting of continued fractions, and the present authors proved a similar result in the context of densities of Cantor measure (see
[
5
]
).

Since the map
\eta_{\beta}:t\mapsto\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
is a decreasing devil’s staircase, it is natural to consider also the
dimension bifurcation set

\mathscr{B}_{\beta}:=\left\{t\geq 0:\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t^{\prime})<\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)~\forall t^{\prime}>t\right\},\qquad\beta\in(1,2].

Clearly
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
. As noted in
[
22
]
, the inclusion is proper for most values of
\beta
, since
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
typically has isolated points whereas
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
does not. However, Baker and Kong
[
7
]
showed that
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}=\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
when
\beta
is a multinacci number. We extend this result here as follows:

Theorem 1.2
.

The difference
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
has positive Hausdorff dimension for Lebesgue-almost every
\beta\in(1,2]
. On the other hand, for each
k\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}\cup\{\aleph_{0}\}
, there are infinitely many values of
\beta
such that
|\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}|=k
. However, there is no
\beta\in(1,2]
such that
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
is uncountable but of zero Hausdorff dimension.

Regarding the local dimension of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
and
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
we can prove the following.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\beta\in(1,2]
.

(i)

For each
t\in\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap(t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon)\big)=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,t+\varepsilon)\big)=\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t).

(ii)

If
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic, then for each
t\in\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
, we also have

\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{B}_{\beta}\cap(t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon)\big)=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{B}_{\beta}\cap[t,t+\varepsilon)\big)=\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t).

Statement (i) is an easy consequence of Theorem
1.1
, but (ii) will follow only after we prove several other results. Therefore, we defer the proof until the end of the paper. At present, we do not know how to compute the local dimension of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
at points of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
. Note that, since
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
and
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
are defined as
right
bifurcation sets, it is much less clear how to compute their
left
local dimension functions.

We prove the main theorems above using tools from symbolic dynamics. A critical role is played here by the
greedy expansion

b(t,\beta)
, defined as the lexicographically largest expansion of the number
t
in base
\beta
, and by the
quasi-greedy expansion

\alpha(\beta)
of 1 in base
\beta
, defined as the lexicographically largest
\beta
-expansion of 1 not ending in
0^{\infty}
.

We first define the symbolic equivalent of
K_{\beta}(t)
, that is,

\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t):=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:b(t,\beta)\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\prec\alpha(\beta)\ \forall n\geq 0\},

where
\sigma
denotes the left shift map on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Note that
K_{\beta}(t)=\pi_{\beta}(\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t))
, where
\pi_{\beta}:\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{R}
is the projection map given by

\pi_{\beta}((d_{i})):=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{d_{i}}{\beta^{i}}.

It is convenient to slightly enlarge the set
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t)
and consider the closed set

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t):=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:b(t,\beta)\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\ \forall n\geq 0\}.

Clearly
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
, and the difference
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)\backslash\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t)
is at most countable. The point is that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
is always a subshift, hence we can apply theorems from symbolic dynamics. It was shown in
[
22
]
that

\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)=\frac{h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))}{\log\beta}\qquad\forall\beta\in(1,2],\quad\forall t\in(0,1),

where for a subshift
\mathcal{X}
of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
h(\mathcal{X})
denotes the
(topological) entropy
of
\mathcal{X}
, that is,

h(\mathcal{X}):=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\frac{\log\#\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathcal{X})}{n}}.

Here
\mathcal{B}_{n}(\mathcal{X})
is the set of all words of length
n
occurring in some sequence in
\mathcal{X}
, and
\#B
denotes the number of elements of the finite set
B
.

The reader may observe that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
is a special case of the subshifts

(1.4)

\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}:=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\mathbf{a}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{b}}\ \forall\,n\geq 0\},

where
\mathbf{a}
and
{\mathbf{b}}
are sequences in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. These subshifts have many important applications in dynamical systems and number theory. They arose in the 1990s in connection with kneading sequences of Lorenz maps
[
17
,
20
,
21
]
; see the next subsection. The symmetric case
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},\tilde{\mathbf{a}}}
, where
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
is the
reflection
of
\mathbf{a}
obtained by interchanging zeros and ones, plays a prominent role in the study of unique expansions in non-integer bases (see
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
13
,
14
,
16
,
18
,
23
]
). The general subshifts
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
were studied in detail by Labarca and Moreira
[
25
]
and more recently by Komornik, Steiner and Zou
[
24
]
. Several of our results here have new implications for these subshifts.

The complement of the bifurcation set
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
consists of intervals on which
h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
(and hence
\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
) is constant. For
\beta=2
these intervals, called
entropy plateaus
, were characterized by Nilsson
[
29
]
. Say a finite
0-1
word
\mathbf{w}
is
Lyndon
if it is aperiodic and lexicographically smallest among all its cyclic permutations. Nilsson proved that the plateaus of
\eta_{2}:t\mapsto h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{2}(t))
are precisely the intervals
[\pi_{2}(\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}),\pi_{2}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})]
, as
\mathbf{w}
ranges over all Lyndon words. It is easy to see that these intervals are pairwise disjoint.

For
1<\beta<2
the situation is more complex. First of all the sequences
\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}
and
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
may not be valid greedy
\beta
-expansions. We say a Lyndon word
\mathbf{w}
is
\beta
-Lyndon
if
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}=b(t,\beta)
for some
t\in[0,1)
. By Lemma
2.2
below this is the case if and only if
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta)
for all
n\geq 0
. If
\mathbf{w}
is a
\beta
-Lyndon word, we call the interval
[\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}),\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})]
a
\beta
-Lyndon interval
; see
[
7
, Definition 1.2]
. Furthermore, for each
\beta
-Lyndon word
\mathbf{w}
we define the
extended
\beta
-Lyndon interval
(EBLI for short) by

I_{\mathbf{w}}:=\begin{cases}[\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}),\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})]&\mbox{if $\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\succ\mathbf{w}^{\infty}$ for all $n\geq 0$},\\
[\pi_{\beta}\big(\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}\big),\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})]&\mbox{otherwise},\end{cases}

where in the second case,
m:=\min\{n\geq 1:\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\}
, and for any word
\mathbf{w}=w_{1}\dots w_{n}
ending in 1,
\mathbf{w}^{-}:=w_{1}\dots w_{n-1}0
. In general, the EBLIs need not be disjoint. However, we will show that if two EBLIs intersect then one contains the other. Our last main result is the following generalization of Nilsson’s theorem:

Theorem 1.4
.

Assume
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic. Then the plateaus of
\eta_{\beta}:t\mapsto h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
(hence of
t\mapsto\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
) are precisely
[\tau(\beta),1)
and the maximal (with respect to set inclusion) EBLIs in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
.

We will prove this theorem in Section
14
, where we also indicate how to determine whether a given EBLI is maximal.

Since every subshift
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
is essentially (up to a countable set) of the form
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
for some parameter pair
(\beta,t)
(see Subsection
1.1
below), Theorem
1.4
also gives the entropy plateaus for the map
\mathbf{a}\mapsto h(\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}})
for fixed
{\mathbf{b}}
, or, by symmetry, for the map
{\mathbf{b}}\mapsto h(\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}})
for fixed
\mathbf{a}
. For completeness, we state a precise result for the former:

Corollary 1.5
.

Let
{\mathbf{b}}\succ 10^{\infty}
be a sequence in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
be the lexicographically largest sequence less than or equal to
{\mathbf{b}}
such that
0^{\infty}\prec\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
for all
n\geq 0
. Then
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}=\alpha(\beta)
for some
\beta\in(1,2]
, and if
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
is eventually periodic, the entropy plateaus of
\mathbf{a}\mapsto h(\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}})
with strictly positive entropy value are the lexicographic intervals

\tilde{I}_{\mathbf{w}}:=\begin{cases}[\mathbf{w}^{-}{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}^{\infty}]&\mbox{if}\ I_{\mathbf{w}}=[\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}),\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})],\\
[\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty},\mathbf{w}^{\infty}]&\mbox{if}\ I_{\mathbf{w}}=[\pi_{\beta}\big(\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}\big),\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})],\end{cases}

as
\mathbf{w}
ranges over all
\beta
-Lyndon words such that
I_{\mathbf{w}}\subseteq[0,\tau(\beta)]
.

Note that the symbolic plateaus in the first case extend a bit further to the left, due to the fact that no sequence in
[\mathbf{w}^{-}{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},\mathbf{w}0^{\infty})
is a greedy
\beta
-expansion. Further, in the above corollary we did not describe the
0
-plateau (that is, the plateau with entropy value
0
) of
\mathbf{a}\mapsto h(\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}})
, as doing so requires some notation to be developed later; see Remark
14.9
. In any case, the
0
-plateau was already determined previously in
[
25
]
; see also
[
24
]
.

In order to prove the identity (
1.3
), it is necessary to better understand the dynamics of the subshifts
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
. We say a subshift
\mathcal{X}
of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is
(topologically) transitive
1
1

1

Our definition is slightly stronger than the usual one, which requires that for any two finite words
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{w}
in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})
, there is a word
\mathbf{v}
such that
\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})
. However, it is possible to show that for the subshifts
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
, the two definitions are equivalent. We adopt the stronger definition here for convenience.
if for any word
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})
and any sequence
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}
there is a word
\mathbf{v}
such that
\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}
.
Here
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})
denotes the
language
of
\mathcal{X}
; that is, the set of all finite words occurring in sequences in
\mathcal{X}
. Unfortunately,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
may fail to be transitive; in that case, we try to identify a subshift of full entropy that contains the sequence
b(t,\beta)
. Even this is not always possible; in Section
10
we will identify so-called
non-transitivity windows
, i.e., intervals inside of which no subshift of full entropy containing the sequence
b(t,\beta)
exists. What comes to the rescue in that situation is that the entropy
h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
is constant on these intervals. (The closures of these non-transitivity windows are in fact EBLIs.)

The topological structure of the subshift
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
depends very heavily on
\beta
(and, to a lesser extent, on
t
). We decompose the interval
(1,2]
into countably many disjoint subsets which are of four essentially different types:
basic intervals
, the
exceptional set
, the set of
finitely renormalizable
\beta
, and the set of
infinitely renormalizable
\beta
. Different sections of the paper deal with different parts of this decomposition. In the course of this work, we completely characterize transitivity for all points
t
whose greedy
\beta
-expansion is purely periodic. Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is a subshift of the
\beta
-shift

(1.5)

\Sigma_{\beta}:=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\ \forall n\geq 0\},

and therefore, this paper also characterizes transitivity for a large class of subshifts of
\beta
-shifts. We are not aware of any earlier work of this nature.

1.1.
Connection with kneading sequences of Lorenz maps

The symbolic survivor set
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t)
and the subshift
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
are closely related to the set of kneading sequences of Lorenz maps. Below we follow roughly the notion and terminology of Hubbard and Sparrow
[
21
]
. A function
f:[0,1]\to[0,1]
is called a
Lorenz map
if there is a point
c\in(0,1)
such that
f
is continuous and strictly increasing on
[0,c)
and
(c,1]
,
f(c-)=1
and
f(c+)=0
. Such maps arise in the study of a geometric model of the Lorenz differential equations. The function
T_{\beta}
for
\beta\in(1,2]
is a Lorenz map, with
c=1/\beta
. For a point
x\in[0,1]
that is not a pre-image of
c
, the
kneading sequence
of
x
is the sequence
\mathbf{k}_{f}(x)=\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}\dots\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
given by

\varepsilon_{n}=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{if $f^{n-1}(x)<c$},\\
1&\mbox{if $f^{n-1}(x)>c$}.\end{cases}

If
x
is a pre-image of
c
, we define two kneading sequences
\mathbf{k}_{f}^{+}(x)
and
\mathbf{k}_{f}^{-}(x)
by

\mathbf{k}_{f}^{+}(x):=\lim_{y\searrow x}\mathbf{k}_{f}(y),\qquad\mathbf{k}_{f}^{-}(x):=\lim_{y\nearrow x}\mathbf{k}_{f}(y),

where
y
runs through points which are not pre-images of
c
, and the limits are with respect to the product topology on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. For
f=T_{\beta}
,
\mathbf{k}_{f}^{+}(x)
is precisely the greedy expansion of
x
in base
\beta
, whereas
\mathbf{k}_{f}^{-}(x)
is the quasi-greedy expansion. The
kneading invariant
of a Lorenz map
f
is the pair
(\mathbf{a}(f),{\mathbf{b}}(f))
given by
\mathbf{a}(f)=\mathbf{k}_{f}^{+}(0)
and
{\mathbf{b}}(f)=\mathbf{k}_{f}^{-}(1)
. For
f=T_{\beta}
, note that
\mathbf{a}(f)=0^{\infty}
because
T_{\beta}(0)=0
, whereas
{\mathbf{b}}(f)=\alpha(\beta)
.

Hubbard and Sparrow
[
21
]
consider
topologically expansive
Lorenz maps, a relatively weak notion of expansive which is equivalent to the set of pre-images of
c
being dense in
[0,1]
. The map
T_{\beta}
is topologically expansive, as is any Lorenz map with
C^{1}
branches and derivative bounded above 1. Hubbard and Sparrow prove that a pair
(\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}})
of sequences in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is the kneading invariant of some topologically expansive Lorenz map if and only if

(1.6)

\mathbf{a}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{a})\prec{\mathbf{b}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathbf{a}\prec\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{b}})\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{b}}\qquad\forall\,n\geq 0.

Moreover, the corresponding map
f
is unique up to conjugacy. Now observe that, for any pair of sequences
(\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}})
satisfying (
1.6
), there is a parameter pair
(\beta,t)\in(1,2]\times[0,1)
such that
\mathbf{a}=b(t,\beta)
and
{\mathbf{b}}=\alpha(\beta)
. (This follows from Lemmas
2.1
and
2.2
below.) By the second main result of
[
21
]
, the set of all (upper or lower) kneading sequences of points in
[0,1]
for the map
f
is then precisely
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
.

Vice versa, suppose
\mathbf{a}
and
{\mathbf{b}}
are two
0-1
sequences beginning with
0
and
1
, respectively, and consider the set
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
from (
1.4
).
If
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}\neq\emptyset
, then there is a pair of sequences
(\mathbf{a}^{\prime},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})
such that
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}=\Sigma_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}
and

(1.7)

\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{a}^{\prime})\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}\qquad\forall\,n\geq 0.

(Simply take
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}:=\min\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
and
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}:=\max\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
.) Labarca and Moreira
[
25
]
call the set of pairs
(\mathbf{a}^{\prime},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})
satisfying (
1.7
) the
lexicographical world
, and investigate it in detail. They show moreover that, if
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
is uncountable, then there is a pair
(\tilde{\mathbf{a}},\tilde{{\mathbf{b}}})
of sequences satisfying the kneading inequalities (
1.6
) such that
\Sigma_{\tilde{\mathbf{a}},\tilde{{\mathbf{b}}}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}\backslash\Sigma_{\tilde{\mathbf{a}},\tilde{{\mathbf{b}}}}
is countable. Namely, let
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
be the smallest, and
\tilde{{\mathbf{b}}}
be the largest condensation point of
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
; see
[
25
, p. 689]
. (Recall that a point
x
is a
condensation point
of a set
A
in a topological space if every neighborhood of
x
contains uncountably many points of
A
.) The sequences
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
and
\tilde{{\mathbf{b}}}
are well defined and have the required properties because the set of condensation points of
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
, like
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
itself, is closed and invariant under
\sigma
. While this characterization of
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}
and
\tilde{{\mathbf{b}}}
is somewhat abstract, it is also not too difficult to devise an algorithm for their construction.

From these observations, we conclude that

(i)

For each pair
(\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}})
such that
\mathbf{a}
begins with
0
and
{\mathbf{b}}
begins with
1
, there is a parameter pair
(\beta,t)
such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
is countable;

(ii)

For each pair
(\beta,t)
such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
is uncountable, there is a parameter pair
(\beta^{\prime},t^{\prime})
such that
(\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}):=(b(t,\beta),\alpha(\beta))
satisfies (
1.6
),

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t),\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})\ \mbox{is countable}.

Thus, loosely speaking, each
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
is almost realized as
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
for some pair
(\beta,t)
, and each set
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
is almost the set of kneading sequences for some topologically expansive Lorenz map, where “almost” here means, up to a countable set.

Labarca and Moreira
[
25
]
give a further analysis of the sets
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
. For instance, they (implicitly) determine for a given sequence
\mathbf{a}
the critical
{\mathbf{b}}
at which
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
becomes uncountable, and show that when
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
is uncountable, it has positive entropy and hence positive Hausdorff dimension. More recently, their results have been generalized by Komornik, Steiner and Zou
[
24
]
. Both of these works are also very closely related to the recent paper
[
4
]
by the present authors.

1.2.
Connection with the doubling map with a general hole

In 2015, Glendinning and Sidorov
[
19
]
considered the doubling map
T_{2}
on the circle
[0,1)
with an arbitrary hole
(a,b)
, and determined for which pairs
(a,b)
with
0\leq a<b<1
the survivor set

K_{2}(a,b):=\{x\in[0,1):T_{2}^{n}(x)\not\in(a,b)\ \forall\,n\geq 0\}

is uncountable. Replacing the numbers
a
and
b
with their greedy binary expansions
\mathbf{a}
and
{\mathbf{b}}
, respectively, the set
K_{2}(a,b)
can be identified with

\Omega_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}:=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{a}\ \mbox{or}\ \sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\succcurlyeq{\mathbf{b}}\ \forall\,n\geq 0\}.

Since it is easy to see that
\Omega_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
is uncountable when
\mathbf{a}
and
{\mathbf{b}}
have the same first digit, Glendinning and Sidorov focused on the case when
\mathbf{a}
begins with
0
and
{\mathbf{b}}
begins with
1
, say
\mathbf{a}=0\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{b}}=1{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
. It can further be assumed that
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
begin with
1
and
0
, respectively, since else
\Omega_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
consists of only the two points
0^{\infty}
and
1^{\infty}
. Now for such
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
, we have the relationship

(1.8)

\Omega_{0\mathbf{a}^{\prime},1{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}=\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}0^{n}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\cup\bigcup_{n=0}^{\infty}1^{n}\Sigma_{{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\cup\{0^{\infty},1^{\infty}\}

(see
[
24
, Theorem 2.5 (vi)]
). Hence
\Omega_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
has the same cardinality and entropy as
\Sigma_{{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}
, except in trivial cases where the latter is finite and the former countably infinite. We can thus relate the set
\Omega_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
to a symbolic survivor set
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
for a suitable pair
(\beta,t)
, as explained in the previous subsection.

1.3.
Organization of the paper

After developing some notation and conventions, Section
2
introduces the main building blocks of this article: Farey words and substitutions on such words. Associated with these concepts are several important sets, not least of which is the set
E
of bases
\beta
for which
\alpha(\beta)
is
Sturmian
, i.e. aperiodic and balanced. Here we also define three collections of intervals:
basic intervals
,
Farey intervals
, and (higher order)
Lyndon intervals
arising from the substitutions on Farey words. We see that the interval
(1,2]
is decomposed into several pieces, namely the set
E
; finitely renormalizable
\beta
’s; infinitely renormalizable
\beta
’s; and the basic intervals.

In Section
3
we state and prove a useful characterization of the bifurcation set
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
. Section
4
then outlines the main ideas of the proofs of our results; we state a more technical theorem whose proof takes up much of the rest of the paper, and use it to prove Theorem
1.1
.

Section
5
deals with the simplest case, when
\beta
lies in the above-mentioned set
E
. (In fact we include here also the left endpoints of first-order basic intervals.) We show that here the subshift
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive for every
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]
, and that the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,1-1/\beta]
. Section
6
then proves the same facts for the right endpoints of first-order basic intervals, which require a somewhat separate argument but behave much more like points in
E
than like points in the interiors of basic intervals.

Section
7
treats the case of finitely renormalizable
\beta
; that is, those
\beta
’s that can be renormalized to a point of
E
in a finite number of steps. In this case we show inductively that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive only for
t_{R}
below a certain threshold, but for
t_{R}
above this threshold there still exists a transitive subshift of full entropy containing the sequence
b(t_{R},\beta)
. We show furthermore that the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals remain dense in this case.

Section
8
considers the cardinality of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
when
\beta
is an endpoint of a basic interval or one of the special Lyndon intervals; the main result here is a crucial element of the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Section
9
deals with the infinitely renormalizable case; the results are much like those in Section
7
, but the induction argument proceeds slightly differently.

Sections
10
,
11
and
12
, the most complex of the paper, deal with the interiors of basic intervals. Section
10
introduces a collection of intervals of
t
-values called
non-transitivity windows
, inside of which
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is not transitive and does not contain a transitive subshift of full entropy containing the sequence
b(t_{R},\beta)
. These intervals are generated by a sequence
(\mathbf{v}_{k})
of Lyndon words which are extracted from the sequence
\alpha(\beta)
. Most of this section is devoted to proving several necessary facts about these words. Section
11
then proves the main result, which is that (for
\beta
in a first-order basic interval),
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive if and only if
t_{R}
does not lie in any non-transitivity window, and the entropy of
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
is constant in each non-transitivity window. Section
12
then extends this result to the higher order basic intervals, by combining the methods of the previous two sections with the renormalization technique from Section
7
.

Section
13
give a brief treatment of gaps between
\beta
-Lyndon intervals; its main purpose is to show that for certain values of
\beta
, the difference
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
is countably infinite, thus establishing part of Theorem
1.2
.

Section
14
develops several properties of extended
\beta
-Lyndon intervals (EBLIs), and proves Theorem
1.4
and Corollary
1.5
. Finally, Section
15
gives the proofs of the remaining theorems.

1.4.
List of notation

•

T_{\beta}(x):=\beta x\!\!\mod 1
is the
\beta
-transformation on
[0,1)

•

K_{\beta}(t):=\{x\in[0,1):T_{\beta}^{n}(x)\geq t\ \forall n\geq 0\}

•

\pi_{\beta}((c_{i})):=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}c_{i}\beta^{-i},\quad(c_{i})\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}

•

\alpha(\beta)
is the quasi-greedy expansion of
1
in base
\beta

•

b(t,\beta)
is the greedy expansion of
t
in base
\beta

•

\sigma:\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is the left shift map

•

\Sigma_{\beta}:=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\ \forall n\geq 0\}
is the
\beta
-shift

•

\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}:=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\mathbf{a}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{b}}\ \forall n\geq 0\}

•

\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t):=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:b(t,\beta)\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\prec\alpha(\beta)\ \forall n\geq 0\}

•

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t):=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:b(t,\beta)\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\ \forall n\geq 0\}

•

h(\mathcal{X})
is the topological entropy of the subshift
\mathcal{X}

•

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})
is the language of the subshift
\mathcal{X}
; i.e. the set of all finite words that occur in some sequence in
\mathcal{X}
.

•

\mathscr{E}_{\beta}:=\{t\in[0,1):K_{\beta}(t^{\prime})\neq K_{\beta}(t)\ \forall\,t^{\prime}>t\}

•

\mathscr{E}_{\beta}^{+}:=\{t\in[0,1):T_{\beta}^{n}(t)\geq t\ \forall n\geq 0\}=\mathscr{E}_{\beta}

•

\mathscr{B}_{\beta}:=\left\{t\geq 0:\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t^{\prime})<\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)~\forall t^{\prime}>t\right\}

•

F^{*}
is the set of all Farey words of length at least 2

•

L^{*}
is the set of all Lyndon words of length at least 2

•

L^{*}(\beta)
is the set of all
\beta
-Lyndon words of length at least 2

•

\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
is the lexicographically largest cyclic permutation of
{\mathbf{s}}

•

w_{1}\dots w_{m}^{+}:=w_{1}\dots w_{m-1}(w_{m}+1)
if
w_{m}=0

•

w_{1}\dots w_{m}^{-}:=w_{1}\dots w_{m-1}(w_{m}-1)
if
w_{m}=1

•

\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}}
is the base such that
\alpha(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}

•

\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}
is the base such that
\alpha(\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty}

•

\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{s}}}
is the base such that
\alpha(\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{s}}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}

•

E:=(1,2]\backslash\bigcup_{s\in F^{*}}[\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}},\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}]

•

E_{L}:=(1,2]\backslash\bigcup_{s\in F^{*}}(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}},\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}]

•

U_{0}
is the substitution
0\mapsto 0
,
1\mapsto 01

•

U_{1}
is the substitution
0\mapsto 01
,
1\mapsto 1

•

\tau(\beta):=\min\{t>0:\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)=0\}

•

\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)
is the set of all right endpoints
t_{R}
of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals
[t_{L},t_{R}]
in
[0,\tau(\beta)]

•

{\mathbf{s}}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}:=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{r}})
is the substitution defined in (
2.8
)

•

\Lambda_{k}:=\left\{{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{s}}_{1}\bullet{\mathbf{s}}_{2}\bullet\cdots\bullet{\mathbf{s}}_{k}:\ {\mathbf{s}}_{i}\in F^{*}\textrm{ for any }1\leq i\leq k\right\}

•

\Lambda:=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\Lambda_{k}

•

I^{\mathbf{S}}=[\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}]
is the basic interval generated by
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda

•

J^{\mathbf{S}}=[\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{S}}}]
is the Lyndon interval generated by
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda

•

\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}):=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\ \ \forall n\geq 0\}

2.
Preliminaries

First we introduce some notation and conventions. By a
word
we mean a finite string of zeros and ones. Let
\left\{0,1\right\}^{*}
be the set of all words over the alphabet
\left\{0,1\right\}
together with the empty word
\epsilon
. For a word
{\mathbf{c}}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{*}
we denote its length by
|{\mathbf{c}}|
, and for a digit
a\in\left\{0,1\right\}
we denote by
|{\mathbf{c}}|_{a}
the number of occurrences of
a
in the word
{\mathbf{c}}
. The concatenation of two words
\mathbf{c}=c_{1}\ldots c_{m}
and
\mathbf{d}=d_{1}\ldots d_{n}
in
\left\{0,1\right\}^{*}
is denoted by
\mathbf{cd}=c_{1}\ldots c_{m}d_{1}\ldots d_{n}
. Similarly,
\mathbf{c}^{n}
denotes the
n
-fold concatenation of
\mathbf{c}
with itself, and
\mathbf{c}^{\infty}
denotes the periodic sequence with period block
\mathbf{c}
. If
\mathbf{c}=c_{1}\ldots c_{m}
with
c_{m}=0
, then we define
\mathbf{c}^{+}:=c_{1}\ldots c_{m-1}1
; and if
\mathbf{c}=c_{1}\ldots c_{m}
with
c_{m}=1
, we set
\mathbf{c}^{-}:=c_{1}\ldots c_{m-1}0
.

Throughout the paper we will use the lexicographical order ‘
\prec,\preccurlyeq,\succ
’ or ‘
\succcurlyeq
’ between sequences and words. For example, for two sequences
(c_{i}),(d_{i})\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, we say
(c_{i})\prec(d_{i})
if
c_{1}<d_{1}
, or there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
c_{1}\ldots c_{n}=d_{1}\ldots d_{n}
and
c_{n+1}<d_{n+1}
. For two words
\mathbf{c},\mathbf{d}
, we say
\mathbf{c}\prec\mathbf{d}
if
\mathbf{c}1^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{d}0^{\infty}
. Finally, for a word
\mathbf{c}
and an infinite sequence
\mathbf{d}
, we say
\mathbf{c}\prec\mathbf{d}
if
\mathbf{c}1^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{d}
, and define
\mathbf{c}\succ\mathbf{d}
similarly. Thoughout this paper, when discussing words and sequences, adjectives like
smallest
,
greater
, etc. will always mean smallest, greater, etc. with respect to the lexicographical order.

Recall that
\alpha(\beta)
is the quasi-greedy expansion of
1
in base
\beta
. The following useful result is well known (cf.
[
6
]
).

Lemma 2.1
.

The map
\beta\mapsto\alpha(\beta)
is strictly increasing and bijective from
(1,2]
to the set of sequences
(\alpha_{i})\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
satisfying

0^{\infty}\prec\sigma^{n}((\alpha_{i}))\preccurlyeq(\alpha_{i})\quad\forall n\geq 1.

We recall the following characterization of greedy
\beta
-expansions due to Parry
[
30
]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\beta\in(1,2]
. The map
t\mapsto b(t,\beta)
is an increasing bijection from
[0,1)
to

\big\{\mathbf{z}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\prec\alpha(\beta)~\forall n\geq 0\big\}.

Furthermore, the map
t\mapsto b(t,\beta)
is right-continuous everywhere in
[0,1)
with respect to the order topology in
\left\{0,1\right\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

2.1.
Farey words, Farey intervals and the set
E

Farey words have attracted much attention in the literature due to their intimate connection with rational rotations on the circle (see
[
28
, Chapter 2]
) and their one-to-one correspondence with the rational numbers in
[0,1]
(see (
2.1
) below). In the following we adopt the definition from
[
9
]
.

First we recursively define a sequence of ordered sets
F_{n},n=0,1,2,\ldots
. Let
F_{0}=(0,1)
; and for
n\geq 0
the ordered set
F_{n+1}=(\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{2^{n+1}+1})
is obtained from
F_{n}=(\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{2^{n}+1})
by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathbf{v}_{2i-1}=\mathbf{w}_{i}&\textrm{for}&1\leq i\leq 2^{n}+1,\\
\mathbf{v}_{2i}=\mathbf{w}_{i}\mathbf{w}_{i+1}&\textrm{for}&1\leq i\leq 2^{n}.\end{array}\right.

In other words,
F_{n+1}
is obtained from
F_{n}
by inserting for each
1\leq j\leq 2^{n}
the new word
\mathbf{w}_{j}\mathbf{w}_{j+1}
between the two neighboring words
\mathbf{w}_{j}
and
\mathbf{w}_{j+1}
. So,

\begin{split}&F_{1}=(0,01,1),\qquad F_{2}=(0,001,01,011,1),\\
F_{3}&=(0,0001,001,00101,01,01011,011,0111,1),\quad\dots\end{split}

Note that for each
n\geq 0
the ordered set
F_{n}
consists of
2^{n}+1
words which are listed from the left to the right in lexicographically increasing order.
We call
\mathbf{w}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{*}
a
Farey word
if
\mathbf{w}\in F_{n}
for some
n\geq 0
. We denote by
F:=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}F_{n}
the set of all Farey words, and by
F^{*}:=F\backslash\{0,1\}
the set of all Farey words of length at least two. As shown in
[
9
, Proposition 2.3]
, the set
F
can be bijectively mapped to
\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
via the map

(2.1)

\xi:F\to\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1];\quad{\mathbf{s}}\mapsto\frac{|{\mathbf{s}}|_{1}}{|{\mathbf{s}}|}.

So,
\xi({\mathbf{s}})
is the frequency of the digit
1
in
{\mathbf{s}}
.

The Farey words can also be obtained recursively via the substitutions

(2.2)

U_{0}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}0&\mapsto&0\\
1&\mapsto&01,\end{array}\right.\quad\textrm{and}\quad U_{1}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}0&\mapsto&01\\
1&\mapsto&1.\end{array}\right.

We extend the definitions of
U_{0}
and
U_{1}
to
\left\{0,1\right\}^{*}
and
\left\{0,1\right\}^{\mathbb{N}}
via homomorphism; that is,
U_{0}(c_{1}\dots c_{m})=U_{0}(c_{1})\dots U_{0}(c_{m})
, etc. Observe that the maps
U_{0}
and
U_{1}
, when viewed as functions on
\left\{0,1\right\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, are both strictly increasing with respect to the lexicographical order.

The following lemma can be deduced from
[
9
, Propositions 2.3 and 2.9]
. (See
[
4
, Section 2]
for more details.)

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}}\in F^{*}
; then one of the following holds:

(i)

{\mathbf{s}}=01
;

(ii)

{\mathbf{s}}=U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
for some Farey word
\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}\in F^{*}
;

(iii)

{\mathbf{s}}=U_{1}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
for some Farey word
\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}\in F^{*}
.

Vice versa, if
{\mathbf{s}}
is a Farey word, then both
U_{0}({\mathbf{s}})
and
U_{1}({\mathbf{s}})
are Farey.

Observe that for any word
\mathbf{w}
which begins with
0
and ends with
1
, both
U_{0}(\mathbf{w})
and
U_{1}(\mathbf{w})
begin with
0
and end with
1
. Furthermore, for any word
\mathbf{w}
, the word
U_{0}(\mathbf{w})
does not contain
11
as a subword, and
U_{1}(\mathbf{w})
does not contain
00
.

For a word
\mathbf{c}=c_{1}\ldots c_{m}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{*}
, let
\mathbb{S}(\mathbf{c})
and
\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{c})
be the smallest and largest cyclic permutations of
\mathbf{c}
, respectively; that is, the smallest and largest words among

c_{1}c_{2}\ldots c_{m},\quad c_{2}\ldots c_{m}c_{1},\quad c_{3}\ldots c_{m}c_{1}c_{2},\quad\cdots,\quad c_{m}c_{1}\ldots c_{m-1}.

The following properties of Farey words are well known (see, e.g.,
[
9
, Proposition 2.5]
).

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}}=s_{1}\ldots s_{m}\in F^{*}
. Then

(i)

\mathbb{S}({\mathbf{s}})={\mathbf{s}}
and
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})=s_{m}s_{m-1}\ldots s_{1}
.

(ii)

{{\mathbf{s}}^{-}}
is a palindrome; that is,
s_{1}\ldots s_{m-1}(s_{m}-1)=(s_{m}-1)s_{m-1}s_{m-2}\ldots s_{1}
.

We recall from
[
22
]
that a
Farey interval
is an interval
J^{\mathbf{s}}=[\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}},\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}]
defined by

\alpha(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty},\qquad\alpha(\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty},

where
{\mathbf{s}}\in F^{*}
. We define the sets

E:=(1,2]\backslash\bigcup_{s\in F^{*}}J^{\mathbf{s}}=(1,2]\backslash\bigcup_{{\mathbf{s}}\in F^{*}}[\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}},\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}]

and

(2.3)

E_{L}:=(1,2]\backslash\bigcup_{{\mathbf{s}}\in F^{*}}(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}},\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}].

We let
\overline{E}
denote the topological closure of
E
, and observe that

\overline{E}=\overline{E_{L}}={[1,2]}\backslash\bigcup_{{\mathbf{s}}\in F^{*}}(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}},\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}).

A word or sequence
\mathbf{w}
is said to be
balanced
if for any two subwords
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{v}
of
\mathbf{w}
with equal length,
\big||\mathbf{u}|_{1}-|\mathbf{v}|_{1}\big|\leq 1
. All Farey words are balanced, and any subword of a balanced word or sequence is again balanced.

Lemma 2.5
.

For each
\beta\in\overline{E}{\cap(1,2]}
, the sequence
\alpha(\beta)
is balanced.

Proof.

Write
\alpha(\beta)=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\dots
.
Since
\beta\in\overline{E}
,
\alpha(\beta)
is the component-wise limit of the sequence
(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}}_{n})^{\infty})
for some sequence
({\mathbf{s}}_{n})
of Farey words. Without loss of generality we may assume that the length of
{\mathbf{s}}_{n}
strictly increases with
n
. Then for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
there is an integer
N_{k}
such that
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k}
is a prefix of
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}}_{n})
for all
n\geq N_{k}
. Since Farey words are balanced, it follows that
\alpha(\beta)
is balanced as well.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}}
and
\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}
be Farey words and suppose
{\mathbf{s}}=U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
or
{\mathbf{s}}=U_{1}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
.

(i)

If
{\mathbf{s}}=U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
, then

0\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})=U_{0}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}))0,\qquad 0\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}=U_{0}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})^{+}),\qquad{\mathbf{s}}^{-}=U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}^{-})0.

(ii)

If
{\mathbf{s}}=U_{1}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
, then

\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})1=1U_{1}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})),\qquad\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}=1U_{1}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})^{+}),\qquad{\mathbf{s}}^{-}1=U_{1}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}^{-}).

Proof.

We prove (i); the proof of (ii) is similar.

Assume
{\mathbf{s}}=U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
. By Lemma
2.4
,
\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
is the word obtained from
\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}
by changing the first
0
to a
1
and the last
1
to a
0
. Hence
U_{0}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}))
is obtained from
U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
by replacing the first block,
U_{0}(0)=0
, with
U_{0}(1)=01
, and the last block,
U_{0}(1)=01
with
U_{0}(0)=0
. On the other hand,
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})=\mathbb{L}(U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}))
is obtained from
U_{0}(\hat{\mathbf{s}})
by replacing the first digit
0
with
1
and the last block
01
with
00
. It follows that

(2.4)

0\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})=U_{0}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}))0.

Next, since
\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
ends in a
0
and
U_{0}(1)=01=U_{0}(0)1
, by (
2.4
) we have

U_{0}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})^{+})=U_{0}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}))1=U_{0}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}))0^{+}=0\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}.

Finally,
\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}^{-}
is obtained from
\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}
by changing the last digit 1 to a 0, so
U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}^{-})
is obtained from
U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
by removing the last 1, whereas
{\mathbf{s}}^{-}
is obtained from
U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
by changing the last 1 to a 0. Thus,
{\mathbf{s}}^{-}=U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}^{-})0
.
∎

Lemma 2.7
.

If
\beta\in E_{L}
and
\alpha(\beta)\neq(10)^{\infty}
, then
\alpha(\beta)
does not end in
(01)^{\infty}
.

Proof.

Assume
\alpha(\beta)\neq(10)^{\infty}
. Note that
\alpha(\beta)
is balanced by Lemma
2.5
. So if
\alpha(\beta)
ends in
(01)^{\infty}
, then
\alpha(\beta)
cannot contain a block
11(01)^{k}1
for any
k\geq 0
, because this block has length
2k+3
and contains
k
zeros, whereas the block
(01)^{k+1}0
also has length
2k+3
but contains
k+2
zeros. This leaves only the possibility
\alpha(\beta)=11(01)^{\infty}
, but then
\beta=\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}
for
{\mathbf{s}}=01
, so
\beta\not\in E_{L}
. Hence,
\alpha(\beta)
cannot end in
(01)^{\infty}
.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\beta\in E_{L}
. Then there is a base
\hat{\beta}\in E_{L}
such that

0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))\qquad\mbox{or}\qquad 0\alpha(\beta)=U_{1}(0\alpha(\hat{\beta})).

Note that in the second case, it is possible that
\beta=\hat{\beta}
; this happens when
\beta=2
, in which case
\alpha(\beta)=\alpha(\hat{\beta})=1^{\infty}
.

Proof.

If
\alpha(\beta)=(10)^{\infty}
, then
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(1^{\infty})=U_{0}(\alpha(2))
, and
2\in E_{L}
. So assume
\alpha(\beta)\neq(10)^{\infty}
. Then
\alpha(\beta)
does not end in
(01)^{\infty}
by Lemma
2.7
.
Furthermore, since
\alpha(\beta)
is balanced by Lemma
2.5
, it either does not contain the word
00
or does not contain the word
11
.

Case 1
:
\alpha(\beta)
does not contain
11
. Then
0\alpha(\beta))=U_{0}(\mathbf{d})
for some sequence
\mathbf{d}=d_{1}d_{2}\dots\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
with
d_{1}=1
. Since
\alpha(\beta)
does not end in
0^{\infty}=U_{0}(0^{\infty})
,
\mathbf{d}
does not end in
0^{\infty}
either. We claim that
\sigma^{k}(\mathbf{d})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{d}
for all
k\geq 1
. Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We can choose
n\geq 1
so that

U_{0}(\sigma^{k}(\mathbf{d}))=\sigma^{n}(U_{0}(\mathbf{d}))=\sigma^{n}(0\alpha(\beta)),

and moreover,
\sigma^{n}(0\alpha(\beta))
begins with a
0
. Hence,

U_{0}(\sigma^{k}(\mathbf{d}))=\sigma^{n}(0\alpha(\beta))=0\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq 0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\mathbf{d}),

and since
U_{0}
is strictly increasing, it follows that
\sigma^{k}(\mathbf{d})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{d}
. Thus, by Lemma
2.1
,
\mathbf{d}=\alpha(\hat{\beta})
for some base
\hat{\beta}
. In other words,

(2.5)

0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\alpha(\hat{\beta})).

We next verify that
\hat{\beta}\in E_{L}
. Suppose, by way of contradiction, that
\hat{\beta}\in(\beta_{\ell}^{\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}},\beta_{r}^{\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}}]
for some Farey word
\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}
. That is,

(2.6)

\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})^{\infty}\prec\alpha(\hat{\beta})\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})^{+}\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}^{\infty}.

Set
{\mathbf{s}}:=U_{0}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
. Then
{\mathbf{s}}
is Farey, and since
U_{0}
is strictly increasing, by (
2.5
) and Lemma
2.6
(i) we obtain

0\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}=U_{0}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})^{\infty})\prec U_{0}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))=0\alpha(\beta)\preccurlyeq U_{0}\big(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})^{+}\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}^{\infty}\big)=0\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty},

Thus,
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}},\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}]
, contradicting that
\beta\in E_{L}
. We conclude that
\hat{\beta}\in E_{L}
.

Case 2
:
\alpha(\beta)
does not contain
00
. Then
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{1}(0\mathbf{d})
for some sequence
\mathbf{d}=d_{1}d_{2}\dots\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
with
d_{1}=1
. Since
\alpha(\beta)
does not end in
(01)^{\infty}=U_{1}(0^{\infty})
,
\mathbf{d}
does not end in
0^{\infty}
.

We next show that
\sigma^{k}(\mathbf{d})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{d}
for all
k\geq 1
.
Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\mathbf{c}\preccurlyeq 1\mathbf{c}
for any sequence
\mathbf{c}
, we may assume without loss of generality that
d_{k}=0
. We can choose
n\geq 1
so that
U_{1}(0\sigma^{k}(\mathbf{d}))=U_{1}(\sigma^{k-1}(\mathbf{d}))=\sigma^{n}(0\alpha(\beta))
, and this expression begins with a
U_{1}(0)=01
. Hence,

U_{1}(0\sigma^{k}(\mathbf{d}))=\sigma^{n}(0\alpha(\beta))=0\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq 0\alpha(\beta)=U_{1}(0\mathbf{d}).

Since
U_{1}
is strictly increasing, it follows that
\sigma^{k}(\mathbf{d})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{d}
, as desired. Thus,
\mathbf{d}=\alpha(\hat{\beta})
for some
\hat{\beta}\in(1,2]
, and then

0\alpha(\beta)=U_{1}(0\mathbf{d})=U_{1}(0\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
.

We next verify that
\hat{\beta}\in E_{L}
. Suppose, by way of contradiction, that
\hat{\beta}\in(\beta_{\ell}^{\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}},\beta_{r}^{\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}}]
for some Farey word
\hat{{\mathbf{s}}}
. That is, (
2.6
) holds.
Set
{\mathbf{s}}:=U_{1}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})
. Then
{\mathbf{s}}
is Farey, and since
U_{1}
is strictly increasing, we obtain from (
2.6
) and Lemma
2.6
(ii) that

\displaystyle 0\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}

\displaystyle=01U_{1}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})^{\infty})=U_{1}(0\mathbb{L}(\hat{{\mathbf{s}}})^{\infty})

\displaystyle\prec U_{1}(0\alpha(\hat{\beta}))=0\alpha(\beta)\preccurlyeq U_{1}(0\mathbb{L}(\hat{\mathbf{s}})^{+}\hat{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty})

\displaystyle=01U_{1}(\mathbb{L}(\hat{\mathbf{s}})^{+}\hat{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty})=0\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty}.

Hence
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}\prec\alpha(\beta)\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty}
. But then
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}},\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}]
, contradicting that
\beta\in E_{L}
. Thus,
\hat{\beta}\in E_{L}
.
∎

The last result in this subsection may be of independent interest. Recall that a
0-1
sequence is
Sturmian
if it is balanced and not eventually periodic (cf.
[
28
]
).

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
\beta\in(1,2)
.

(i)

\alpha(\beta)
is balanced if and only if
\beta\in\overline{E}
.

(ii)

\alpha(\beta)
is Sturmian if and only if
\beta\in E
.

Proof.

(i) We have already seen from Lemma
2.5
that, if
\beta\in\overline{E}
, then
\alpha(\beta)
is balanced. Conversely, suppose
\beta\not\in\overline{E}
. Then
\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}}<\beta<\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}
for some Farey word
{\mathbf{s}}=s_{1}\dots s_{m}
, and so
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}\prec\alpha(\beta)\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty}
. By Lemma
2.1
this implies that
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}
and there is an
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\alpha_{n+1}\dots\alpha_{n+m}\prec{\mathbf{s}}
, where
(\alpha_{i}):=\alpha(\beta)
. Let
k
be the smallest integer such that
\alpha_{n+k}\neq s_{k}
. Then
\alpha_{n+1}\dots\alpha_{n+k}=s_{1}\dots s_{k}^{-}=0s_{2}\dots s_{k}^{-}
. By Lemma
2.4
,
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}
is obtained from
{\mathbf{s}}
by changing the first 0 to a 1, so
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}
begins with
1s_{2}\dots s_{k}
. Thus,
\alpha_{n+1}\dots\alpha_{n+k}
has two fewer 1’s than
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k}=1s_{2}\ldots s_{k}
, and we conclude that
\alpha(\beta)
is not balanced.

(ii) If
\alpha(\beta)
is Sturmian, then it is balanced so
\beta\in\overline{E}
by part (i). But
\beta\neq\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}}
or
\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}}
for any Farey word
{\mathbf{s}}
, because
\alpha(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}})
and
\alpha(\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{s}}})
are (eventually) periodic. Thus,
\beta\in E
.

Vice versa, take
\beta\in E
. Then
\alpha(\beta)
is balanced, but we still need to show that it is not eventually periodic.
Suppose
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic. Since
\beta<2
,
\alpha(\beta)\neq 1^{\infty}
. By cyclical shifting, we may assume that the minimal period block of
\alpha(\beta)
is a Lyndon word
\mathbf{w}
. Note that
\alpha(\beta)\neq(10)^{\infty}
, since otherwise
\beta=\beta_{\ell}^{01}\notin E
. By Lemma
2.7
,
\mathbf{w}\neq 01
. Since
\alpha(\beta)
is balanced, so is
\mathbf{w}
, and therefore either
\mathbf{w}=U_{0}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})
or
\mathbf{w}=U_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})
for some Lyndon word
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
, as
\mathbf{w}
cannot contain both of the words
00
and
11
. (Note
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
is also Lyndon because
U_{0}
and
U_{1}
are strictly increasing.) If
\mathbf{w}=U_{0}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})
, then
0\alpha(\beta)=0U_{0}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
for some
\hat{\beta}\in E
, since Lemma
2.8
obviously holds also for
E
instead of
E_{L}
. But then
\alpha(\hat{\beta})
is eventually periodic with the shorter period block
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
. A similar argument holds when
\mathbf{w}=U_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})
. We can now repeat the same argument over and over again, to obtain an infinite sequence of Lyndon words
\mathbf{w}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{2},\dots
, each one shorter than the last. But this is absurd. Therefore,
\alpha(\beta)
is not eventually periodic.
∎

2.2.
The substitution operator

Definition 2.10
.

For a Lyndon word
{\mathbf{s}}\in L^{*}
, we define the substitution map
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}:\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
by

(2.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}(0^{k_{1}}1^{l_{1}}0^{k_{2}}1^{l_{2}}\dots)={\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{k_{1}-1}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{l_{1}-1}{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{k_{2}-1}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{l_{2}-1}\dots,\\
\Phi_{s}(1^{k_{1}}0^{l_{1}}1^{k_{2}}0^{l_{2}}\dots)=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{k_{1}-1}{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{l_{1}-1}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{k_{2}-1}{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{l_{2}-1}\dots,\end{split}

where
1\leq k_{i},l_{i}\leq\infty
for all
i
.
We allow one of the exponents
k_{i}
or
l_{i}
to take the value
+\infty
, in which case we ignore the remainder of the sequence. We denote the range of
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}
by
X({\mathbf{s}})
.
We define
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{r}})
for a finite word
{\mathbf{r}}
in the same way, and set
X^{*}({\mathbf{s}}):=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}\big(\{0,1\}^{*}\big)
.

The order in which the four blocks
{\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{s}}^{-},\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
and
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}
can appear in
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{r}})
is illustrated in Figure
1
.

{\mathbf{s}}
{\mathbf{s}}^{-}

\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}
Start-
1
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
Start-
0
1
1
0
0
1
1
0
1
0
0

Figure 1.
The directed graph illustrating the map
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}
.

For example,

\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}(0110^{\infty})={\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty},\qquad\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}(11100)=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{2}{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}}).

Now for any two words
{\mathbf{s}}\in L^{*}
and
{\mathbf{r}}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{*}
we define the substitution operation

(2.8)

{\mathbf{s}}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}:=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{r}}).

Example 2.11
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}}=01
and
{\mathbf{r}}=011
. Then

{\mathbf{s}}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{r}})=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}(011)={{\mathbf{s}}^{-}}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}=001101.

The following lemma collects properties of the map
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}
and the substitution operator
\bullet
. The proofs can be found in
[
4
, Section 3]
. To summarize,
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}
is increasing and commutes with the operators
\cdot^{+},\cdot^{-}
and
\mathbb{L}
; the set
L^{*}
of Lyndon words is closed under
\bullet
, and
\bullet
is associative.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}}\in L^{*}
.

(i)

The map
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}
is strictly increasing on
\left\{0,1\right\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

(ii)

For any word
{\mathbf{d}}=d_{1}\ldots d_{k}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
with
k\geq 2
, we have

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{d}}^{-})=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{d}})^{-}&\textrm{if}&d_{k}=1,\\
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{d}}^{+})=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{d}})^{+}&\textrm{if}&d_{k}=0.\end{array}\right.

(iii)

For any two sequences
{\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{d}}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, we have the implications

\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{c}})\prec{\mathbf{d}}~\forall n\geq 0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\sigma^{n}(\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{c}}))\prec\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{d}})~\forall n\geq 0

and

\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{c}})\succ{\mathbf{d}}~\forall n\geq 0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\sigma^{n}(\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{c}}))\succ\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{d}})~\forall n\geq 0.

(iv)

For any
{\mathbf{r}}\in L^{*}
, we have
{\mathbf{s}}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}\in L^{*}
and
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}}\bullet{\mathbf{r}})={\mathbf{s}}\bullet\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})
.

(v)

For any
{\mathbf{r}}\in L^{*}
and
{\mathbf{t}}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0,1\}^{*}
, we have
({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})\bullet{\mathbf{t}}={\mathbf{r}}\bullet({\mathbf{s}}\bullet{\mathbf{t}})
.

Observe that the map
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}
is not a homomorphism on
\{0,1\}^{*}
: For instance,
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}(00)={\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})\neq{\mathbf{s}}^{-}{\mathbf{s}}^{-}=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}(0)\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}(0)
. Nonetheless,
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}
is a partial homomorphism in the sense described below.

Say a finite or infinite sequence of words
{\mathbf{b}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{b}}_{n}
or
{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2},\dots
is
connectible
if for each
i
, the last digit of
{\mathbf{b}}_{i}
differs from the first digit of
{\mathbf{b}}_{i+1}
. Thus, for instance, the sequence
1101,00111
is connectible whereas the sequence
11010,0111
is not.

Lemma 2.13
.

(
[
4
, Section 3]
)

(i)

Let
{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2},\dots
be a (finite or infinite) connectible sequence of words. Then for any
{\mathbf{s}}\in L^{*}
,

\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{b}}_{1}{\mathbf{b}}_{2}\dots)=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{b}}_{1})\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{b}}_{2})\dots.

(ii)

Let
{\mathbf{s}},{\mathbf{r}}\in L^{*}
. Then
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{r}}^{\infty})=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
and
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty})=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}))^{\infty}
.

Now for
k\in\mathbb{N}
define

(2.9)

\Lambda_{k}:=\left\{{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{s}}_{1}\bullet{\mathbf{s}}_{2}\bullet\cdots\bullet{\mathbf{s}}_{k}:\ {\mathbf{s}}_{i}\in F^{*}\textrm{ for any }1\leq i\leq k\right\},

and set

\Lambda:=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}\Lambda_{k}.

If
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda_{k}
, we call
k
the
degree
of
{\mathbf{S}}
. Note that
F^{*}\subseteq\Lambda\subseteq L^{*}
by Lemma
2.12
(iv). Both inclusions are proper, e.g.
001011=01\bullet 001\in\Lambda\backslash F^{*}
and
0010111\in L^{*}\backslash\Lambda
.

We recall some terminology and facts from
[
4
]
.
For
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, we call the interval
I^{\mathbf{S}}:=[\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}]
given implicitly by

\alpha(\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\alpha(\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty},

a
basic interval
generated by the Lyndon word
{\mathbf{S}}
. We also define an interval
J^{\mathbf{S}}:=[\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{r}^{\mathbf{S}}]
, where
\beta_{r}^{\mathbf{S}}
is given by

\alpha(\beta_{r}^{\mathbf{S}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}.

The interval
J^{\mathbf{S}}
is called a
Lyndon interval
generated by
{\mathbf{S}}
.
For any
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, the intervals
J^{{\mathbf{S}}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}},{\mathbf{r}}\in F^{*}
are pairwise disjoint and contained in
J^{\mathbf{S}}\setminus I^{\mathbf{S}}
; let

(2.10)

E^{\mathbf{S}}:=(J^{\mathbf{S}}\setminus I^{\mathbf{S}})\setminus\bigcup_{{\mathbf{r}}\in F^{*}}J^{{\mathbf{S}}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}}.

It is a consequence of
[
4
, Proposition 5.4]
that
\beta\in E^{\mathbf{S}}
if and only if
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
for some
\hat{\beta}\in E
. In this case we say that
\beta
is
renormalizable
2
2

2

Our definition differs slightly from that of Hubbard and Sparrow
[
21
]
, who call the above notion
properly renormalizable
and consider
\beta\in E
to be
trivially renormalizable
.
by the word
{\mathbf{S}}
.

We further define the set

(2.11)

E_{\infty}:=\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda_{k}}J^{{\mathbf{S}}}.

These are the values of
\beta
that lie in infinitely many of the intervals
J^{{\mathbf{S}}}
, and are hence
infinitely renormalizable
. (In
[
4
]
,
E_{\infty}
is called the
infinitely Farey set
, because its elements arise from substitutions of an infinite sequence of Farey words.) The set
E_{\infty}
is uncountable but of zero Hausdorff dimension.
By
[
4
, Theorem 3]
, we have the decomposition in disjoint sets,

(2.12)

(1,2]=E\cup E_{\infty}\cup\bigcup_{{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda}E^{{\mathbf{S}}}\cup\bigcup_{{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda}I^{{\mathbf{S}}}.

In this paper, different parts of this decomposition require different analysis, with the case of
\beta
in a basic interval being the most involved.

Remark 2.14
.

Our presentation of the substitution map
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}
and its properties differs somewhat from the substitutions used by most authors, but can be seen to be equivalent. Precisely, let
\mathbf{w}_{+}
(resp.
\mathbf{w}_{-}
) be the word
{\mathbf{s}}
(resp.
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
) shifted cyclically one place to the right.
Alternatively,
\mathbf{w}_{-}
is the largest cyclic permutation of
{\mathbf{s}}
beginning with
0
, and
\mathbf{w}_{+}
is the smallest cyclic permutation of
{\mathbf{s}}
beginning with
1
; see
[
19
]
. For example, if
{\mathbf{s}}=01011
, then
\mathbf{w}_{+}=10110
and
\mathbf{w}_{-}=01101
.
If
\mathbf{y}=y_{1}y_{2}\dots
is a sequence beginning with
1
, then
0\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{y})
is a concatenation of words from
\{\mathbf{w}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{-}\}
, where the first block is
\mathbf{w}_{-}
and for each
i\geq 2
, the
i
th block is
\mathbf{w}_{-}
if
y_{i-1}=0
, and
\mathbf{w}_{+}
if
y_{i-1}=1
. Similarly, if
\mathbf{y}
is a sequence beginning with
0
, then
1\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}(\mathbf{y})
is also such a concatenation, but beginning instead with
\mathbf{w}_{+}
.

Although the approach using the words
\mathbf{w}_{+}
and
\mathbf{w}_{-}
has been the traditional way to express renormalizations (see
[
21
]
), we prefer here the map
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}
because (i) it emphasizes that the renormalization is parametrized by a single word rather than two different ones; (ii) it emphasizes the importance of Lyndon and Farey words; and (iii) it greatly facilitates operations on finite words, which are used frequently in future sections. On the other hand, our map
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}
is essentially equivalent (up to the above-mentioned cyclical shift) to the map
\rho_{r}
of Glendinning and Sidorov
[
19
]
, where
r=|{\mathbf{s}}|_{1}/|{\mathbf{s}}|
. The maps
\rho_{r}
are useful for studying the sets
\Omega_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
, but for dealing with the sets
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
we find the maps
\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}
more practical, despite their somewhat clunkier definition.

We point out also that it is possible to write the substitution maps
\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}
,
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
completely in terms of just three basic substitutions, namely

L:\begin{cases}0&\mapsto 0\\
1&\mapsto 10\end{cases},\qquad M:\begin{cases}0&\mapsto 01\\
1&\mapsto 10\end{cases},\qquad R:\begin{cases}0&\mapsto 01\\
1&\mapsto 1\end{cases},

as was done by Komornik, Steiner and Zou
[
24
]
and before that (with different notation) by Labarca and Moreira
[
25
]
.

3.
Characterization of the bifurcation set
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}

In the introduction we have followed the notation and definitions of Baker and Kong
[
7
]
. However, we point out that
K_{\beta}(t)
is slightly different from the set denoted
K_{\beta}(t)
in
[
22
]
, and as a result, the bifurcation set
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
defined in (
1.2
) is slightly different from the set called
E_{\beta}
in
[
22
]
. In fact, setting
\check{K}_{\beta}(t):=\{x\in[0,1):T_{\beta}^{n}(x)\not\in(0,t)\ \forall n\geq 0\}
, we have

(3.1)

E_{\beta}=\left\{t\in[0,1):\check{K}_{\beta}(t^{\prime})\neq\check{K}_{\beta}(t)~\forall t^{\prime}>t\right\}.

It is also convenient to define the set

\mathscr{E}_{\beta}^{+}:=\{t\in[0,1):T_{\beta}^{n}(t)\geq t\ \forall n\geq 0\}=\{t\in[0,1):t\in K_{\beta}(t)\}.

(This is the set denoted
E_{\beta}^{+}
in
[
22
]
.) The following characterization will play an important role throughout this article.

Lemma 3.1
.

For each
\beta\in(1,2]
, we have

\mathscr{E}_{\beta}=\left\{t\in[0,1):K_{\beta}(t-\varepsilon)\neq K_{\beta}(t+\varepsilon)~\forall\varepsilon>0\right\}=\mathscr{E}_{\beta}^{+}.

Proof.

If
t\in\mathscr{E}_{\beta}^{+}
, the definition of
K_{\beta}(t)
implies that
t\in K_{\beta}(t)\setminus K_{\beta}(t+\varepsilon)
for any
\varepsilon>0
, and so
t\in\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
.

Next, if
t\in\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
, then
K_{\beta}(t+\varepsilon)\neq K_{\beta}(t)
for all
\varepsilon>0
, so certainly
K_{\beta}(t-\varepsilon)\neq K_{\beta}(t+\varepsilon)
for all
\varepsilon>0
.

Finally, suppose
t\not\in\mathscr{E}_{\beta}^{+}
. Then there is an
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
T_{\beta}^{n_{0}}(t)<t
. By the continuity of
T_{\beta}^{n_{0}}
there is a sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
such that

(3.2)

T_{\beta}^{n_{0}}(x)<t-\varepsilon\quad\forall x\in[t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon].

Suppose there exists
x_{0}\in K_{\beta}(t-\varepsilon)\setminus K_{\beta}(t+\varepsilon)
. Then there is an integer
n_{1}
such that
t-\varepsilon\leq T_{\beta}^{n_{1}}(x_{0})<t+\varepsilon
. Thus,
T_{\beta}^{n_{0}+n_{1}}(x_{0})<t-\varepsilon
by (
3.2
), contradicting that
x_{0}\in K_{\beta}(t-\varepsilon)
. Hence,
K_{\beta}(t-\beta)=K_{\beta}(t+\varepsilon)
.
∎

By a similar argument it can be shown that
E_{\beta}=\left\{t\in[0,1):T_{\beta}^{n}(t)\notin(0,t)~\forall n\geq 0\right\}
. Thus,
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\subseteq E_{\beta}
, and
E_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
is at most countable. As a result, when considering questions of Hausdorff dimension the two sets may be freely interchanged. Because of this, (
1.3
) is indeed equivalent to the conjecture of Kalle
et al.

4.
The key ideas

In this section we outline the main idea of the proof of Theorem
1.1
. The key is to prove the more technical Theorem
4.4
below; its proof takes up much of the rest of the paper. First, we define the concepts appearing in the theorem.

The metric
d_{2}
on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
defined by

d_{2}((x_{i}),(y_{i})):=2^{-\inf\{i:x_{i}\neq y_{i}\}},\qquad(c_{i}),(d_{i})\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}},

induces a Hausdorff dimension on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, which we also denote by
\dim_{H}
.

Next, we recall the definition of the
\beta
-shift from (
1.5
).
It was shown recently by Li
[
26
, Theorem 1.1]
that

(4.1)

\dim_{H}\pi_{\beta}(A)=\frac{\log 2}{\log\beta}\dim_{H}A\qquad\forall\,A\subseteq\Sigma_{\beta}.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\subseteq(0,1)
be an index set. A collection
\{\mathcal{X}(t):t\in\mathcal{T}\}
is a
strictly descending collection of subshifts
if for any
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
with
t<t^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{X}(t^{\prime})
is a proper subshift of
\mathcal{X}(t)
.

Definition 4.2
.

A (finite or infinite) sequence
\mathcal{I}=(I_{1},I_{2},\dots)
of intervals is
left-progressing
if for each
k\geq 1
,
\sup I_{k+1}<\inf I_{k}
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be a subshift of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and
\mathcal{X}
a subshift of
\mathcal{Y}
. We say
\mathcal{X}
has
full entropy
in
\mathcal{Y}
if
h(\mathcal{X})=h(\mathcal{Y})
. Likewise, we say
\mathcal{X}
has
full Hausdorff dimension
in
\mathcal{Y}
if
\dim_{H}\mathcal{X}=\dim_{H}\mathcal{Y}
.

Recall from the Introduction that a
\beta
-Lyndon interval
is an interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]
determined by

b(t_{L},\beta)=\mathbf{w}0^{\infty},\qquad b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty},

where
\mathbf{w}
is a
\beta
-Lyndon word. (Recall this means
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon and
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta)
for all
n\geq 0
.)
We denote the set of all
\beta
-Lyndon words by
L^{*}(\beta)
.
We denote by
\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)
the set of all right endpoints of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
, where
\tau(\beta)
was defined in (
1.1
).

Theorem 4.4
.

For each
\beta\in(1,2]
there is a (finite or infinite, possibly empty) left-progressing sequence
\mathcal{I}
of intervals such that

(i)

For any
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)\backslash\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
has a transitive subshift
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
of full entropy and full Hausdorff dimension that contains the sequence
b(t_{R},\beta)
;

(ii)

The collection

\{\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}):t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)\backslash\textstyle{\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I}\}

is a strictly descending collection of subshifts;

(iii)

The entropy function
t\mapsto h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
is constant throughout each interval
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

Theorem
4.4
describes the most general case; for many values of
\beta
the situation is simpler. In the following sections, we will prove Theorem
4.4
for different cases of
\beta
. But first, we show how it implies Theorem
1.1
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
t\in\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap(0,1)
. Then
t
is either the right endpoint of a
\beta
-Lyndon interval, or
t
is the limit from the left of a sequence of right endpoints of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1
,
b(t,\beta)\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(b(t,\beta))\prec\alpha(\beta)
for all
n\geq 0
. If
b(t,\beta)
is periodic, say
b(t,\beta)=(b_{1}\dots b_{k})^{\infty}
where
k
is the minimal period of
b(t,\beta)
, then
b_{1}\dots b_{k}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, and hence
t
is the right endpoint of a
\beta
-Lyndon interval.

Assume now that
b(t,\beta)=b_{1}b_{2}\dots
is not periodic. Then by
[
22
, Lemma 3.5]
, there are infinitely many integers
k
such that
b_{1}\dots b_{k}
is Lyndon. But then
b_{1}\dots b_{k}
is in fact
\beta
-Lyndon, since for each
n\geq 0
there is an integer
j<k
such that

\sigma^{n}\big((b_{1}\dots b_{k})^{\infty}\big)=b_{j+1}\dots b_{k}(b_{1}\dots b_{k})^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{j}(b(t,\beta))\prec\alpha(\beta).

Since
(b_{1}\dots b_{k})^{\infty}\to b(t,\beta)
in the order topology as
k\to\infty
and
(b_{1}\dots b_{k})^{\infty}\prec b(t,\beta)
for all
k
by the assumption that
t\in\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
, it follows that
t
is the limit from the left of a sequence of right endpoints of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
, assuming Theorem
4.4
.

The proof is based roughly on the argument in the proof of
[
7
, Lemma 3.8]
, but requires several new ideas. Observe that we only need to prove the “
\geq
” inequality, since
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,1)\subseteq K_{\beta}(t)
by Lemma
3.1
. Furthermore, it suffices to establish the inequality for
t\in\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
: For arbitrary
t\in(0,1)
, let
t^{\prime}:=\inf(\mathscr{B}_{\beta}\cap[t,1])
; we then have

\displaystyle\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,1])

\displaystyle\geq\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t^{\prime},1])=\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t+)=\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t),

where the last equality follows from the continuity of
t\mapsto\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
.

Observe that each
t\in\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
can be approximated from the right by a sequence of right endpoints of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals
[t_{L}^{(n)},t_{R}^{(n)}],n\in\mathbb{N}
. (If this were not the case, then some right neighborhood
(t,t+\varepsilon)
would not contain any points of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
by Lemma
4.5
, hence
(t,t+\varepsilon)
would not contain any points of
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
. But every point of
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
is an accumulation point from the right of
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
by the continuity of
t\mapsto\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
.) We can moreover choose the sequence
(t_{R}^{(n)})
so that
\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t_{R}^{(n)})
is strictly increasing in
n
. Thus, we have

h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}^{(n)})\big)<h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))\quad\forall\,n\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}^{(n)})\big)\nearrow h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))\quad\mbox{as $n\to\infty$}.

Since the sequence
\mathcal{I}
of intervals in Theorem
4.4
is left-progressing, there is at most one point all of whose neighborhoods intersect infinitely many of the intervals in
\mathcal{I}
; if such a point exists, it lies to the left of all the intervals in
\mathcal{I}
and we denote it by
t_{0}
.

We assume first that
t\neq t_{0}
. Then, except for finitely many values of
n
, the points
t_{R}^{(n)}
do not lie in any interval from the collection
\mathcal{I}
, because
t\in\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
and entropy is constant on each interval of
\mathcal{I}
by Theorem
4.4
(iii). Hence, by Theorem
4.4
(i) it follows that for all large enough
n
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}^{(n)})
has a transitive subshift
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}^{(n)})
of full Hausdorff dimension containing the sequence
b(t_{R}^{(n)},\beta)
.

Write
\alpha(\beta)=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\dots
.
First assume
t\in\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
such that
t=t_{R}
for a
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]
generated by the
\beta
-Lyndon word
\mathbf{w}=w_{1}\dots w_{p}
, so
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
. Since the greedy expansion
b(t,\beta)
is right-continuous in
t
, we may assume that
t_{R}^{(n)}
is close enough to
t_{R}
so that
b(t_{R}^{(n)},\beta)
begins with
\mathbf{w}
. Furthermore, since
b(t_{R}^{(n)},\beta)\succ b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
, there is for each
n
a sufficiently large integer
k_{n}
such that

(4.2)

b(t_{R}^{(n)},\beta)\succ\mathbf{w}^{k_{n}}1^{\infty}.

Let

\mathcal{F}_{n}:=\left\{\mathbf{w}^{k_{n}}\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}=x_{1}x_{2}\dots\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big),x_{1}\dots x_{p}=\mathbf{w},\ \mbox{and}\ \sigma^{j}(\mathbf{x})\prec\alpha(\beta)\ \forall\,j\geq 0\right\},

and

F_{n}:=\pi_{\beta}(\mathcal{F}_{n}).

Take
\mathbf{w}^{k_{n}}\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
. First we show that

(4.3)

\sigma^{j}(\mathbf{w}^{k_{n}}\mathbf{x})\prec\alpha(\beta)\quad\forall j\geq 0.

Since
\sigma^{l}(\mathbf{x})\prec\alpha(\beta)
for all
l\geq 0
, (
4.3
) holds for
j\geq k_{n}|\mathbf{w}|
. Observing that
\mathbf{x}
begins with
\mathbf{w}
, the inequality will follow for all
j<k_{n}|\mathbf{w}|
once we prove that

(4.4)

\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbb{L}(w_{1}\ldots w_{p})\prec\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{p}.

Since
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta)
for all
n\geq 0
,
\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{w})\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{p}
. If
\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{w})=\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{p}
, then for some
i\leq p
,

(\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{p})^{\infty}=\sigma^{i}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta).

But then
\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{p}\prec\alpha_{\ell+1}\ldots\alpha_{\ell+p}
for some
\ell\geq 0
, contradicting Lemma
2.1
. Therefore, (
4.4
) holds, and thus we have (
4.3
).

Since
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)
, we have
\mathbf{x}\succcurlyeq b(t_{R}^{(n)},\beta)\succ\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
. Furthermore, by (
4.3
),
\mathbf{w}^{k_{n}}\mathbf{x}
is a greedy
\beta
-expansion. Since
\sigma^{j}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta)
for each
j\geq 0
,
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
is also a greedy
\beta
-expansion. Thus,

\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{k_{n}}\mathbf{x})\geq\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})=t_{R}.

Furthermore, using (
4.2
),

\sigma^{j}(\mathbf{x})\succcurlyeq b(t_{R}^{(n)},\beta)\succ\mathbf{w}^{k_{n}}1^{\infty}\succ\mathbf{w}^{k_{n}}\mathbf{x}\qquad\forall j\geq 0,

and
w_{j+1}\dots w_{p}\succ w_{1}\dots w_{p-j}
for all
1\leq j<p
since
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon. Hence

\sigma^{j}(\mathbf{w}^{k_{n}}\mathbf{x})\succcurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{k_{n}}\mathbf{x}\qquad\forall j\geq 0.

We conclude that

(4.5)

F_{n}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t_{R},1].

Next, observe that the condition
\sigma^{j}(\mathbf{x})\prec\alpha(\beta)\ \forall\,j\geq 0
in the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
removes at most a countable number of sequences from
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)
.
Since
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)
is transitive and
\mathbf{w}
is allowed in
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)
, there is for each sequence
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)
a word
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{z})
such that
\mathbf{w}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)
. For each finite word
\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, denote by
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{v}}
the set of all sequences
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)
such that
\mathbf{v}(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{v}
. Then

\bigcup_{\mathbf{v}}\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{v}}=\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathcal{F}_{n}\supseteq\left(\bigcup_{\mathbf{v}}\mathbf{w}^{k_{n}+1}\mathbf{v}\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{v}}\right)\backslash\mathcal{C},

where
\mathcal{C}
is a countable set. (Here our stronger definition of
transitive
is particularly convenient.) Hence,

\dim_{H}\mathcal{F}_{n}\geq\sup_{\mathbf{v}}\dim_{H}\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{v}}=\dim_{H}\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)=\dim_{H}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big),

where the last equality follows since
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)
has full Hausdorff dimension in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)
.
Since all of the sets involved are subsets of
\Sigma_{\beta}
, it follows from (
4.1
) that

\dim_{H}F_{n}=\dim_{H}\pi_{\beta}(\mathcal{F}_{n})\geq\dim_{H}\pi_{\beta}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)\right)=\dim_{H}K_{\beta}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big).

Thus, together with (
4.5
), we obtain

\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t_{R},1]\big)\geq\dim_{H}K_{\beta}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big).

Letting
t_{R}^{(n)}\searrow t_{R}
gives
\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t_{R},1]\big)\geq\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t_{R})
, in view of the continuity of
t\mapsto\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
.

Finally, for arbitrary
t\in\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
(including the accumulation point
t=t_{0}
if it exists),
t
can be approximated from the right by a sequence
\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big)
of right endpoints of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals, none of which are equal to
t_{0}
; so

\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,1]\big)\geq\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t_{R}^{(n)},1]\big)\geq\dim_{H}K_{\beta}\big(t_{R}^{(n)}\big),

and, again by the continuity of the map
t\mapsto\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
, it follows that
\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,1]\big)\geq\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
.
∎

In this proof we did not use property (ii) of Theorem
4.4
. However, it will be needed later in the proof of Theorem
1.2
. The following result illustrates the main idea.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\beta\in(1,2]
, and suppose that

(i)

For any
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
has a transitive sofic subshift
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
of full entropy;

(ii)

The collection
