\{\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}):t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)\}
is a strictly descending collection of subshifts; and

(iii)

The
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
.

Then

\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[0,\tau(\beta))=\mathscr{B}_{\beta}\cap[0,\tau(\beta)).

Proof.

We show that the plateaus of
\delta_{\beta}:t\mapsto\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
are precisely the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals. This gives the desired conclusion, since
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
intersects the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals only in their right endpoints, and the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
.

It is enough to show that if
[t_{L},t_{R}]
and
[u_{L},u_{R}]
are two distinct
\beta
-Lyndon intervals with
t_{R}<u_{R}
, then
h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})\big)>h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(u_{R})\big)
. By hypotheses (i) and (ii),
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
is sofic and transitive, and
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(u_{R})
is a proper subshift of
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
. Hence, by
[
27
, Corollary 4.4.9]
,

h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})\big)=h\big(\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})\big)>h\big(\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(u_{R})\big)=h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(u_{R})\big).

This completes the proof.
∎

As we will see later, the hypotheses of Proposition
4.6
are satisfied when
\beta\in\{\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{r}^{\mathbf{S}}\}
, for any
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
. Thus, for these values of
\beta
, any points of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
must lie to the right of
\tau(\beta)
; see Theorem
8.1
.

5.
The case
\beta\in E_{L}

We begin with the case when
\beta\in E_{L}
. Our main result in this section is:

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\beta\in E_{L}
. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive for any right endpoint
t_{R}
of a
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]
with
t_{R}<\tau(\beta)=1-(1/\beta)
. In other words, the conclusion of Theorem
4.4
holds with
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})=\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
for each
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}
.

We will prove Theorem
5.1
after developing some lemmas.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
1<\beta<2
, and
0<t<1-1/\beta
. Then for every sequence
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
there exist a word
\mathbf{u}
beginning with
0
and a word
\mathbf{v}
beginning with
1
such that
\mathbf{u}\mathbf{z},\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
. Suppose
1\mathbf{z}\not\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
. Then
1\mathbf{z}\succ\alpha(\beta)
, and hence
0\mathbf{z}\succ 0\sigma(\alpha(\beta))=b(1-1/\beta,\beta)\succ b(t,\beta)
. Therefore,
0\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
. So
\mathbf{z}
can always be extended to the left.

Let
N
be the number of consecutive
1
’s at the beginning of
\alpha(\beta)
, and
M
be the number of consecutive
0
’s at the beginning of
b(t,\beta)
. Then
N,M\geq 1
since
\beta>1
and
t<1-1/\beta<1/\beta
. Clearly, no sequence in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
can have more than
N
consecutive
1
’s or
M
consecutive
0
’s anywhere. Hence, the lemma follows.
∎

Recall that
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})
denotes the language of the subshift
\mathcal{X}
, and
\mathcal{X}
is transitive if for any word
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})
and sequence
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}
, there is a word
\mathbf{v}
such that
\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{X}
. The next definition and lemma will be used to simplify transitivity proofs in this section, by removing the word
\mathbf{u}
from consideration.

If
b(t,\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
for a Lyndon word
\mathbf{w}
, we also write
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})
instead of
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
. So

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}):=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\ \forall n\geq 0\}.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\beta\in(1,2]
and write
\alpha(\beta)=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\ldots
. We say a Lyndon word
\mathbf{w}
has
Property TL
for
\beta
if for each sequence
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})
there is a word
\mathbf{v}=v_{1}\dots v_{n}
such that

(i)

\mathbf{v}\prec\alpha_{j+1}\dots\alpha_{j+n}
for all
j\geq 0
; and

(ii)

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{i}(\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
for all
i\geq 0
, i.e.
\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\beta\in\overline{E}
, let
[t_{L},t_{R}]
be a
\beta
-Lyndon interval with
t_{R}<1-(1/\beta)
, and let
\mathbf{w}=w_{1}\dots w_{m}\in L^{*}(\beta)
such that
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
. If
\mathbf{w}
satisfies Property TL, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive.

Proof.

We first show that

(5.1)

0\sigma(\alpha(\beta))=0\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\dots\preccurlyeq\sigma^{j}(\alpha(\beta))\qquad\forall\,j\geq 0.

This is trivial for
j=0
, so assume
j\geq 1
. Suppose the contrary; then there is an index
k\geq 2
such that
0\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k-1}=\alpha_{j+1}\dots\alpha_{j+k-1}
and
\alpha_{k}=1
,
\alpha_{j+k}=0
. But then the word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k}
has two more
1
’s than the word
\alpha_{j+1}\dots\alpha_{j+k}
of the same length, contradicting that
\alpha(\beta)
is balanced. Therefore, we have (
5.1
).

Let
\mathbf{u}=u_{1}\dots u_{n}\in\mathcal{L}\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})\big)
and
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
be given. Let
j
be the smallest index such that
u_{j+1}\dots u_{n}
is a prefix of
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
, say
u_{j+1}\dots u_{n}=(w_{1}\dots w_{m})^{k-1}w_{1}\dots w_{r}
with
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq r<m
. If no such
j
exists, set
j:=n
,
r:=0
and
k:=1
. Let

\mathbf{u}^{\prime}:=u_{1}\dots u_{n}w_{r+1}\dots w_{m}=u_{1}\dots u_{j}\mathbf{w}^{k}.

Let
\mathbf{v}
be the word satisfying (i) and (ii) in Definition
5.3
for the given
\mathbf{z}
. We claim that
{\mathbf{c}}:=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
.

We check first that
\sigma^{i}({\mathbf{c}})\succcurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
for all
i\geq 0
. For
0\leq i<j
this follows from the minimality of
j
. For
j\leq i<j+km
and
m\nmid(i-j)
it follows since
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon, so
w_{l+1}\dots w_{m}\succ w_{1}\dots w_{m-l}
for all
1\leq l<m
. And for all other
i\geq j
the inequality follows from assumption (ii).

Next, we check that
\sigma^{i}({\mathbf{c}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
for all
i\geq 0
. For
0\leq i<j
, this follows since

(a)

u_{i+1}\dots u_{j}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j-i}
;

(b)

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec b(1-1/\beta,\beta)=0\sigma(\alpha(\beta))
, so
\mathbf{w}^{k}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{j-i+1}\dots\alpha_{j-i+km}
by (
5.1
); and

(c)

\mathbf{v}\prec\alpha_{j-i+km+1}\dots\alpha_{j-i+km+n}
by (i).

For
j\leq i<j+km
, the inequality follows since
\sigma^{i-j}(\mathbf{w}^{k})\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{km-(i-j)}
because
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, and
\mathbf{v}\prec\alpha_{km-(i-j)+1}\dots\alpha_{km-(i-j)+n}
by (i). Finally, for
i\geq j+km
,
\sigma^{i}({\mathbf{c}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
follows immediately from (ii).
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\beta
and
\hat{\beta}
be bases such that
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
.
Let
\mathbf{z}
and
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
be sequences in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathbf{z}=U_{0}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
. Then

\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\quad\forall\,n\geq 0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\hat{\beta})\quad\forall\,k\geq 0.

Proof.

Suppose first
\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\hat{\beta})
for all
k\geq 0
. Write
\mathbf{z}=z_{1}z_{2}\dots
. Clearly it is enough to show that
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
whenever
z_{n+1}=1
. This implies
n\geq 1
, because
\mathbf{z}=U_{0}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
begins with
0
. Since furthermore
\mathbf{z}
does not contain the word
11
, we have
\sigma^{n-1}(\mathbf{z})=U_{0}(\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}))
for some
k\geq 0
. Since
U_{0}
is increasing, this implies

0\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})=\sigma^{n-1}(\mathbf{z})=U_{0}(\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}))\preccurlyeq U_{0}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))=0\alpha(\beta),

and hence,
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
.

Suppose next that
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
for all
n\geq 0
. For given
k\geq 0
, we can find
n\geq 0
such that
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})=U_{0}(\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}))
and moreover,
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})
begins with
0
. So

U_{0}(\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}))=\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})=0\sigma^{n+1}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq 0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\alpha(\hat{\beta})),

and as a result,
\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\hat{\beta})
.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\beta
and
\hat{\beta}
be bases such that
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{1}(0\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
.
Let
\mathbf{z}
and
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
be sequences in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathbf{z}=U_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
. Then

\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\quad\forall\,n\geq 0\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\hat{\beta})\quad\forall\,k\geq 1.

Proof.

Suppose first that
\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\hat{\beta})
for all
k\geq 1
. Write
\mathbf{z}=z_{1}z_{2}\dots
. Since
\mathbf{y}\preccurlyeq 1\mathbf{y}
for any sequence
\mathbf{c}
, it suffices to show
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
whenever
z_{n}=0
. In that case, there is a
k\geq 0
such that
0\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})=U_{1}(\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}))
. This implies that
\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
begins with
0
, so
\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})=0\sigma^{k+1}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
. Since
U_{1}
is increasing, it follows that
0\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})=U_{1}(0\sigma^{k+1}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}))\preccurlyeq U_{1}(0\alpha(\hat{\beta}))=0\alpha(\beta)
, and hence,
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
.

Conversely, suppose
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
for all
n\geq 0
. Write
\hat{\mathbf{z}}=\hat{z}_{1}\hat{z}_{2}\dots
. Let
k\geq 1
be given. We may assume as above that
\hat{z}_{k}=0
.
Then we can find
n\geq 0
such that
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})=U_{1}(\sigma^{k-1}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}))
, and in particular,
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})
begins with
0
. Thus,

U_{1}(0\sigma^{k}(\mathbf{z}))=U_{1}(\sigma^{k-1}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}))=\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})=0\sigma^{n+1}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq 0\alpha(\beta)=U_{1}(0\alpha(\hat{\beta})),

and since
U_{1}
is strictly increasing, it follows that
\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\hat{\beta})
.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

(a) Both Lemmas
5.5
and
5.6
continue to hold if we replace the inequalities with strict inequalities, because the maps
U_{0}
and
U_{1}
are strictly increasing.

(b) In Lemma
5.6
we had to exclude the value
k=0
. For example, let
\alpha(\hat{\beta})
begin with
10
, so
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{1}(0\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
begins with
01101
and consists of blocks
01
and
011
. Let
\hat{\mathbf{z}}=110^{\infty}
, so
\mathbf{z}=U_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})=11(01)^{\infty}
. Then
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
for all
n\geq 0
, yet
\hat{\mathbf{z}}\succ\alpha(\hat{\beta})
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

By Lemma
5.4
it is enough verify Property TL for each
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}
with
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec 0\sigma(\alpha(\beta))
. We use induction on the length of the generating word
\mathbf{w}
of
[t_{L},t_{R}]
.

The shortest Lyndon word that can generate a
\beta
-Lyndon interval is
\mathbf{w}=01
. Since
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, we have

\alpha(\beta)\succ\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{w})^{\infty}=\alpha(\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{w}})
.
Furthermore,
\mathbf{w}\in F^{*}
, and
\beta\in E_{L}
. So by (
2.3
) it follows that

\alpha(\beta)\succ\alpha(\beta_{r}^{\mathbf{w}})=\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{w})^{+}\mathbf{w}^{\infty}=11(01)^{\infty}.

In particular,
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
11
. By Lemma
2.7
,
\alpha(\beta)
does not end in
(01)^{\infty}
, so there is an
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\alpha(\beta)
does not contain the word
(01)^{M}
. Assume
\mathbf{z}
begins with
0
, and set
\mathbf{v}:=(01)^{M}
. Since
\alpha(\beta)
is balanced by Lemma
2.5
, and
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
11
, it does not contain the word
00
. Conditions (i) and (ii) of Definition
5.3
are clearly satisfied. Hence
\mathbf{w}
satisfies Property TL.

Now let
\mathbf{w}
be a Lyndon word of length
\geq 3
. By Lemma
2.8
, there is a base
\hat{\beta}\in E_{L}
such that

0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
or
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{1}(0\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
.

Case (A)
. Assume first that

(5.2)

0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\alpha(\hat{\beta})).

Then
\alpha(\beta)
does not contain the word 11, so
\mathbf{w}
does not contain this word either, since
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}(\beta)
. Thus, since
|\mathbf{w}|\geq 3
and
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon,
\mathbf{w}
must begin with
00
, which implies
\mathbf{w}=U_{0}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})
for some word
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
beginning with
0
and ending with
1
. Since
U_{0}
is strictly increasing,
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
is again Lyndon. Lemma
5.5
(with strict, instead of weak inequalities) implies that
\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\hat{\beta})
for all
k\geq 0
. Thus,
\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}=b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta})
for some
\hat{t}_{R}\in(0,1)
, and

(5.3)

U_{0}(b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta}))=U_{0}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty})=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}=b(t_{R},\beta).

Furthermore, the assumption
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec 0\sigma(\alpha(\beta))
implies
\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\prec 0\sigma(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
, because (
5.2
) implies
\alpha(\beta)=1U_{0}\big(\sigma(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))\big)
, and so

(5.4)

0\sigma(\alpha(\beta))=0U_{0}\big(\sigma(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))\big)=U_{0}\big(0\sigma(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))\big).

Hence,
\hat{t}_{R}<1-1/\hat{\beta}
.

Now let
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
be given, and assume
\mathbf{z}
begins with
0
. Observe that

(5.5)

b(t_{R},\beta)\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\quad\forall n\geq 0.

Since
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
10
,
\mathbf{z}
cannot contain the word
11
, and so
\mathbf{z}=U_{0}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
for some sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
. By (
5.3
), (
5.5
) and Lemma
5.5
it follows that

b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta})=\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\hat{\beta})\quad\textrm{for all }k\geq 0.

As a result,
\hat{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})
with
\hat{t}_{R}<1-1/\hat{\beta}
.

Since
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
is shorter than
\mathbf{w}
, the induction hypothesis now implies that
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
has property TL for
\hat{\beta}
, so with
\alpha(\hat{\beta})=:\hat{\alpha}_{1}\hat{\alpha}_{2}\dots
, there is a word
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
such that

(i)

\hat{\mathbf{v}}\prec\hat{\alpha}_{j+1}\dots\hat{\alpha}_{j+|\hat{\mathbf{v}}|}
for all
j\geq 0
; and

(ii)

\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{i}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}\hat{\mathbf{z}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\hat{\beta})
for all
i\geq 0
.

Set
\mathbf{v}:=U_{0}(\hat{\mathbf{v}})
. Then
\mathbf{v}
satisfies condition (i) of Definition
5.3
because
U_{0}
is strictly increasing. Furthermore, the lower bound
\sigma^{i}(\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z})\succcurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
is satisfied because
U_{0}
is increasing and
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon. Finally, the upper bound
\sigma^{i}(\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
follows from (ii) above and Lemma
5.5
. Hence,
\mathbf{w}
has Property TL for
\beta
.

Case (B).
Assume next that

(5.6)

0\alpha(\beta)=U_{1}(0\alpha(\hat{\beta})).

Then
\alpha(\beta)\neq(10)^{\infty}
, for otherwise
\alpha(\hat{\beta})
would equal
0^{\infty}
, which isn’t possible. So
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
11
, and by Lemma
2.7
,
\alpha(\beta)
does not end in
(01)^{\infty}
. Hence there is a positive integer
M
such that
\alpha(\beta)
does not contain the word
(01)^{M}
. Furthermore,
\alpha(\beta)
does not contain the word
00
.

If
\mathbf{w}
begins with
00
, take
\mathbf{v}=(01)^{M}
; then it is easy to see that
\mathbf{v}
satisfies the conditions of Definition
5.3
. Hence,
\mathbf{w}
has Property TL for
\beta
.

Assume therefore, for the remainder of the proof, that
\mathbf{w}
begins with
01
. Then
\mathbf{w}
does not contain the word
00
because
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon, and hence
\mathbf{w}=U_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})
for some word
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
. As in Case (A), we can deduce from Lemma
5.6
(with strict inequality) that
\hat{\mathbf{w}}\in L^{*}(\hat{\beta})
, so
\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}=b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta})
for some
\hat{t}_{R}\in(0,1)
. Furthermore, the assumption (
5.6
)
implies that
U_{1}\big(0\sigma(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))\big)=0\sigma(\alpha(\beta))
, because
0\sigma(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
is simply
0\alpha(\hat{\beta})
without the first ‘1’, and
U_{1}(1)=1
. Therefore, since
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec 0\sigma(\alpha(\beta))
, we also have
\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\prec 0\sigma(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
, and hence
\hat{t}_{R}<1-1/\hat{\beta}
.

Now let
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
be given, and assume that
\mathbf{z}
begins with
0
. Since
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\succcurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
for all
n\geq 0
and
\mathbf{w}
begins with
01
,
\mathbf{z}
does not contain the word
00
. Hence,
\mathbf{z}=U_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
for some sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
which again begins with
0
. As in Case (A), we can deduce from Lemma
5.6
that

b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta})=\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{k}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\hat{\beta})\quad\textrm{for all }k\geq 0,

where for
k=0
the last inequality holds since
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
begins with
0
.
Hence,
\hat{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})
with
\hat{t}_{R}<1-1/\hat{\beta}
.
The induction hypothesis now comes in to complete the proof in the same way as in Case (A), using Lemma
5.6
instead of Lemma
5.5
.
∎

5.1.
Density of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals

In this subsection we show that the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,1-1/\beta]
for
\beta\in\overline{E}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\beta\in\overline{E}
, and let
\mathbf{w}
be a Lyndon word such that

(i)

\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}
is a greedy
\beta
-expansion; and

(ii)

\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}\prec 0\sigma(\alpha(\beta))
.

Then
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}(\beta)
.

Proof.

We use induction on the length of
\mathbf{w}
. Precisely, we prove: For any Lyndon word
\mathbf{w}
, if
\beta\in\overline{E}
is such that (i) and (ii) are satisfied, then
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}(\beta)
.

If
\mathbf{w}=01
, then (ii) implies
\alpha(\beta)\succcurlyeq 110^{\infty}
, so
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\preccurlyeq(10)^{\infty}\prec\alpha(\beta)
for each
n\geq 0
. Hence
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}(\beta)
.

Now let
k\geq 3
and assume the statement is true for all Lyndon words of length
<k
. Let
\mathbf{w}
be a Lyndon word of length
k
, and let
\beta\in\overline{E}
be such that (i) and (ii) are satisfied. By Lemma
2.8

(in which we can clearly replace
E_{L}
with
\overline{E}
) there is a base
\hat{\beta}\in\overline{E}
such that
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
or
\alpha(\beta)=1U_{1}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
.

First assume the former, i.e.
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
. Then
\alpha(\beta)
does not contain the word
11
, so
\mathbf{w}
does not contain this word either by assumption (i). Since
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}
, this implies
\mathbf{w}=U_{0}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})
for some
\hat{\mathbf{w}}\in L^{*}
. Furthermore,
U_{0}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}0^{\infty})=\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}
. Thus, by Lemma
5.5
,
\hat{\mathbf{w}}0^{\infty}
is a greedy
\hat{\beta}
-expansion. It also follows from (ii) and (
5.4
) that
\hat{\mathbf{w}}0^{\infty}\prec 0\sigma(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
. Hence, by the induction hypothesis,
\hat{\mathbf{w}}\in L^{*}(\hat{\beta})
. But then, again by Lemma
5.5
,
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}(\beta)
.

Next, suppose
\alpha(\beta)=1U_{1}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
. Then
\alpha(\beta)
does not contain the word
00
, so if
\mathbf{w}
begins with
00
, then
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta)
for all
n\geq 0
: setting
m:=n\!\!\mod|\mathbf{w}|
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})

\displaystyle=w_{m+1}\dots w_{|\mathbf{w}|}\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{|\mathbf{w}|-m}\mathbf{w}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\prec\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{|\mathbf{w}|-m}\sigma^{|\mathbf{w}|-m}(\alpha(\beta))=\alpha(\beta).

So assume
\mathbf{w}
begins with
01
. Then
\mathbf{w}=U_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})
for some
\hat{\mathbf{w}}\in L^{*}
because
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}
. Now observe that
\alpha(\beta)=1U_{1}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
implies
\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\succ(01)^{\infty}
for all
n\geq 0
, so
\mathbf{w}(01)^{\infty}=U_{1}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}0^{\infty})
is also a greedy
\beta
-expansion. Thus, by Lemma
5.6
,
\hat{\mathbf{w}}0^{\infty}
is a greedy
\hat{\beta}
-expansion.
The rest of the proof goes as in the first case above, using Lemma
5.6
instead of Lemma
5.5
.
∎

Proposition 5.9
.

For each
\beta\in\overline{E}\cap(1,2]
, the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
\big(0,1-\frac{1}{\beta}\big)
.

Proof.

Fix
t\in\big(0,1-\frac{1}{\beta}\big)
and write
b(t,\beta)=t_{1}t_{2}\dots
. We will show that
t
either lies in a
\beta
-Lyndon interval or else
t\in\mathscr{E}_{\beta}^{+}
, in which case
t
can be approximated arbitrarily closely by endpoints of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals by Lemma
4.5
.

Note that
t_{1}=0
since
t<1-\frac{1}{\beta}\leq\frac{1}{\beta}
. Suppose
t\not\in\mathscr{E}_{\beta}^{+}
. Then
\sigma^{k}((t_{i}))\prec(t_{i})
for some
k
; choose
k
as small as possible with this property. Then
\mathbf{w}:=t_{1}\dots t_{k}
is either Lyndon or a power of a Lyndon word. Assume the former; in the latter case we replace
k
with
k^{\prime}<k
so that
t_{1}\dots t_{k^{\prime}}
is Lyndon and
k^{\prime}\mid k
. By Lemma
5.8
,
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}(\beta)
. Since furthermore,
\sigma^{k}((t_{i}))\prec(t_{1}\dots t_{k})^{\infty}
, it follows that
t
lies in the
\beta
-Lyndon interval generated by
\mathbf{w}
.
∎

The conclusion of Proposition
5.9
may fail if
\beta\not\in\overline{E}
; in fact the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals may not even be dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
, as the following example shows.

Example 5.10
.

Let
\beta
be any base such that
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
111\,01\,011\,001
. Set
{\mathbf{s}}=011
. Then
\beta
lies in the basic interval
[\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{s}}]
, where
\alpha(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{s}}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}=(110)^{\infty}
and
\alpha(\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{s}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}=111\,010\,(110)^{\infty}
. Hence, by
[
4
, Theorem 2]
,

\tau(\beta)=\pi_{\beta}\big({\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}\big)=\pi_{\beta}\big(010(110)^{\infty}\big).

Let
\mathbf{w}=01010111
and
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}=01011
. Note that
\mathbf{w}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}
are both
\beta
-Lyndon words, and
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec\tilde{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\prec 010(110)^{\infty}
. So the (symbolic)
\beta
-Lyndon intervals
[\mathbf{w}0^{\infty},\mathbf{w}^{\infty}]
and
[\tilde{\mathbf{w}}0^{\infty},\tilde{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}]
are disjoint and their projections under
\pi_{\beta}
both lie inside
[0,\tau(\beta)]
. But it is not difficult to see that there is no other
\beta
-Lyndon interval in between these intervals, because there is no
\beta
-Lyndon word that extends
\mathbf{w}
. Hence, the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are not dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
. We will have more to say about this example in Example
10.1
.

Corollary 5.11
.

If
\beta=\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{s}}
is the left endpoint of a Farey interval
[\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{s}},\beta_{r}^{\mathbf{s}}]
, then
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}=\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
.

Proof.

Note that
\beta=\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{s}}\in E_{L}
, so
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive for every
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]
by Theorem
5.1
. Furthermore, the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,1-1/\beta]
by Proposition
5.9
. Since
\alpha(\beta)=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}
, we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})=\left\{\mathbf{z}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}~\forall n\geq 0\right\},

so
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is a SFT, hence sofic. Proposition
4.6
now implies that there are no points of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
in
[0,1-1/\beta)
. On the other hand, as shown in the proof of
[
22
, Proposition 5.2]
,
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[1-1/\beta,1]\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(1-1/\beta)=\emptyset
. Hence,
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}=\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
.
∎

6.
Right endpoints of first-order basic intervals

Our main result in this section is the following.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\beta_{*}=\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{s}}
be the right endpoint of a basic interval generated by
{\mathbf{s}}\in F^{*}
. Then for any
\beta_{*}
-Lyndon interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]
in
[0,\tau(\beta_{*})]
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta_{*}}(t_{R})
is a transitive sofic subshift.
In particular, the conclusion of Theorem
4.4
holds for
\beta=\beta_{*}
.

We prove the theorem after establishing a useful lemma, which uses the following fact from
[
22
, Proposition 4.4]
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For
{\mathbf{s}}\in F^{*}
, define the set

\mathcal{X}({\mathbf{s}}):=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:{\mathbf{s}}0^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}\ \forall n\geq 0\}.

Then
\mathcal{X}({\mathbf{s}})=\{\sigma^{j}({\mathbf{s}}^{\infty}):0\leq j<|{\mathbf{s}}|\}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell},\beta_{*}]
, where
[\beta_{\ell},\beta_{*}]
is a basic interval generated by a Farey word
{\mathbf{s}}=s_{1}\dots s_{m}
, and let
[t_{L},t_{R}]
be a
\beta
-Lyndon interval in
(0,\tau(\beta))
. Let
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
.
Then
\mathbf{z}
can be extended to the left to a sequence
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
beginning with
{\mathbf{s}}
, and also to a sequence
\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
beginning with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
.

Proof.

Set
\mathbf{z}^{(0)}:=\mathbf{z}
, and, for
k=0,1,2,\dots
, proceed inductively as follows. If
\mathbf{z}^{(k)}
has been constructed and does not begin with the prefix
{\mathbf{s}}
, set
\mathbf{z}^{(k+1)}=1\mathbf{z}^{(k)}
if
1\mathbf{z}^{(k)}
is legal in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
, or
\mathbf{z}^{(k+1)}=0\mathbf{z}^{(k)}
otherwise. We end the procedure if we reach a sequence
\mathbf{z}^{(k)}
beginning with
{\mathbf{s}}
.

Suppose the procedure never ends. Let
k\geq 1
, and write
\mathbf{z}^{(k)}=z_{1}^{(k)}z_{2}^{(k)}\dots
. Since
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell},\beta_{*}]
,
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}
. So, if
z_{1}^{(k)}=0
, this means
1\sigma(\mathbf{z}^{(k)})\succ\alpha(\beta)
and hence

1z_{2}^{(k)}\dots z_{m}^{(k)}\succcurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+},

which implies

z_{1}^{(k)}z_{2}^{(k)}\dots z_{m}^{(k)}=0\sigma(1z_{2}^{(k)}\dots z_{m}^{(k)})\succcurlyeq 0\sigma(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+})={\mathbf{s}},

where the last equality follows from Lemma
2.4
(i). Since
\mathbf{z}^{(k)}
does not begin with
{\mathbf{s}}
, this means that
z_{1}^{(k)}z_{2}^{(k)}\dots z_{m}^{(k)}\succ{\mathbf{s}}
. Clearly, this inequality holds also when
z_{1}^{(k)}=1
.

On the other hand, for
k>m
we claim that
\mathbf{z}^{(k)}
cannot begin with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}
. For, suppose it did. Then, since
\alpha(\beta)\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta_{*})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}
and
\mathbf{z}^{(k)}
is legal in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
, we would have

z_{1}^{(k-m)}\dots z_{m}^{(k-m)}=z_{m+1}^{(k)}\dots z_{2m}^{(k)}\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\prec{\mathbf{s}},

contradicting what we just showed. Hence, we also have
z_{1}^{(k)}\dots z_{m}^{(k)}\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
.

We have now constructed a sequence, extending infinitely to the left, all of whose subwords of length
m
are strictly greater than
{\mathbf{s}}
but smaller than or equal to
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
. But such a sequence cannot exist, in view of Lemma
6.2
. This contradiction shows that we eventually end up with an extension
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{z}
beginning with
{\mathbf{s}}
. But then
{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
as well, and since
{\mathbf{s}}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
begins with
{\mathbf{s}}^{2}
and
{\mathbf{s}}^{2}
contains the word
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
, we can extend
\mathbf{z}
to a sequence
\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
beginning with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{s}}={\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}{\mathbf{s}}^{(2)}
be the
standard factorization
of
{\mathbf{s}}
into Farey words
{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}
and
{\mathbf{s}}^{(2)}
. It is well known that such a factorization exists and is unique (cf.
[
9
]
). We can write

\alpha(\beta_{*})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty},

where the second equality can be deduced from
[
9
, Lemma 2.6]
and the palindrome property of Farey words (see Lemma
2.4
(ii)).

Recall from
[
4
, Theorem 2]
that

\tau(\beta_{*})=\pi_{\beta}\big(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}\big).

Let
[t_{L},t_{R}]
be a
\beta_{*}
-Lyndon interval and assume
t_{R}<\tau(\beta_{*})
; then
b(t_{R},\beta_{*})\prec{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}={\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty}
. So, letting
\mathbf{w}
be the word such that
b(t_{R},\beta_{*})=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
, we can choose an integer
N
so large that

(6.1)

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}{\mathbf{s}}^{N}0^{\infty},

and

(6.2)

\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}{\mathbf{s}}^{N}0^{\infty}\quad\forall n\geq 0.

(The latter condition holds for large enough
N
since
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}(\beta_{*})
and
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
is periodic.)

Now let
\mathbf{u}=u_{1}\dots u_{r}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta_{*}}(t_{R}))
and
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta_{*}}(t_{R})
. By Lemma
6.3
we may assume that
\mathbf{z}
begins with the word
{\mathbf{s}}
. We also extend
\mathbf{u}
to the right to a word
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
as follows: Let
k
be the largest integer such that
u_{r-k+1}\dots u_{r}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k}
, where
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\dots:=\alpha(\beta_{*})
. If no such
k
exists, set
k=0
. Then set

\mathbf{u}^{\prime}:=u_{1}\dots u_{r-k}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}{\mathbf{s}}^{N_{1}},

where
N_{1}\geq N
is chosen large enough so that
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
actually extends
\mathbf{u}
. It is not difficult to see using (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) that
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta_{*}}(t_{R}))
.

Now if in fact
\mathbf{z}\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty}
, we have immediately that
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta_{*}}(t_{R})
, in view of the inequalities

s_{i+1}\ldots s_{m}s_{1}\ldots s_{i}\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}\quad\textrm{and}\quad s_{i+1}\ldots s_{m}\succ s_{1}\ldots s_{m-i}\succcurlyeq w_{1}\ldots w_{m-i}\quad\forall\,0\leq i<m.

Otherwise, there is an integer
l\geq 1
and a word
\mathbf{v}\succ{\mathbf{s}}
such that
\mathbf{z}
begins with
{\mathbf{s}}^{l}\mathbf{v}
. Now observe that
{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}\mathbf{z}\succ{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty}\succ\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
, and also
{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}\mathbf{z}\prec{\mathbf{s}}^{\infty}
, since
{\mathbf{s}}
being Lyndon implies
{\mathbf{s}}={\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}{\mathbf{s}}^{(2)}\prec{\mathbf{s}}^{(2)}{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}
and hence
{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}{\mathbf{s}}\prec{\mathbf{s}}{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}
. Using the definition of
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
and the assumptions (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) it now follows readily that

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\qquad\forall n\geq 0,

and therefore,
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}{\mathbf{s}}^{(1)}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta_{*}}(t_{R})
. As a result,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta_{*}}(t_{R})
is transitive.
∎

7.
The case of finitely renormalizable
\beta

In this section we consider value of
\beta
in the set
\overline{E^{\mathbf{S}}}
for
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
. Recall
E^{\mathbf{S}}
is defined in (
2.10
).
Recall further that
\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)
is the set of all right endpoints of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\beta\in\overline{E^{\mathbf{S}}}
for some
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
. Then:

(i)

For every
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
has a transitive subshift
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
of full entropy and full Hausdorff dimension that contains the sequence
b(t_{R},\beta)
. Moreover, these transitive subshifts can be chosen so that
\{\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}):t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)\}
is a strictly descending collection of subshifts, and if
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic, then
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
is sofic for each
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)
.

(ii)

The
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
.

Note that (i) implies Theorem
4.4
for
\beta\in\overline{E^{\mathbf{S}}}
.
We first establish a series of lemmas.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{r}}\in\Lambda
, and let
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
be sequences in
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
beginning with
0
and
1
respectively. Suppose a sequence
\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
begins with
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
or
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}
and satisfies

(7.1)

\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{x})\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{y})\qquad\forall\,n\geq 0.

Then
\mathbf{z}=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
for some sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
.

Proof.

From Definition
2.10
, we see that
\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{x})
begins with
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{j}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}
for some
j\geq 0
, and
\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{y})
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}{\mathbf{r}}^{k}{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
for some
k\geq 0
, where
j
and
k
may be infinite. So by (
7.1
),

{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{j}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}0^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}{\mathbf{r}}^{k}{\mathbf{r}}^{-}1^{\infty}\qquad\forall\,n\geq 0,

and any block
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{l}
(with
0\leq l\leq j
) in
\mathbf{z}
must be followed by
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})
or
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}
, whereas any block
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}{\mathbf{r}}^{m}
(with
0\leq m\leq k
) in
\mathbf{z}
must be followed by
{\mathbf{r}}
or
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
. Hence
\mathbf{z}\in X({\mathbf{r}})
, and so
\mathbf{z}=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
for some sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
.
∎

For any
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, we define the set

(7.2)

\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}):=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\ \ \forall n\geq 0\}.

The next result, which extends Lemma
6.2
to arbitrary words in
\Lambda
, was proved in
[
4
, Proposition 4.1]
. It also follows from
[
19
, Lemma 2.12]
via the relationship (
1.8
), or can alternatively be deduced, with some effort, from
[
24
, Theorem 2.5]
.
We include a short proof here for the reader’s convenience.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
. Then the set
\Gamma({\mathbf{S}})
is countable.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the degree
k
of
{\mathbf{S}}
.
The case
k=1
follows from Lemma
6.2
. Let
k\geq 2
, and suppose the statement holds for all
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda_{k-1}
. Take
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda_{k}
, and write
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}}
where
{\mathbf{r}}\in F^{*}
and
{\mathbf{s}}\in\Lambda_{k-1}
. Let
\mathbf{z}\in\Gamma({\mathbf{S}})
. Since
{\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}}
begins with
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
and
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}
, it follows that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{r}}^{-}0^{\infty}

\displaystyle\prec\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}({\mathbf{s}}^{\infty})=({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty})\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}1^{\infty}\qquad\forall n\geq 0,

so either
\mathbf{z}\in\Gamma({\mathbf{r}})
, or else
\mathbf{z}
eventually contains
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
or
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}
, which by Lemma
7.2
implies
\sigma^{k}(\mathbf{z})=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
for some
k\geq 0
and sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
. But
\mathbf{z}\in\Gamma({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})
implies that
\hat{\mathbf{z}}\in\Gamma({\mathbf{s}})
by Lemma
2.12
(i). Therefore,

\Gamma({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})\subseteq\Gamma({\mathbf{r}})\cup\bigcup_{{\mathbf{d}}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{*}}{\mathbf{d}}\;\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\Gamma({\mathbf{s}})).

Since both
\Gamma({\mathbf{r}})
and
\Gamma({\mathbf{s}})
are countable by the induction hypothesis, we conclude that
\Gamma({\mathbf{S}})=\Gamma({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})
is countable as well.
∎

The next lemma describes the relationship between the substitution map
\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}
and the maps
U_{0}
and
U_{1}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
{\mathbf{r}}\in F^{*}
. Suppose
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{y})
for some sequence
\mathbf{y}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and either

(i)

{\mathbf{r}}=U_{0}(\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}})
for some
\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}\in F^{*}
, and
\tilde{\beta}
is the base such that
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\alpha(\tilde{\beta}))
; or

(ii)

{\mathbf{r}}=U_{1}(\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}})
for some
\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}\in F^{*}
, and
\tilde{\beta}
is the base such that
\alpha(\beta)=1U_{1}(0\alpha(\tilde{\beta}))
.

Then
\alpha(\tilde{\beta})=\Phi_{\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}}(\mathbf{y})
.

Proof.

Suppose
{\mathbf{r}}=U_{0}(\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}})
and
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\alpha(\tilde{\beta}))
. Since
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{y})
begins with 1 and
\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(0)={\mathbf{r}}^{-}
begins with 0, it follows that
\mathbf{y}
begins with 1. Thus, according to the definition (
2.7
) we can write

\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{y})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}{\mathbf{r}}^{k_{1}}{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{l_{1}}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}{\mathbf{r}}^{k_{2}}{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{l_{2}}\dots,

where
k_{i},l_{i}
are nonnegative integers, possibly one of them taking the value
+\infty
. By repeatedly applying the identities of Lemma
2.6
(i), it follows that

U_{0}(\alpha(\tilde{\beta}))=0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\Phi_{\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}}(\mathbf{y})).

Thus,
\alpha(\tilde{\beta})=\Phi_{\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}}(\mathbf{y})
.
The proof in case (ii) is similar, using Lemma
2.6
(ii).
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
{\mathbf{r}}\in F^{*}
and let
\beta\in(1,2)
such that
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{y})
for some sequence
\mathbf{y}\succ 10^{\infty}
.
Let
\mathbf{w}
be a
\beta
-Lyndon word such that
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
. Then for any sequence
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})
there is a word
\mathbf{v}
such that
\mathbf{w}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})
.

Proof.

We use induction on the length of the Farey word
{\mathbf{r}}
. For the purpose of this proof, we will consider
\mathbf{w}=0
a
\beta
-Lyndon word, and the degenerate interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]=\{0\}
a
\beta
-Lyndon interval. Note that for this case, the statement of the lemma is trivial.

Step 1.
First we take
{\mathbf{r}}=01
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
\mathbf{z}
begins with
0
. Since
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}=00(10)^{\infty}
, there is an integer
M
such that

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec 00(10)^{M}0^{\infty}.

Likewise, since
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbf{y})
,
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}
. Furthermore,
1^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\mathbf{y}\succ 10^{\infty}
implies
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}{\mathbf{r}}^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\succ\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}=1100(10)^{\infty}
, so there is a further integer
N\geq M
such that

11(01)^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\succ 1100(10)^{N}1^{\infty}.

Now set

\mathbf{v}:=00(10)^{N}1.

Then it is not difficult to see that
\mathbf{w}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})
.
This completes the basis for the induction.

Step 2.
Now let
k\geq 3
and assume the statement of the lemma holds for all Farey words of length
<k
. Let
{\mathbf{r}}
be a Farey word of length
k
. So either
{\mathbf{r}}=U_{0}(\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}})
or
{\mathbf{r}}=U_{1}(\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}})
for some Farey word
\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}
of length
<k
.

Case (A):
Suppose
{\mathbf{r}}=U_{0}(\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}})
. Let
\tilde{\beta}
be the base such that
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{0}(\alpha(\tilde{\beta}))
. Then
\alpha(\tilde{\beta})=\Phi_{\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}}(\mathbf{y})
by Lemma
7.4
, and
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
10
. Thus,
\mathbf{w}
cannot contain the word
11
, and so
\mathbf{w}=U_{0}(\tilde{\mathbf{w}})
for some
\tilde{\beta}
-Lyndon word
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}
. Furthermore, by similar reasoning as in the proof of Lemma
7.4
, we obtain

U_{0}(\tilde{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty})=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}=U_{0}\big(\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}^{-}\mathbb{L}(\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}})^{\infty}\big).

Thus,
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\prec\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}^{-}\mathbb{L}(\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}})^{\infty}
.

Assume without loss of generality that
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})
begins with
0
. It then follows in the same way as before that
\mathbf{z}=U_{0}(\tilde{\mathbf{z}})
for some sequence
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}
, and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\tilde{\beta}}(\tilde{\mathbf{w}})
. Observe that
\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}
,
\tilde{\beta}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}
and
\tilde{t}_{R}
satisfy the hypotheses of the lemma. Therefore, by the induction hypothesis, there is a word
\tilde{\mathbf{v}}
such that
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\tilde{\beta}}(\tilde{\mathbf{w}})
. Set
\mathbf{v}:=U_{0}(\tilde{\mathbf{v}})
; then
\mathbf{w}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}=U_{0}(\tilde{\mathbf{w}}\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\tilde{\mathbf{z}})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})
,
as desired.

Case (B):
Suppose
{\mathbf{r}}=U_{1}(\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}})
. Let
\tilde{\beta}
be the base such that
0\alpha(\beta)=U_{1}(0\alpha(\tilde{\beta}))
. Then by Lemma
7.4
we have
\alpha(\tilde{\beta})=\Phi_{\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}}(\mathbf{y})
.

Assume without loss of generality that
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})
begins with
0
. Note that
\alpha(\beta)
does not contain the word
00
, and
\alpha(\beta)
does not end in
(01)^{\infty}
, because if it did, then
\alpha(\tilde{\beta})
would end in
0^{\infty}
, which is impossible. If
\mathbf{w}
begins with
00
, then we can find a positive integer
M
such that
(01)^{M}
does not occur in
\alpha(\beta)
, and then
\mathbf{w}(01)^{M}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})
.

So assume
\mathbf{w}
begins with
01
. Since
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon, we can then write
\mathbf{w}=U_{1}(\tilde{\mathbf{w}})
, where
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}
is again Lyndon. (Possibly,
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}=0
.) As before, we find that
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}
is in fact
\tilde{\beta}
-Lyndon.
Since
\mathbf{w}\prec{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
, we can deduce in a similar manner as in Case (A) that
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}\prec\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}}^{-}\mathbb{L}(\tilde{{\mathbf{r}}})^{\infty}
.

Since
\mathbf{z}
begins with
0
, the same reasoning as before yields that
\mathbf{z}=U_{1}(\tilde{\mathbf{z}})
for some sequence
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}
. As before, we have
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\tilde{\beta}}(\tilde{\mathbf{w}})
. By the induction hypothesis, there is a word
\tilde{\mathbf{v}}
such that
\tilde{\mathbf{w}}\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\tilde{\beta}}(\tilde{\mathbf{w}})
. Set
\mathbf{v}:=U_{1}(\tilde{\mathbf{v}})
; then
\mathbf{w}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}=U_{1}(\tilde{\mathbf{w}}\tilde{\mathbf{v}}\tilde{\mathbf{z}})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(\mathbf{w})
.
∎

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
\beta
be renormalizable, so
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
for some Farey word
{\mathbf{r}}
and base
\hat{\beta}\in(1,2]
. Let
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)
. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive if and only if
b(t_{R},\beta)\prec{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{w}
be the
\beta
-Lyndon word such that
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
. Set
\hat{\mathbf{y}}:=\alpha(\hat{\beta})
, so
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})
and
\hat{\mathbf{y}}\succ 10^{\infty}
.

Assume first that
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
. Then we can find positive integers
N
and
k
such that

(7.3)

\mathbf{w}^{k}1^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{N}0^{\infty}.

Let
\mathbf{u}=u_{1}\dots u_{l}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))
and
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. By Lemma
7.5
there is a word
\mathbf{v}
such that
\mathbf{w}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. It is easy to see that then
\mathbf{w}^{k}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
also. Now let
j
be the smallest positive integer such that
u_{j+1}\dots u_{l}
is a prefix of
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
; or set
j=l
if no such integer exists.
By choosing
k
even larger if necessary, we may assume
k|\mathbf{w}|>l-j
. Consider the sequence

{\mathbf{c}}:=u_{1}\dots u_{j}\mathbf{w}^{k}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}.

Then
{\mathbf{c}}
begins with
\mathbf{u}
and ends in
\mathbf{z}
, and
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{z}
are separated at least by the word
\mathbf{v}
. We now check that
{\mathbf{c}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
.

Since
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})
, by Lemma
2.12
(iii) it follows that
\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\succcurlyeq\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(0^{\infty})={\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
for all
n\geq 0
. Hence, for
n<j
, (
7.3
) gives

\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{c}})=u_{n+1}\dots u_{j}\mathbf{w}^{k}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\prec u_{n+1}\dots u_{j}{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{N}0^{\infty}\prec\alpha(\beta),

since
u_{n+1}\dots u_{j}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j-n}
, and
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{N}0^{\infty}\prec\alpha_{j-n+1}\alpha_{j-n+2}\dots
.
For
n\geq j
,
\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{c}})\prec\alpha(\beta)
follows since
\mathbf{w}^{k}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. The other requisite inequality,
\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{c}})\succcurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
, follows from the choice of
j
. Hence,
{\mathbf{c}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. This proves that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive.

Next, assume
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succcurlyeq{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
. Note that this actually means
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succ{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
since
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
is periodic. Write
\alpha(\hat{\beta})=\hat{\alpha}_{1}\hat{\alpha}_{2}\dots
. Since
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec b(\tau(\beta),\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(0\hat{\alpha}_{2}\hat{\alpha}_{2}\dots)
, and the latter expression begins with
{\mathbf{S}}^{-}
which in turn begins with
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
,
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
must begin with
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
. Since
\mathbf{w}
is also
\beta
-Lyndon and
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})
, by Lemma
7.2
it follows that
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\hat{\mathbf{x}})
for some sequence
\hat{\mathbf{x}}
.
Again by Lemma
7.2
, any sequence
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
that begins with
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
must be of the form
\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
for some sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
. Note that
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\in\mathcal{L}\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})\big)
and
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. But, since each block
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})
can only be preceded by another block
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})
or by
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
, any sequence of the form
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbf{v}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
would have to end in
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
, which is impossible since
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
. This shows
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is not transitive.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\beta\in(1,2]
be a base such that
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic, and let
[t_{L},t_{R}]
be a
\beta
-Lyndon interval. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is a sofic subshift.

Proof.

It is well known (see
[
8
]
) that the
\beta
-shift
\Sigma_{\beta}
is sofic if and only if
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic. Let
\mathbf{w}
be the
\beta
-Lyndon word such that
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
, and define the set

\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{w}}:=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\succcurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\ \forall n\geq 0\}.

Then
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{w}}
is a SFT and hence sofic. Since the intersection of two sofic shifts is sofic (see
[
27
, Proposition 3.4.10]
), it follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})=\Sigma_{\beta}\cap\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{w}}
is sofic.
∎

Proposition 7.8
.

The statement of Theorem
7.1
(i) holds for all
\beta\in E_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}
, where
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
and

E_{L}^{\mathbf{S}}:=E^{\mathbf{S}}\cup\bigcup_{{\mathbf{r}}\in F^{*}}\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}}
.

Proof.

Write
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k}
, where
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}\in F^{*}
for each
i
. Set
{\mathbf{S}}_{i}:={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,k
, and note that we can write
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{{\mathbf{S}}_{i}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}_{i}))
for certain bases
\hat{\beta}_{1},\dots,\hat{\beta}_{k}
, where in particular
\hat{\beta}_{k}\in E_{L}
. Let
\mathbf{w}
be a
\beta
-Lyndon word and
t_{R}
the number such that
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
.

If
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{r}}_{1}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{1})^{\infty}={\mathbf{S}}_{1}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{1})^{\infty}
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive by Proposition
7.6
. Otherwise, there is a smallest integer
i\geq 1
such that
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succ{\mathbf{S}}_{i}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{i})^{\infty}
. Plainly
i\leq k
. Recall from the proof of Proposition
7.6
that this implies
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}=\Phi_{{\mathbf{S}}_{i}}(\mathbf{x})
for some sequence
\mathbf{x}
. Since
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
is periodic and
\mathbf{w}
begins with 0 and ends with 1, it follows that the sequence
\mathbf{x}
is also periodic, so we can write
\mathbf{x}=\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}
for some word
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
, and it is easy to see that
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
is
\hat{\beta}_{i}
-Lyndon, using that
\Phi_{{\mathbf{S}}_{i}}
is increasing. Let
\hat{t}_{R}
be the point such that
b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta}_{i})=\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}
. Now we consider two cases:

•

If
i<k
, then
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}_{i+1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{i+1})^{\infty}
, and so
\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\prec\Phi_{{\mathbf{S}}_{i}}^{-1}\big({\mathbf{S}}_{i+1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{i+1})^{\infty}\big)={\mathbf{r}}_{i+1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{i+1})^{\infty}
. Observe that
\alpha(\hat{\beta}_{i})=\Phi_{{\mathbf{r}}_{i+1}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}_{i+1}))
. So by Proposition
7.6
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}_{i}}(\hat{t}_{R})
is transitive.

•

If
i=k
, then
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec\tau(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(0\hat{\alpha}_{1}\hat{\alpha}_{2}\dots)
, where
(\hat{\alpha}_{i}):=\alpha(\hat{\beta}_{k})
. This implies
\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\prec 0\hat{\alpha}_{1}\hat{\alpha}_{2}\dots
, in other words,
\hat{t}_{R}<1-(1/\hat{\beta}_{k})
. Observe furthermore that
\hat{\beta}_{k}\in E_{L}
. Hence,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}_{k}}(\hat{t}_{R})
is transitive by Theorem
5.1
.

We conclude that in both cases,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}_{i}}(\hat{t}_{R})
is transitive. From here on we simply write
\hat{\beta}:=\hat{\beta}_{i}
. We also set
\mathbf{R}:={\mathbf{S}}_{i}
.

Now we set

(7.4)

\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}):=\{\sigma^{n}(\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})):\hat{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R}),\ n\geq 0\},

and claim that
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
has the desired properties.

First of all, it is clear that
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
is invariant under
\sigma
, and it is closed because
\mathcal{K}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})
is closed and
\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}
is continuous on the range
X(\mathbf{R})
of
\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}
. So
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
is a subshift of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Next, since each sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})
satisfies

\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{j}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\hat{\beta})\qquad\forall j\geq 0.

Lemma
2.12
(iii) implies that

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{j}(\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}))\preccurlyeq\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))=\alpha(\beta)\qquad\forall j\geq 0.

Hence,
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
is in fact a subshift of
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. Furthermore, (
7.4
) implies that
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
is a factor of
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})
. If
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic, then so is
\alpha(\hat{\beta})=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}(\alpha(\beta))
; hence
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})
is sofic by Lemma
7.7
. But a factor of a sofic subshift is sofic (see
[
27
, Corollary 3.2.2]
), and so
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
is sofic.

Note that
b(t_{R},\beta)\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
because
b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta})\in\mathcal{K}_{\hat{\beta}}^{\prime}(\hat{t}_{R})
and
b(t_{R},\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta}))
.

We next verify that
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
is transitive. Let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}))
and
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
. By the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
we can extend
\mathbf{u}
to the left and right to obtain a word
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}))
containing
\mathbf{u}
that consists of blocks from
\{\mathbf{R}^{-},\mathbf{R},\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R}),\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R})^{+}\}
. Similarly we can extend
\mathbf{z}
to the left to a sequence
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
consisting of such blocks. By putting more blocks in front of
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
if necessary, we may assume that
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
begins with the block
\mathbf{R}^{-}
. Also by adding more blocks to the right of
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
, we can obtain a word
\mathbf{u}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}))
ending in
\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R})^{+}
. Similarly, by putting more blocks in front of
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
we obtain a sequence
\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
beginning with
\mathbf{R}^{-}\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R})^{l}
, where
l
is chosen as large as possible so that
\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime}
is still in
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
. (Since
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succ\mathbf{R}^{-}\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R})^{\infty}
, there is a largest such
l
.)

Now we will connect the word
\mathbf{u}^{\prime\prime}
to the sequence
\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime}
. Observe that
\mathbf{u}^{\prime\prime}=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{u}})
for some word
\hat{\mathbf{u}}
, and
\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime}=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
for some sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
. Since
\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}
is strictly increasing,
\hat{\mathbf{u}}\in\mathcal{L}\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})\big)
and
\hat{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathcal{K}_{\hat{\beta}}^{\prime}(\hat{t}_{R})
. Moreover,
\hat{\mathbf{u}}
begins with
0
and ends in
1
. Similarly,
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
begins with
0^{l+1}
and extending
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
by another 0 would create a sequence that is not in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})
.

Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})
is transitive, there is a (possibly empty) word
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
such that
\hat{\mathbf{u}}\hat{\mathbf{v}}\hat{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})
. By the above remark,
\hat{\mathbf{v}}
must end with
1
. We can write
\hat{\mathbf{v}}=1^{k}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}
, where
k\geq 0
and
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}
is either empty or begins with
0
. Now the sequence
(\hat{\mathbf{u}}1^{k},\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},\hat{\mathbf{z}})
is connectible, and hence (see Lemma
2.13
)

\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}\big((\hat{\mathbf{u}}1^{k})\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\hat{\mathbf{z}}\big)=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{u}}1^{k})\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime})\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}).

Set
\mathbf{v}:=\mathbf{R}^{k}\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime})
. Then
\mathbf{u}^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime}=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{u}}1^{k}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\hat{\mathbf{z}})=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{u}}\hat{\mathbf{v}}\hat{\mathbf{z}})\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
. This shows that
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
is transitive.

Next, we verify that
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
has full entropy and full Hausdorff dimension in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. If
\mathbf{z}=z_{1}z_{2}z_{3}\dots\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
, then

\mathbf{R}^{-}0^{\infty}\prec\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty})=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))\prec\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R})^{+}1^{\infty}\qquad\forall n\geq 0.

So either
\mathbf{z}\in\Gamma(\mathbf{R})
, where
\Gamma(\mathbf{R})
is the set defined in Lemma
7.3
, or else
\mathbf{z}
contains the word
\mathbf{R}^{-}
or
\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R})^{+}
somewhere, in which case there is by Lemma
7.2
an
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
for some sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})
, and the prefix
z_{1}\dots z_{n}
is a word in
\mathcal{L}(\Gamma(\mathbf{R}))
. This implies

h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})\big)=\max\left\{h(\Gamma(\mathbf{R})),h\big(\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})\big)\right\}.

But
\Gamma(\mathbf{R})
is countable by Lemma
7.3
. Hence, we obtain

h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})\big)=h\big(\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})\big).

As a result,
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
has full entropy. By the same reasoning, we also have that

\dim_{H}\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})=\dim_{H}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}).

Finally, it is easy to see from (
7.4
) that
\{\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}):t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)\}
is a strictly descending collection of subshifts because
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R}):\hat{t}_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\hat{\beta})\}
is one.
∎

Proposition 7.9
.

The statement of Theorem
7.1
(i) holds for
\beta=\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}
, for all
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as the previous one, with a minor modification. Here we may assume that
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k}
with
k\geq 2
and each
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}\in F^{*}
, since for
{\mathbf{S}}\in F^{*}
we already know
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive for all
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)
by Theorem
6.1
. Set
{\mathbf{S}}_{i}:={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,k
, and write
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{{\mathbf{S}}_{i}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}_{i}))
for
i=1,\dots,k-1
, and certain bases
\hat{\beta}_{1},\dots,\hat{\beta}_{k-1}
. Observe, however, that
\alpha(\beta)=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{k})^{+}{\mathbf{S}}_{k}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{k})^{\infty}=\Phi_{{\mathbf{S}}_{k}}(10^{\infty})
so there is no corresponding base
\hat{\beta}_{k}
.

Let
\mathbf{w}
be a
\beta
-Lyndon word and
t_{R}
the corresponding point such that
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
. If
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{r}}_{1}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{1})^{\infty}={\mathbf{S}}_{1}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{1})^{\infty}
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive by Proposition
7.6
. Otherwise, there is a unique integer
i\leq k-1
such that

(7.5)

{\mathbf{S}}_{i}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{i})^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}_{i+1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{i+1})^{\infty},

because
\tau(\beta)=\pi_{\beta}\big({\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\big)
. Let
\hat{t}_{R}
be the point such that
b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta}_{i})=\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}
.

•

If
i<k-1
, then
\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{r}}_{i+1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{i+1})^{\infty}
by (
7.5
). Observe that
\alpha(\hat{\beta}_{i})=\Phi_{{\mathbf{r}}_{i+1}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}_{i+1}))
. So by Proposition
7.6
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}_{i}}(\hat{t}_{R})
is transitive.

•

If
i=k-1
, then
\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{r}}_{k}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{k})^{\infty}
. Also
\beta_{k-1}=\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{r}}_{k}}
. Hence,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}_{k-1}}(\hat{t}_{R})
is transitive by Theorem
6.1
.

In both cases,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}_{i}}(\hat{t}_{R})
is transitive. The rest of the proof is now the same as before.
∎

7.1.
Density of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals

In order to complete the proof of Theorem
7.1
, we need the following extension of Proposition
5.9
.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, and
\beta\in\overline{E^{\mathbf{S}}}
. Then the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
.

The proof uses the following lemma.

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, and let bases
\beta
and
\hat{\beta}
be related by
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
. Define the map

(7.6)

\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}:[0,1)\to[0,1);\qquad\hat{t}\mapsto\pi_{\beta}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}\circ b(\hat{t},\hat{\beta}).

If
[\hat{t}_{L},\hat{t}_{R}]
is a
\hat{\beta}
-Lyndon interval, then
[\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{L}-),\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{R})]
is a
\beta
-Lyndon interval containing the point
\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{L})
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{\mathbf{w}}
be the
\hat{\beta}
-Lyndon word such that
b(\hat{t}_{L},\hat{\beta})=\hat{\mathbf{w}}0^{\infty}
. Write
\alpha(\hat{\beta})=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\dots
. Consider the sequence
(\hat{\mathbf{z}}_{n})
defined by

\hat{\mathbf{z}}_{n}:=\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{-}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{n}0^{\infty},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},

and set
t_{n}:=\pi_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}_{n})
. It is clear that
t_{n}\nearrow\pi_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{-}\alpha(\hat{\beta}))=\pi_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}0^{\infty})=\hat{t}_{L}
, and that
\hat{\mathbf{z}}_{n}
is the greedy
\hat{\beta}
-expansion of
t_{n}
. So, using that
\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}
is strictly increasing and the maps
\pi_{\beta}
and
\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}
are continuous, we obtain

\displaystyle\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{L}-)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\nearrow\hat{t}_{L}}\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(t)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{\beta}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}}_{n})

\displaystyle=\pi_{\beta}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{-}\alpha(\hat{\beta}))=\pi_{\beta}\big(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{-})\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))\big)

\displaystyle=\pi_{\beta}\big(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})^{-}\alpha(\beta)\big)=\pi_{\beta}\big(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})0^{\infty}\big).

Moreover,
\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{R})=\pi_{\beta}\big(\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty})\big)
. Thus,
[\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{L}-),\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{R})]
is the
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}),\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})]
, where
\mathbf{w}:=\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})
. That this interval contains the point
\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{L})
follows since

\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})0^{\infty}\prec\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}){\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}=\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}0^{\infty})\preccurlyeq\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})^{\infty}.

∎

Proof of Proposition
7.10
.

We set
t_{*}:=\pi_{\beta}\big({\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\big)
, and divide the proof in two parts.

Step 1.
The interval
[t_{*},\tau(\beta)]
. If
\beta=\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}
, then
\tau(\beta)=t_{*}
so this interval degenerates to a point and there is nothing to prove. So assume
\beta\in\overline{E^{\mathbf{S}}}\backslash\{\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}\}
. Then
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
for some
\hat{\beta}\in\overline{E}
. Using the map
\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}
from Lemma
7.11
, we observe that
t_{*}=\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(0)
and
\tau(\beta)=\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\tau(\hat{\beta}))
(see
[
4
, Proposition 6.2]
). Proposition
5.9
tells us that the
\hat{\beta}
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau(\hat{\beta})]
. Since the Lyndon intervals have the property that any two of them are either disjoint or one contains the other (see
[
22
, Proposition 4.1]
), it follows that every Lyndon interval in
[0,\tau(\hat{\beta})]
is contained in a
\hat{\beta}
-Lyndon interval.

Although the function
\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}
is not continuous (it jumps where the greedy expansion
t\mapsto b(t,\hat{\beta})
does), we claim that the intervals
[\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{L}-),\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{R})]
, as
[\hat{t}_{L},\hat{t}_{R}]
ranges over the
\hat{\beta}
-Lyndon intervals, cover all the jumps of
\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}
. Note that
\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}
jumps only at points of the form
\pi_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathbf{w}0^{\infty})
, where
\mathbf{w}
is a word ending in 1 such that
\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}
is a greedy
\hat{\beta}
-expansion. Let
{\mathbf{r}}
be the longest prefix of
\mathbf{w}
that is Lyndon. Then the Lyndon interval
[\pi_{\hat{\beta}}({\mathbf{r}}0^{\infty}),\pi_{\hat{\beta}}({\mathbf{r}}^{\infty})]
contains
\pi_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathbf{w}0^{\infty})
. Since this Lyndon interval is contained in some
\hat{\beta}
-Lyndon interval
[\hat{t}_{L},\hat{t}_{R}]
, and
[\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{L}-),\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{R})]
contains the “jump”
[\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{L}-),\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{L})]
by Lemma
7.11
, the claim follows. But then the intervals
[\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{L}-),\Theta_{{\mathbf{S}},\beta}(\hat{t}_{R})]
, which are
\beta
-Lyndon by Lemma
7.11
, are dense in
[t_{*},\tau(\beta)]
.

Step 2.
The interval
[0,t_{*})
. Write
\alpha(\beta)=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\dots
, and
{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\gamma_{3}\dots
. Let
t\in(0,t_{*})
, so
(t_{i}):=b(t,\beta)\prec(\gamma_{i})
. We will show that

\sigma^{n}((t_{1}\dots t_{k})^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta)\qquad\forall\,n\geq 0,\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}.

The density of the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals then follows in the same way as in the proof of Proposition
5.9
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq n<k
. Since
(t_{i})
is a greedy
\beta
-expansion,
t_{n+1}\dots t_{k}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k-n}
. We will now show that
(t_{1}\dots t_{k})^{\infty}\prec\sigma^{k-n}(\alpha(\beta))
.
Since
\beta\in\overline{E^{\mathbf{S}}}
, it follows that
\alpha(\beta)\in X({\mathbf{S}})
and so
\sigma^{l}(\alpha(\beta))\succcurlyeq{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\ldots
for all
l\geq 0
. Thus, it suffices to show that

(7.7)

(t_{1}\dots t_{k})^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}.

Let
m:=|{\mathbf{S}}|
. Observe that
t_{1}\dots t_{k}0^{\infty}\preccurlyeq(t_{i})\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}
. If
k\geq m
, then we see that
t_{1}\dots t_{m}\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\prec\gamma_{k+1}\dots\gamma_{k+m}
, giving (
7.7
).

So suppose
k<m
. Let
q
and
r
be integers such that
m=qk+r
and
0\leq r<k
. Then since
{\mathbf{S}}
is Lyndon,

t_{1}\dots t_{k}\preccurlyeq s_{1}\dots s_{k}\preccurlyeq s_{pk+1}\dots s_{(p+1)k},\qquad p=0,1,\dots,q-1,

and

t_{1}\dots t_{r}\preccurlyeq s_{1}\dots s_{r}\preccurlyeq s_{qk+1}\dots s_{m}^{-}.

Thus,

(t_{1}\dots t_{k})^{q}t_{1}\dots t_{r}\preccurlyeq s_{1}\dots s_{m}^{-},

and if this holds with equality, then

t_{r+1}\dots t_{k}(t_{1}\dots t_{k})^{q-1}t_{1}\dots t_{r}=s_{r+1}\dots s_{m}^{-}\prec s_{r+1}\dots s_{m}\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})_{1:m-r},

where the last inequality follows from the definition of
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})
. Putting these results together, we obtain

(t_{1}\dots t_{k})^{2q}t_{1}\dots t_{r}\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})_{1:m-r},

yielding (
7.7
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

Statement (i) follows Propositions
7.8
and
7.9
, and (ii) follows from Proposition
7.10
. ∎

8.
Comparison of the bifurcation sets

Our aim in this section is to prove the following extension of Corollary
5.11
.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{2}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k}\in\Lambda
, where
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}\in F^{*}
for each
i
. Suppose
\beta\in\{\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{r}^{\mathbf{S}}\}
.

(i)

If
\beta=\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}}
, then
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
consists exactly of the
k-1
points whose greedy
\beta
-expansions, in decreasing order, are

(8.1)

{\mathbf{r}}_{1}^{\infty},\qquad({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{2})^{\infty},\qquad\dots,\qquad({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{2}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k-1})^{\infty}.

(ii)

If
\beta=\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}
or
\beta_{r}^{\mathbf{S}}
, then
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
consists exactly of the
k
points whose greedy
\beta
-expansions, in decreasing order, are

{\mathbf{r}}_{1}^{\infty},\qquad({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{2})^{\infty},\qquad\dots,\qquad({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{2}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k})^{\infty}.

The proof uses the following lemma. Recall the definition of
\Gamma({\mathbf{r}})
from (
7.2
).

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{r}},{\mathbf{s}}\in\Lambda
, and suppose
\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
satisfies

(8.2)

{\mathbf{r}}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})^{+}({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}\qquad\forall n\geq 0.

Then
\mathbf{z}\in\Gamma({\mathbf{r}})
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})^{+}
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}
and
{\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}}
begins with
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
, the word
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})^{+}({\mathbf{r}}\bullet{\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}{\mathbf{r}}^{k}{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
for some finite
k
. Hence,
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}1^{\infty}
for all
n\geq 0
, and if
\mathbf{z}
ever contains
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}
, then the next block of length
(k+1)|{\mathbf{r}}|
must be
\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{r}}^{k}{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
and also
\succcurlyeq{\mathbf{r}}^{k+1}
by the lower bound in (
8.2
). This is impossible, so
\mathbf{z}\in\Gamma({\mathbf{r}})
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.1
.

We begin with (i). For
k=1
this is simply Corollary
5.11
, so assume
k\geq 2
. Let
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{2}\bullet\cdots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k}\in\Lambda
, where
{\mathbf{r}}_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{r}}_{k}\in F^{*}
. Let
\beta=\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}}
. From Theorem
7.1
and Proposition
4.6
it follows that
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[0,\tau(\beta))=\mathscr{B}_{\beta}\cap[0,\tau(\beta))
, so it suffices to show that
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
has precisely
k-1
points in the interval
[\tau(\beta),1)
, whose greedy
\beta
-expansions are given by (
8.1
).

Observe from
[
4
, Theorem 2]
that

\tau(\beta)=\pi_{\beta}\big({\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\big)
.
Let

t_{j}:=\pi_{\beta}\big(({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j})^{\infty}\big),\qquad j=1,2,\dots,k-1.

Note that
({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j})^{\infty}
is indeed the greedy
\beta
-expansion of
t_{j}
, since

\mathbb{L}(({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j})^{\infty})\prec\mathbb{L}(({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k})^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta)
. Noting also that
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j}
is Lyndon, it follows that
T^{n}(t_{j})\geq t_{j}
for every
n\geq 0
, so that
t_{j}\in\mathscr{E}_{\beta}^{+}=\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
.
Furthermore,
({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\cdots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j})^{\infty}\succ{\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\cdots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k}^{-}1^{\infty}\succ{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}
. Thus,
t_{j}\in(\tau(\beta),1)
.

It remains to show that there are no further points of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
in
(\tau(\beta),1)
.

Recall that
k\geq 2
. We first claim that
K_{\beta}(t)=\emptyset
for all
t>t_{1}
, so there are no points of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
to the right of
t_{1}
. This follows since

\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t_{1})=\{\mathbf{z}:{\mathbf{r}}_{1}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\ \ \forall n\geq 0\}=\Gamma({\mathbf{r}}_{1})

by Lemma
8.2
, so
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t_{1})
consists only of
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}^{\infty}
and its shifts by Lemma
6.2
, and none of these lie in
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t)
for
t>t_{1}
.

Next, we show that there are no points of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
in
(\tau(\beta),t_{k-1})
; in other words,
K_{\beta}(t)
is constant on
(\tau(\beta),t_{k-1}]
. Note that

\tau(\beta)=\pi_{\beta}\big({\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\big)=\pi_{\beta}\big({\mathbf{S}}0^{\infty}\big)=\pi_{\beta}\big(({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k})0^{\infty}\big)

and
t_{k-1}=\pi_{\beta}\big(({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k-1})^{\infty}\big)
. Let
t\in(\tau(\beta),t_{k-1})
and suppose
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t)
. We claim that
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t_{k-1})
. Observe that

(8.3)

{\mathbf{S}}0^{\infty}\prec b(t,\beta)\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\quad\forall n\geq 0.

Since
{\mathbf{S}}
begins with
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k-1}^{-}
, we have
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\succcurlyeq({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k-1}^{-})0^{\infty}
for all
n\geq 0
. Suppose
\mathbf{z}
contains the word
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k-1}^{-}
; without loss of generality we may assume
\mathbf{z}
begins with it. Then by Lemma
7.2
,

\mathbf{z}=\Phi_{{\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k-1}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})

for some sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
satisfying

{\mathbf{r}}_{k}0^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{k})^{\infty}\quad\forall n\geq 0,

in view of (
8.3
). However, by Lemma
6.2
such a sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
does not exist. Therefore,
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\succcurlyeq({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k-1})^{\infty}
for all
n\geq 0
, which implies
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t_{k-1})
.

Note that we are now done with the case
k=2
, so fix
k\geq 3
from now on.
Fix
1\leq j\leq k-2
, and let
t\in(t_{j+1},t_{j})
. Suppose by way of contradiction that there exists a sequence
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t)\backslash\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t_{j})
. Then

(8.4)

{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j+1})^{\infty}=b(t_{j+1},\beta)\prec b(t,\beta)\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\prec\alpha(\beta)=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\qquad\forall n\geq 0,

and there is an integer
n_{0}\geq 0
such that
\sigma^{n_{0}}(\mathbf{z})\prec b(t_{j},\beta)=({\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j})^{\infty}
.
Since
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j+1}
begins with
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j}^{-}
, there is therefore a further integer
m_{0}\geq n_{0}
such that
\sigma^{m_{0}}(\mathbf{z})
begins with
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j}^{-}
. Set
{\mathbf{r}}:={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j}
and
{\mathbf{s}}:={\mathbf{r}}_{j+1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k}
. Applying Lemma
7.2
with
\mathbf{x}={\mathbf{s}}^{\infty}
and
\mathbf{y}=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}
we conclude that

\mathbf{z}=\Phi_{{\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{j}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})

for some sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
, which by (
8.4
) satisfies the inequalities

(8.5)

{\mathbf{r}}_{j+1}^{\infty}\prec\sigma^{n}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{j+1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{k})^{\infty}\qquad\forall n\geq 0.

But then

(8.6)

{\mathbf{r}}_{j+1}^{\infty}\prec\sigma^{n}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{j+1})^{\infty}\qquad\forall n\geq 0

by the obvious modification of Lemma
8.2
. However, no sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
satisfying (
8.6
) exists by Lemma
6.2
. Hence,
K_{\beta}(t)=K_{\beta}(t_{j})
.

This proves (i). The proof of (ii) is essentially the same, using that

\tau(\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}})=\pi_{\beta}\big({\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\big),\qquad\tau(\beta_{r}^{\mathbf{S}})=\pi_{\beta}\big({\mathbf{S}}0^{\infty}\big).

The reason for the extra bifurcation point is that when
\beta=\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}
or
\beta_{r}^{\mathbf{S}}
, the word
{\mathbf{S}}
is actually
\beta
-Lyndon, whereas for
\beta=\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}}
it is not.
∎

9.
The case of infinitely renormalizable
\beta

Recall the set
E_{\infty}
from (
2.11
). In this section we prove the following.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
\beta\in E_{\infty}
. Then

(i)

For every
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
has a transitive subshift
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
of full entropy and full Hausdorff dimension that contains the sequence
b(t_{R},\beta)
. Moreover, these transitive subshifts can be chosen so that
\{\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}):t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)\}
is a strictly descending collection of subshifts.

(ii)

The
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
.

Part (i) gives Theorem
4.4
for
\beta\in E_{\infty}
.

Proof.

(i) Since
\beta\in E_{\infty}
, there is an infinite sequence
\{{\mathbf{s}}_{1},{\mathbf{s}}_{2},\dots\}
of Farey words, uniquely determined by
\beta
, such that
\beta\in J^{{\mathbf{s}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{s}}_{n}}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Write
{\mathbf{S}}_{i}:={\mathbf{s}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{s}}_{i}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and note that for each
i
there is a base
\hat{\beta}_{i}
such that
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{{\mathbf{S}}_{i}}\big(\alpha(\hat{\beta}_{i})\big)
. Recall from
[
4
, Proposition 6.3]
that

(9.1)

\tau(\beta)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{\beta}({\mathbf{s}}_{1}\bullet\cdots\bullet{\mathbf{s}}_{n}0^{\infty}).

Hence, given a point
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}
, we either have
b(t_{R},\beta)\prec{\mathbf{s}}_{1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}}_{1})^{\infty}
, in which case
