\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive by Proposition
7.6
, or else there is a unique
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that

{\mathbf{S}}_{i}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{i})^{\infty}\prec b(t_{R},\beta)\prec{\mathbf{S}}_{i+1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{i+1})^{\infty}.

We conclude as in the proof of Proposition
7.8
that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}_{i}}(\hat{t}_{R})
is transitive. The rest of the proof is the same as the proof of Proposition
7.8
.

(ii) For each
n
, the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau_{n}]
by applying the argument in Step 2 of the proof of Proposition
7.10
to
{\mathbf{S}}:={\mathbf{s}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{s}}_{n}
. Since
\tau_{n}\nearrow\tau(\beta)
, this implies the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
.
∎

A simple modification of the proof of Theorem
8.1
yields the following:

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
\beta\in E_{\infty}
with corresponding sequence
{\mathbf{s}}_{1},{\mathbf{s}}_{2},\dots
of Farey words. Then
\tau(\beta)
is given by (
9.1
),
and
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
has countably infinitely many points in the interval
(\tau(\beta),1)
, namely the points whose greedy
\beta
-expansions, in decreasing order, are

{\mathbf{s}}_{1}^{\infty},\qquad({\mathbf{s}}_{1}\bullet{\mathbf{s}}_{2})^{\infty},\qquad({\mathbf{s}}_{1}\bullet{\mathbf{s}}_{2}\bullet{\mathbf{s}}_{3})^{\infty},\qquad\dots.

Unfortunately, the
\beta
-shift
\Sigma_{\beta}
is never sofic for
\beta\in E_{\infty}
, as it is easy to check that
\alpha(\beta)
cannot be eventually periodic. As a result, the subshifts
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
are not all sofic, and therefore our argument from the previous section that
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[0,\tau(\beta)]=\mathscr{B}_{\beta}\cap[0,\tau(\beta)]
is not valid. We suspect, but have been unable to prove, that this equation is nonetheless true, and hence
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
is countably infinite for
\beta\in E_{\infty}
.

10.
Interiors of basic intervals: Construction of non-transitivity windows

We now begin to deal with the most complex case, which is when
\beta
lies in the interior of a basic interval. Before developing the general theory, we give a concrete example which illustrates the main ideas.

Example 10.1
.

Let
\beta
be as in Example
5.10
such that
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
1110101100
, and set
\mathbf{w}=01010111
. For
t=t_{R}
given by
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
, the subshift
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
is not transitive: Note that, in fact,

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:(01010111)^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq(11101010)^{\infty}\ \forall\,n\geq 0\},

so the only sequence in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
beginning with
111
is
(11101010)^{\infty}
, and the legal word
111
cannot be connected to, for instance, the legal word
011011
. On the other hand, disallowing the word
111
removes only countably many sequences from
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
, so the subshift

\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t):=\{\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t):\mathbf{z}\ \mbox{does not contain $111$}\}

has full entropy in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
. Note that
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t)
consists precisely of those sequences of the form

(110)^{k_{1}}10(110)^{k_{2}}10(110)^{k_{3}}10\dots,\qquad k_{i}\geq 1\ \forall i,

together with their orbits under
\sigma
, and is clearly transitive. Now let
\mathbf{w}^{*}=01011
and let
t^{*}
be given by
b(t^{*},\beta)=(\mathbf{w}^{*})^{\infty}
. It is easy to see that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t^{*})=\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t)
. Hence,

h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)\big)=h\big(\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t)\big)=h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t^{*})\big).

Observe that
\mathbf{w}^{*}
is the lexicographically smallest
\beta
-Lyndon word greater than
\mathbf{w}
. Note also that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
does
not
have a transitive subshift of full entropy containing the sequence
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
.

Developing the example a bit further, we set
\mathbf{w}_{k}:=0101(011)^{k}0111
for
k=0,1,2,\dots
, and let
t_{k}
be the point given by
b(t_{k},\beta)=\mathbf{w}_{k}^{\infty}
. As above it can be shown that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{k})
is not transitive, though here there are more sequences in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{k})
beginning with
111
. With a bit more effort, it can be shown that
h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{k})\big)=h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t^{*})\big)
. Observe that
t_{k}\searrow t_{\infty}
, where
t_{\infty}
is given by
b(t_{\infty},\beta)=0101(011)^{\infty}
. By the continuity of
t\mapsto h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
, it follows that
h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{\infty})\big)=h\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t^{*})\big)
.

We call the interval
[t_{\infty},t^{*})
a
non-transitivity window
. The assertions about entropy in the last paragraph will follow from Proposition
11.6
below.

We saw in Section
5
that if
\beta\in E_{L}
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive for any
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]\subseteq[0,\tau(\beta)]
. On the other hand, the above example shows that this need no longer be the case when
\beta
lies in the interior of a basic interval. We will now examine the question of transitivity for the interior of basic intervals in greater detail. We will show that, depending on
\beta
, there is a collection of intervals which we call
non-transitivity windows
, such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive if and only if
t_{R}
does not lie in any of these intervals. The number of non-transitivity windows can be zero, positive and finite, or infinite, depending on
\beta
.

We first prove a simple but useful lemma.

Lemma 10.2
.

If a word
\mathbf{v}
is Lyndon but not
\beta
-Lyndon, then no word extending
\mathbf{v}
is
\beta
-Lyndon.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathbf{v}=v_{1}\dots v_{k}
is Lyndon but not
\beta
-Lyndon, so there is an integer
j<k
such that

(10.1)

v_{j+1}\dots v_{k}v_{1}\dots v_{j}\succcurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k}.

Let
\mathbf{w}=v_{1}\dots v_{k}v_{k+1}\dots v_{l}
be an extension of
\mathbf{v}
. Assume
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon, as otherwise there is nothing to prove. Write
l=qk+r
, where
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq r<k
. Since
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon,
v_{ik+1}\dots v_{(i+1)k}\succcurlyeq v_{1}\dots v_{k}
for all
1\leq i<q
, with strict inequality for
i=q-1
in case
r=0
; and
v_{qk+1}\dots v_{l}=v_{qk+1}\dots v_{qk+r}\succ v_{1}\dots v_{r}
if
r>0
. Thus, with
j
as in (
10.1
),

v_{j+1}\dots v_{l}\succ v_{j+1}\dots v_{k}(v_{1}\dots v_{k})^{q-1}v_{1}\dots v_{r}.

If
q\geq 2
or
r\geq j
, this gives

(v_{j+1}\dots v_{l}v_{1}\dots v_{j})^{\infty}\succ v_{j+1}\dots v_{l}0^{\infty}\succ(v_{j+1}\dots v_{k}v_{1}\dots v_{j})^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\alpha(\beta).

On the other hand, if
q=1
and
r<j
, then
l-k=r<j
and hence

v_{j+1}\dots v_{l}\succ v_{j+1}\dots v_{k}v_{1}\dots v_{l-k}\succcurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{l-j},

using (
10.1
). In both cases, it follows that
\mathbf{w}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
.
∎

Now we fix a basic interval
[\beta_{\ell},\beta_{*}]=[\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}}]
generated by a word
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, and fix
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell},\beta_{*})
. Recall from
[
4
, Theorem 2]
that

\tau(\beta)=\pi_{\beta}\big({\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\big).

Assume first that
\alpha(\beta)=\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\dots
is not periodic. We will inductively build a collection
\mathcal{I}
of intervals in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
as follows.

To begin, set
j_{1}:=|{\mathbf{S}}|
. Then
\sigma^{j_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))\prec\sigma^{j_{1}}(\alpha(\beta_{*}))={\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}
. If
\sigma^{j_{1}+n}(\alpha(\beta))\succ\sigma^{j_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))
for all
n\geq 1
, then we set
\mathcal{I}:=\emptyset
. Otherwise, let

n_{1}:=\min\{n\geq 1:\sigma^{j_{1}+n}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq\sigma^{j_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))\}.

Then we set
\mathbf{v}_{1}:=\alpha_{j_{1}+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}+n_{1}}
, and note that
\mathbf{v}_{1}
is Lyndon (though not necessarily
\beta
-Lyndon). To see this, take
1\leq i<n_{1}
and observe by the definition of
n_{1}
that

\sigma^{j_{1}+n_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq\sigma^{j_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))\prec\sigma^{j_{1}+n_{1}-i}(\alpha(\beta)).

Thus, by the minimality of
n_{1}
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{j_{1}+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}+n_{1}-i}\sigma^{j_{1}+n_{1}-i}(\alpha(\beta))

\displaystyle=\sigma^{j_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))\prec\sigma^{j_{1}+i}(\alpha(\beta))

\displaystyle=\alpha_{j_{1}+i+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}+n_{1}}\sigma^{j_{1}+n_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))

\displaystyle\prec\alpha_{j_{1}+i+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}+n_{1}}\sigma^{j_{1}+n_{1}-i}(\alpha(\beta)),

and this implies
\alpha_{j_{1}+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}+n_{1}-i}\prec\alpha_{j_{1}+i+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{1}}
.

We define an interval
I_{1}=[\underline{t}_{1},\overline{t}_{1})
, where
\underline{t}_{1}
and
\overline{t}_{1}
are given implicitly by

b(\underline{t}_{1},\beta)=\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{\infty}=\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty},\qquad b(\overline{t}_{1},\beta)=(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*})^{\infty},

where for any Lyndon word
\mathbf{v}
,
\mathbf{v}^{*}
denotes the (lexicographically) smallest
\beta
-Lyndon word such that
(\mathbf{v}^{*})^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\mathbf{v}^{\infty}
.
(In particular,
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}=\mathbf{v}_{1}
if
\mathbf{v}_{1}
is already
\beta
-Lyndon.) Note by Lemma
10.2
that if
\mathbf{v}_{1}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
, then
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}
is well defined and
|\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}|<|\mathbf{v}_{1}|
.
We put the interval
I_{1}
in the collection
\mathcal{I}
.

Next, we set
j_{2}:=j_{1}+n_{1}
and continue the procedure in the same way. That is, if
\sigma^{j_{2}+n}(\alpha(\beta))\succ\sigma^{j_{2}}(\alpha(\beta))
for all
n\geq 1
, then we set
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1}\}
and stop. Otherwise, let

n_{2}:=\min\{n\geq 1:\sigma^{j_{2}+n}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq\sigma^{j_{2}}(\alpha(\beta))\},

and set
\mathbf{v}_{2}:=\alpha_{j_{2}+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{2}+n_{2}}
. Observe that
\mathbf{v}_{2}
is Lyndon by the same argument that we used above for
\mathbf{v}_{1}
. We define an interval
I_{2}=[\underline{t}_{2},\overline{t}_{2})
, where
\underline{t}_{2}
and
\overline{t}_{2}
are given implicitly by

b(\underline{t}_{2},\beta)=\mathbf{v}_{2}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{2}}^{-})^{\infty}=\mathbf{v}_{2}^{-}(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-})^{\infty},\qquad b(\overline{t}_{2},\beta)=(\mathbf{v}_{2}^{*})^{\infty}.

We add
I_{2}
to the collection
\mathcal{I}
.

This way we continue: Suppose integers
j_{1},\dots,j_{k}
and
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}
, words
\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{k}
and intervals
I_{1}=[\underline{t}_{1},\overline{t}_{1}),\dots,I_{k}=[\underline{t}_{k},\overline{t}_{k})
have been constructed, and set
j_{k+1}:=j_{k}+n_{k}
. If
\sigma^{j_{k+1}+n}(\alpha(\beta))\succ\sigma^{j_{k+1}}(\alpha(\beta))
for all
n\geq 1
, then we set
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}
and stop. Otherwise, let

n_{k+1}:=\min\{n\geq 1:\sigma^{j_{k+1}+n}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq\sigma^{j_{k+1}}(\alpha(\beta))\},

and set
\mathbf{v}_{k+1}:=\alpha_{j_{k+1}+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k+1}+n_{k+1}}
. We define an interval
I_{k+1}=[\underline{t}_{k+1},\overline{t}_{k+1})
, where
\underline{t}_{k+1}
and
\overline{t}_{k+1}
are given implicitly by

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{k+1},\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{k+1}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k+1}}^{-})^{\infty}=\mathbf{v}_{k+1}^{-}\big(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k-1}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}\big)^{\infty},

\displaystyle b(\overline{t}_{k+1},\beta)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{v}_{k+1}^{*})^{\infty},

However, in the special case when
\mathbf{v}_{k+1}\in L^{*}(\beta)
and
\sigma^{j_{k+1}}(\alpha(\beta))=\mathbf{v}_{k+1}^{\infty}
, we take the interval
I_{k+1}
to be closed, i.e.
I_{k+1}=[\underline{t}_{k+1},\overline{t}_{k+1}]
. We add
I_{k+1}
to the collection
\mathcal{I}
.

This procedure either eventually stops, with a finite collection
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k_{0}}\}
, or it continues ad infinitum, producing an infinite collection
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots\}
of intervals. Note that if
I_{k}
is closed for some
k
, then
\mathbf{v}_{l}=\mathbf{v}_{k}
for all
l>k
and hence
I_{l}\subseteq I_{k}
for all
l>k
.

In case
\alpha(\beta)
is periodic, i.e.
\alpha(\beta)=(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m})^{\infty}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\alpha_{m}=0
, we replace
(\alpha_{i})
in the above procedure with the greedy expansion of
1
, that is, the sequence
(\alpha_{i}^{\prime}):=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m-1}10^{\infty}
. The inductive step needs to be slightly modified: If
\sigma^{j_{k+1}}((\alpha_{i}^{\prime}))=0^{\infty}
, we leave
n_{k+1}
undefined and stop the construction with the finite set
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}
.

The following special case of Theorem
4.4
is our main result in this section.

Theorem 10.3
.

Let
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell},\beta_{*})
for some basic interval
[\beta_{\ell},\beta_{*}]=[\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}]
, where
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda_{1}=F^{*}
, and construct the collection
\mathcal{I}
of intervals as above. Let
[t_{L},t_{R}]\subseteq[0,\tau(\beta)]
be a
\beta
-Lyndon interval. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive if and only if
t_{R}\not\in\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I
. Furthermore, the entropy of
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
is constant on each interval
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

The situation for
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda_{k}
with
k\geq 2
is a bit more involved, and we deal with it later, in Section
12
. However, it is convenient to develop the necessary machinery in greater generality here.

Before proving the theorem, which shows the significance of the words
\mathbf{v}_{k}
and intervals
I_{k}
, we first give some examples.

Example 10.4
.

Let
{\mathbf{S}}=011
, so
{\mathbf{S}}\in F^{*}
and the basic interval
[\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}}]
is given by

\alpha(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}=(110)^{\infty},\qquad\alpha(\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}=111\,010\,(110)^{\infty}.

(a)

Suppose
\alpha(\beta)=111\,01\,00110111\,(001)^{\infty}
. Then
\mathbf{v}_{1}=01
,
\mathbf{v}_{2}=00110111
, and
\mathbf{v}_{k}=001
for all
k\geq 3
. Since each
\mathbf{v}_{k}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, we have
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}=\mathbf{v}_{k}
for each
k
. Now our construction above gives rise to the intervals
I_{1}=[\underline{t}_{1},\overline{t}_{1}),I_{2}=[\underline{t}_{2},\overline{t}_{2}),\dots
given by

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{1},\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}=00\,(110)^{\infty},

\displaystyle\quad b(\overline{t}_{1},\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty}=(01)^{\infty},

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{1},\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{2}^{-}\big(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-}\big)^{\infty}=00110110\,(11100)^{\infty},

\displaystyle\quad b(\overline{t}_{2},\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{2}^{\infty}=(00110111)^{\infty},

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{3},\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{3}^{-}\big(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\mathbf{v}_{2}^{-}\big)^{\infty}=000\,(111\,01\,00110110)^{\infty},

\displaystyle\quad b(\overline{t}_{3},\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{3}^{\infty}=(001)^{\infty},

and for
k\geq 4
,

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{k},\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}\big(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{-}\big)^{\infty}=000\,\big(111\,01\,00110111\,(001)^{k-4}\,000\big)^{\infty},

\displaystyle b(\overline{t}_{k},\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}=(001)^{\infty}.

Observe that
I_{2}\subseteq I_{1}
,
I_{3}
lies to the left of
I_{2}
, and
I_{k+1}\subseteq I_{k}
for all
k\geq 3
.

(b)

Suppose
\alpha(\beta)=111\,01\,001(01)^{\infty}
. Then
\mathbf{v}_{1}=01
, and the procedure stops after one step, giving rise to just one interval
I_{1}=[\underline{t}_{1},\overline{t}_{1})
, which is the same as in (a).

(c)

More extremely, let
\alpha(\beta)=111\,001(01)^{\infty}
. Then
\sigma^{j_{1}+n}(\alpha(\beta))\succ\sigma^{j_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))
for all
n\geq 1
(where
j_{1}=|{\mathbf{S}}|=3
), and hence we obtain the empty collection
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
.

(d)

Let
\alpha(\beta)=111\,01\,001\,0001\dots
. Then
\mathbf{v}_{k}=0^{k}1
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and we get an infinite collection
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots\}
of pairwise disjoint intervals, with left endpoints given by

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{1},\beta)=00\,(110)^{\infty},\qquad b(\underline{t}_{2},\beta)=000\,(11100)^{\infty},

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{k},\beta)=0^{k+1}\,\big(111\,01\,001\,\dots\,0^{k-2}1\,0^{k}\big)^{\infty}\qquad(k\geq 3),

and right endpoints given by

b(\overline{t}_{k},\beta)=(0^{k}1)^{\infty},\qquad k=1,2,\dots.

Note that in this example, the intervals
I_{k}
accumulate at
0
.

(e)

Suppose
\alpha(\beta)=111\,00111\,(001)^{\infty}
. Then
\mathbf{v}_{1}=00111
and
\mathbf{v}_{k}=001
for all
k\geq 2
. Note that here
\mathbf{v}_{1}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
since
\sigma^{2}(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty})=(11100)^{\infty}\succ\alpha(\beta)
. It is easy to check that
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}=01
. Thus, we get a collection of intervals
I_{1}=[\underline{t}_{1},\overline{t}_{1}),I_{2}=[\underline{t}_{2},\overline{t}_{2}),\dots
given by

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{1},\beta)

\displaystyle=00110\,(110)^{\infty},

\displaystyle\qquad b(\overline{t}_{1},\beta)

\displaystyle=(01)^{\infty},

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{2},\beta)

\displaystyle=000\,(111\,00110)^{\infty},

\displaystyle\qquad b(\overline{t}_{2},\beta)

\displaystyle=(001)^{\infty},

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{k},\beta)

\displaystyle=000\,\big(111\,00111\,(001)^{k-3}\,000\big)^{\infty},

\displaystyle\qquad b(\overline{t}_{k},\beta)

\displaystyle=(001)^{\infty}\qquad(k\geq 3).

(f)

Let
\alpha(\beta)=(111\,0101100)^{\infty}
. Since
\alpha(\beta)
is periodic, we apply the construction to the greedy expansion
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\alpha_{2}^{\prime}\dots=111\,01011\,01\,0^{\infty}
. We find
\mathbf{v}_{1}=01011
and
\mathbf{v}_{2}=01
. The intervals
I_{1}=[\underline{t}_{1},\overline{t}_{1})
and
I_{2}=[\underline{t}_{2},\overline{t}_{2})
are given by

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{1},\beta)

\displaystyle=01010\,(110)^{\infty},

\displaystyle\qquad b(\overline{t}_{1},\beta)

\displaystyle=(01011)^{\infty},

\displaystyle b(\underline{t}_{2},\beta)

\displaystyle=00\,(111\,01010)^{\infty},

\displaystyle\qquad b(\overline{t}_{2},\beta)

\displaystyle=(01)^{\infty}.

The proof of Theorem
10.3
is rather involved.
We first prove several lemmas about the words
\mathbf{v}_{k}
. These lemmas lead to Proposition
10.13
, which describes the relative placements of the intervals
I_{k}
. The first lemma deals specifically with the case of periodic
\alpha(\beta)
; see
[
22
, Lemma 3.8]
.

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
\beta\in(1,2)
, and suppose
\alpha(\beta)=(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m})^{\infty}
where
m
is the minimal period of
\alpha(\beta)
. Then

\alpha_{j+1}\dots\alpha_{m}\prec\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m-j}\qquad\forall\,1\leq j<m.

Below we write
\mathbf{v}_{k}=v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,n_{k}}
. Recall also that
n_{k}
is the length of
\mathbf{v}_{k}
.

Lemma 10.6
.

For any
k
and any two integers
0\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq n_{k}
,

(10.2)

v_{k,i_{1}+1}\dots v_{k,i_{2}}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{i_{2}-i_{1}}.

Proof.

When
\alpha(\beta)
is aperiodic the inequality follows immediately from Lemma
2.1
, since
\mathbf{v}_{k}
is a subword of
\alpha(\beta)
. But in the periodic case some extra care is needed. In this case there is a positive integer
k_{0}
such that
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k_{0}}\}
, and
\alpha(\beta)=(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{-})^{\infty}
. The only case that needs extra attention is when
k=k_{0}
and
i_{2}=n_{k_{0}}
. Set
i:=i_{1}
. It seems theoretically possible that
v_{k_{0},i+1}\dots v_{k_{0},n_{k_{0}}}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{k_{0}}-i}^{+}
, violating (
10.2
). However, if that were the case we would have

\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k_{0}}}\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{-}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k_{0}}}v_{k_{0},1}\dots v_{k_{0},i}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{k_{0}}-i},

where
m
is the minimal period of
\alpha(\beta)
, so applying Lemma
10.5
with
j=n_{k_{0}}-i
gives

\sigma^{j_{k_{0}}+i}(\alpha(\beta))=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{k_{0}}-i}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m})^{\infty}\succ\alpha(\beta).

This contradiction shows that (
10.2
) holds for this case as well.
∎

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
k\geq 2
. If
\mathbf{v}_{k}
is a proper prefix of
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
, then
\mathbf{v}_{k}\in L^{*}(\beta)
.

Proof.

By the hypothesis,
n_{k}<n_{k-1}
and we can write
\mathbf{v}_{k}=v_{k-1,1}\dots v_{k-1,n_{k}}
. Fix
0\leq i<n_{k}
. By (
10.2
),
v_{k-1,i+1}\dots v_{k-1,n_{k}}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{k}-i}
. Write
n_{k-1}=qn_{k}+r
, where
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq r\leq n_{k}
. Since
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
is Lyndon, we have

\mathbf{v}_{k}=v_{k-1,1}\dots v_{k-1,n_{k}}\preccurlyeq v_{k-1,pn_{k}+1}\dots v_{k-1,(p+1)n_{k}},\qquad p=0,1,\dots,q-1,

and

v_{k-1,1}\dots v_{k-1,r}\prec v_{k-1,qn_{k}+1}\dots v_{k-1,n_{k-1}}.

Thus, by Lemma
10.6
it follows that

v_{k-1,i+1}\dots v_{k-1,n_{k}}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{q-1}v_{k-1,1}\dots v_{k-1,r}\prec v_{k-1,i+1}\dots v_{k-1,n_{k-1}}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots a_{n_{k-1}-i},

and therefore,
\sigma^{i}(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta)
. Since
i
was arbitrary, this shows
\mathbf{v}_{k}\in L^{*}(\beta)
.
∎

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
k\geq 2
, and suppose
\mathbf{v}_{k}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
. Then

(i)

\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
is not a prefix of
\mathbf{v}_{k}
; and

(ii)

\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

Since
\sigma^{j_{k}}(\alpha(\beta))=\sigma^{j_{k-1}+n_{k-1}}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq\sigma^{j_{k-1}}(\alpha(\beta))
and
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
is a prefix of
\sigma^{j_{k-1}}(\alpha(\beta))
, it follows that

\mathbf{v}_{k}\sigma^{j_{k}+n_{k}}(\alpha(\beta))=\sigma^{j_{k}}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{\infty},

as is easy to check. Furthermore, since
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{k}
are both Lyndon,
\mathbf{v}_{k}
cannot extend
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
. This yields (i), and also yields (ii) in case
\mathbf{v}_{k}
is not a prefix of
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
. If
\mathbf{v}_{k}
is a prefix of
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
, then
\mathbf{v}_{k}
is a proper prefix of
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
by our assumption
\mathbf{v}_{k}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
. So we can write
n_{k-1}=qn_{k}+r
, where
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq r\leq n_{k}
. By a similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
10.7
, the Lyndon property of
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
implies

\mathbf{v}_{k-1}\succ\mathbf{v}_{k}^{q}v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,r},

proving (ii).
∎

Lemma 10.9
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
can occur in
\mathbf{v}_{k}
at most once, and only at the end. In other words, the truncated word
v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,n_{k}-1}
does not contain the word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathbf{v}_{k}=v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,p}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}u_{1}\dots u_{q}
(say), where
p\geq 0
and
q\geq 1
. Then, on the one hand,
u_{1}\dots u_{q}\succ v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,q}
since
\mathbf{v}_{k}
is Lyndon. On the other hand, applying (
10.2
) with
i_{1}=p
and
i_{2}=n_{k}
we obtain
u_{1}\dots u_{q}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{j_{k}+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}+q}=v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,q}
. This contradiction completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 10.10
.

(i)

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(10.3)

\mathbf{v}_{k}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+n_{k}}\qquad\forall\,0\leq m<j_{k}.

(ii)

Suppose either
k=1
, or
k\geq 2
and
\mathbf{v}_{k}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
. If
\mathbf{v}_{k}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
, then

(10.4)

\mathbf{v}_{k}\prec\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+n_{k}}\qquad\forall\,0\leq m<j_{k}.

Proof.

We prove the two statements by induction on
k
. Take first
k=1
. We first show that

(10.5)

\mathbf{v}_{1}\preccurlyeq({\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty})_{1:n_{1}}.

(Here, for a word
\mathbf{u}=u_{1}\dots u_{n}
or sequence
\mathbf{u}=u_{1}u_{2}\dots
, we use the notation
\mathbf{u}_{i:j}:=u_{i}\dots u_{j}
.)
Recall that
j_{1}=|{\mathbf{S}}|
, and
\sigma^{j_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}
.
Furthermore,
\alpha(\beta)\succcurlyeq(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-})^{\infty}
, with equality possible in the periodic case. Thus,

{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\succ\sigma^{j_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))\succcurlyeq(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+})^{\infty}.

This shows that
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-}\preccurlyeq({\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty})_{1:n_{1}}
. If this holds with equality, then we obtain

\sigma^{n_{1}}({\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty})\succ(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-})^{\infty},

which is impossible since
{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}
does not contain the word
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}
. Hence, we have (
10.5
).

It follows that, for any
m<j_{1}
,

(10.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{v}_{1}&\preccurlyeq({\mathbf{S}}0^{\infty})_{1:n_{1}}\preccurlyeq(\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}0^{\infty})_{1:n_{1}}\\
&\preccurlyeq(\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}0^{\infty})_{1:n_{1}}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+n_{1}},\end{split}

where the second inequality holds because
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-}
, and so
\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
is a cyclic permutation of
{\mathbf{S}}
. Observe that the third inequality in (
10.6
) is strict when
n_{1}\geq j_{1}-m
.

Now suppose that
\mathbf{v}_{1}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
, and
n_{1}<j_{1}-m
. Observe that
{\mathbf{S}}\in L^{*}(\beta)
since
\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{S}}^{\infty})\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}0^{\infty}\prec\alpha(\beta)
for each
n\geq 0
. Thus, by Lemma
10.2
,
\mathbf{v}_{1}
cannot be a prefix of
{\mathbf{S}}
. But
|\mathbf{v}_{1}|=n_{1}<j_{1}=|{\mathbf{S}}|
, and hence
\mathbf{v}_{1}\prec s_{1}\dots s_{n_{1}}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+n_{1}}
as in (
10.6
). This completes the basis for the induction.

Next, let
k\geq 2
be given, and suppose that
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+n_{k-1}}
for all
m<j_{k-1}
. Take
m<j_{k}
, and consider two cases:

Case 1.

m\leq j_{k-1}
. If
n_{k}\leq n_{k-1}
, then Lemma
10.8
(ii) and the induction hypothesis imply that
\mathbf{v}_{k}\preccurlyeq v_{k-1,1}\dots v_{k-1,n_{k}}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+n_{k}}
, giving (
10.3
). Furthermore, the first inequality is strict in case
\mathbf{v}_{k}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{k}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
, in view of Lemma
10.7
, so in this case we have (
10.4
). If, on the other hand,
n_{k}>n_{k-1}
, then
v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,n_{k-1}}\prec\mathbf{v}_{k-1}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+n_{k-1}}
, again by the induction hypothesis, where the first inequality follows from Lemma
10.8
(i).

Case 2.

m>j_{k-1}
. Then
j_{k}-m<j_{k}-j_{k-1}=n_{k-1}
, and we use the Lyndon property of
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
to argue as follows: If
j_{k}-m\leq n_{k}
, then

v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,j_{k}-m}\preccurlyeq v_{k-1,1}\dots v_{k-1,j_{k}-m}\prec v_{k-1,m-j_{k-1}+1}\dots v_{k-1,n_{k-1}}=\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}},

which implies (
10.4
); and if
j_{k}-m>n_{k}
, then

\mathbf{v}_{k}\prec v_{k-1,m-j_{k-1}+1}\dots v_{k-1,m+n_{k}-j_{k-1}}=\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+n_{k}},

using that
\mathbf{v}_{k}
does not extend
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
by Lemma
10.8
(i).
∎

Lemma 10.11
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and suppose either
k=1
, or
k\geq 2
and
\mathbf{v}_{k}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
. Then
\mathbf{v}_{k}\in L^{*}(\beta)
if and only if
\mathbf{v}_{k}
does not end in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
.

Proof.

If
\mathbf{v}_{k}=\mathbf{u}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
for some word
\mathbf{u}
, then

\sigma^{|\mathbf{u}|}(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty})=(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}\mathbf{u})^{\infty}=(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}+|\mathbf{u}|})^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\alpha(\beta),

and hence
\mathbf{v}_{k}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
.

Vice versa, suppose
\mathbf{v}_{k}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
. Then there is an index
i\leq n_{k}
such that

(10.7)

(v_{k,i+1}\dots v_{k,n_{k}}v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,i})^{\infty}=v_{k,i+1}\dots v_{k,n_{k}}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\alpha(\beta).

In view of (
10.2
), this implies

(10.8)

v_{k,i+1}\dots v_{k,n_{k}}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{k}-i}.

We claim that
n_{k}-i=j_{k}
, and so
\mathbf{v}_{k}
ends in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
.
First, if
n_{k}-i>j_{k}
, then (
10.8
) gives

v_{k,i+j_{k}+1}\dots v_{k,n_{k}}=\alpha_{j_{k}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{k}-i}=v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,n_{k}-i-j_{k}},

contradicting that
\mathbf{v}_{k}
is Lyndon. On the other hand, if
n_{k}-i<j_{k}
, then
\mathbf{v}_{k}\prec\alpha_{n_{k}-i+1}\dots\alpha_{2n_{k}-i}
by Lemma
10.10
(ii), and together with (
10.8
), this contradicts (
10.7
).

Thus,
n_{k}-i=j_{k}
, and the proof is complete.
∎

We are now ready to characterize
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}
.

Lemma 10.12
.

Let
k\geq 1
, and suppose
\mathbf{v}_{k}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
. If
k\geq 2
and
\mathbf{v}_{k}=\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
, then
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}=\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{*}
. Otherwise, there exist a nonnegative integer
r
and a word
\mathbf{u}
not ending in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-}
such that

(10.9)

\mathbf{v}_{k}=\mathbf{u}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}},

and we have

(10.10)

\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{*}&\mbox{if $k\geq 2$ and $\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{-}$},\\
\mathbf{u}^{+}&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Assume that either
k=1
, or
k\geq 2
and
\mathbf{v}_{k}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
. Then by the previous lemma,
\mathbf{v}_{k}
ends in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
, and this implies the existence of the word
\mathbf{u}
and integer
r\geq 0
satisfying (
10.9
). Before determining
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}
, we first show that
\mathbf{u}
cannot end in a prefix of
\alpha(\beta)
. For, suppose
\mathbf{u}
ends in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
for some
m\geq 1
. We claim that this contradicts Lemma
10.9
. Note by (
10.9
) and (
10.2
) that

(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+(r+1)j_{k}}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{(r+1)j_{k}}.

This implies that
\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+j_{k}}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
or
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-}
. By (
10.9
) it follows that
\mathbf{v}_{k}
contains the word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+j_{k}-1}
, followed by one more digit. Since
m\geq 1
, this means that
\mathbf{v}_{k}
contains the word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
prematurely, contradicting Lemma
10.9
.

We next prove the expression (
10.10
). First, let
k=1
. We need to show that
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}=\mathbf{u}^{+}
. Note that
(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*})^{\infty}\succ\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty}
and
|\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}|<|\mathbf{v}_{1}|
by Lemma
10.2
. First we claim that
\mathbf{u}
cannot be a prefix of
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}
. If
r=0
, then
\mathbf{v}_{1}=\mathbf{u}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}
by (
10.9
), and since
|\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}|<|\mathbf{v}_{1}|
, the constraint (
10.2
) implies that
|\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}|<|\mathbf{u}|
, with strict inequality because
\mathbf{u}
ends in
0
and
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}
ends in
1
.

If
r\geq 1
, we need to rule out the possibility that
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}
might equal
\mathbf{w}:=\mathbf{u}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{p}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}
for some
p<r
. But notice that

(10.11)

\sigma^{|\mathbf{u}|+pj_{1}}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succ\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty}=(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{1}})^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\alpha(\beta),

where the second equality holds since
\mathbf{v}_{1}
ends in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}
, so
n_{1}>j_{1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1}=\alpha_{j_{1}+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}+n_{1}}=\alpha_{j_{1}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{1}}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}
.
As a consequence of (
10.11
),
\mathbf{w}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
, and so
\mathbf{u}
cannot be a prefix of
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}
. This proves the claim.

It now suffices to show that
\mathbf{u}^{+}\in L^{*}(\beta)
. Note
\mathbf{u}^{+}
is Lyndon because
\mathbf{u}
is a prefix of
\mathbf{v}_{1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1}
is Lyndon.
Note that
j_{1}=|{\mathbf{S}}|
. Write
{\mathbf{S}}=s_{1}\dots s_{j_{1}}
and
\mathbf{u}=u_{1}\dots u_{l}
. If
l\leq j_{1}
and
\mathbf{u}=s_{1}\dots s_{l}^{-}
, then
\mathbf{u}^{+}=s_{1}\dots s_{l}\in L^{*}(\beta)
by Lemma
10.2
, because
{\mathbf{S}}\in L^{*}(\beta)
. So assume
\mathbf{u}
is not of this form. We showed above that
\mathbf{u}
cannot end in a prefix of
\alpha(\beta)
, so if
\mathbf{u}^{+}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
, then
\mathbf{u}
must end in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-}
for some
m\geq 1
, i.e.
\mathbf{u}=u_{1}\dots u_{l-m}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-}
. Observe that
m\leq j_{1}
by Lemma
10.9
, and
m\neq j_{1}
because we assumed that
\mathbf{u}
does not end in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-}
. Thus,
m<j_{1}
.

We claim next that
\mathbf{u}
is not a prefix of
{\mathbf{S}}^{-}
. Suppose it is, so
\mathbf{u}=s_{1}\dots s_{l}
where
l<j_{1}
. Then

\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-}=s_{1}\dots s_{l}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{r+1}=s_{1}\dots s_{l}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{r+1},

so

\alpha(\beta)\succcurlyeq(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-})^{\infty}=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}s_{1}\dots s_{l}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{r+1}(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-})^{\infty}\succ\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty},

where the last inequality follows from the definition of
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})
. This contradicts that
\beta\in[\beta_{\ell},\beta_{*}]
.

Now there are two cases left to consider.

(a)

{\mathbf{S}}^{-}
is a proper prefix of
\mathbf{u}
. Recall that
m<j_{1}
. Since

u_{1}\dots u_{j_{1}-m}=s_{1}\dots s_{j_{1}-m}\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})_{m+1:j_{1}}\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}_{m+1:j_{1}}=\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}},

it follows that
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}u_{1}\dots u_{j_{1}-m}\prec\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}
, which yields
\sigma^{l-m}((\mathbf{u}^{+})^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta)
.

(b)

There is an index
i<\min\{l,j_{1}\}
such that
u_{1}\dots u_{i-1}=s_{1}\dots s_{i-1}
and
u_{i}<s_{i}
. Then again,
u_{1}\dots u_{i}\prec s_{1}\dots s_{i}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+i}
, with the same conclusion as in case (a).

Therefore,
\mathbf{u}^{+}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, and
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}=\mathbf{u}^{+}
.

Next, let
k\geq 2
and assume
\mathbf{v}_{k}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
. By the same argument as above,
\mathbf{u}
cannot be a prefix of
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}
, so
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}=(\mathbf{u}^{+})^{*}
. We first make a few observations:

1.

\mathbf{u}
cannot be a prefix of
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
. Suppose it is. Recall that
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{\infty}
. Since
|\mathbf{v}_{k-1}|<j_{k}
and
\mathbf{v}_{k}
begins with
\mathbf{u}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}-1}
, the constraint (
10.2
) implies that
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}=\mathbf{u}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
for some
m<j_{k}
. But then
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
is a prefix of
\mathbf{v}_{k}
, contradicting Lemma
10.8
(i).

2.

\mathbf{u}
does not extend
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
, because
\mathbf{v}_{k}
does not extend
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
.

This leaves the following cases, in which we set
l:=|\mathbf{u}|
.

(a)

If
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{-}
, it follows immediately that
(\mathbf{u}^{+})^{*}=\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{*}
.

(b)

If
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
properly extends
\mathbf{u}^{+}
and
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, then
\mathbf{u}^{+}\in L^{*}(\beta)
as well by Lemma
10.2
.

(c)

If
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
properly extends
\mathbf{u}^{+}
and
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
is
not

\beta
-Lyndon, then for any
m
such that
\mathbf{u}
ends in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-}
we must have
m<j_{k-1}
, since otherwise
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
contains the word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k-1}}
prematurely, contradicting Lemma
10.9
. Furthermore,
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}\prec\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+n_{k-1}}
by Lemma
10.10
(ii). Since
\mathbf{u}^{+}
and
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
are both Lyndon, this implies
(\mathbf{u}^{+})^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{v}_{k-1}0^{\infty}\prec\sigma^{m}(\alpha(\beta))
, and hence
\sigma^{l-m}((\mathbf{u}^{+})^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta)
. Thus,
\mathbf{u}^{+}\in L^{*}(\beta)
.

(d)

If none of (a)-(c) above hold, then there is an index
i<\min\{|\mathbf{u}|,|\mathbf{v}_{k-1}|\}
such that
u_{1}\dots u_{i}\prec v_{k-1,1}\dots v_{k-1,i}
. Then
u_{1}\dots u_{i}\prec\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{m+i}
for any
m
by Lemma
10.10
(i). So if
\mathbf{u}=u_{1}\dots u_{l-m}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-}
for some
m\geq 1
, then
\sigma^{l-m}((\mathbf{u}^{+})^{\infty})\prec\alpha(\beta)
. Thus,
\mathbf{u}^{+}\in L^{*}(\beta)
.

From these four cases and
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}=(\mathbf{u}^{+})^{*}
, (
10.10
) follows.
∎

Proposition 10.13
.

Let
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
with
k<\ell
, and suppose
I_{k}=[\underline{t}_{k},\overline{t}_{k})
and
I_{\ell}=[\underline{t}_{\ell},\overline{t}_{\ell})
belong to
\mathcal{I}
. Then:

(i)

Either
\overline{t}_{\ell}\leq\underline{t}_{k}
or
I_{\ell}\subseteq I_{k}
. In other words,
I_{\ell}
is either contained in
I_{k}
or else lies completely to the left of
I_{k}
.

(ii)

I_{\ell}\subseteq I_{k}
if and only if

(10.12)

\mathbf{v}_{\ell}=\mathbf{v}_{k}\quad\mbox{or}\quad\mathbf{v}_{\ell}\ \mbox{begins with}\ \mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{n}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}\ \mbox{for some $n\geq 0$}.

Proof.

It suffices to show that (
10.12
) implies
I_{\ell}\subseteq I_{k}
, and
\overline{t}_{\ell}>\underline{t}_{k}
implies (
10.12
).

Assume first that (
10.12
) holds. If
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}=\mathbf{v}_{k}
, then
\underline{t}_{\ell}>\underline{t}_{k}
and
\overline{t}_{\ell}=\overline{t}_{k}
, so
I_{\ell}\subseteq I_{k}
. If
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k}
, then by iterating Lemma
10.8
,
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}
and
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}
does not extend
\mathbf{v}_{k}
. Hence, we also have
(\mathbf{v}_{\ell}^{*})^{\infty}\preccurlyeq(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*})^{\infty}
since the map
\mathbf{v}\mapsto\mathbf{v}^{*}
which sends a Lyndon word
\mathbf{v}
to the smallest
\beta
-Lyndon word
\mathbf{v}^{*}
greater than or equal to
\mathbf{v}
, is clearly nondecreasing. Therefore,
\overline{t}_{\ell}\leq\overline{t}_{k}
. On the other hand,
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}
begins with
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{n}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
for some
n\geq 0
, and this implies
\underline{t}_{\ell}\geq\underline{t}_{k}
. Thus,
I_{\ell}\subseteq I_{k}
.

Next, we show by induction that for all
\ell\geq k
,

\overline{t}_{\ell}>\underline{t}_{k}
implies (
10.12
).
This is trivial for
\ell=k
, so take
\ell^{\prime}>k
and assume that
\overline{t}_{\ell}>\underline{t}_{k}
implies (
10.12
) for all
k\leq\ell<\ell^{\prime}
. Suppose
\overline{t}_{\ell^{\prime}}>\underline{t}_{k}
, so

(10.13)

(\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{*})^{\infty}\succ\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty}.

Assume
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k}
, as otherwise there is nothing to show.
We consider two cases:

Case 1.

\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}\in L^{*}(\beta)
. Then
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{*}=\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}
and hence

\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\infty}\succ\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty}.

Since
\mathbf{v}_{k}
and
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}
are both Lyndon,
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\infty}
cannot begin with
\mathbf{v}_{k}
, and hence it must begin with
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}
. Thus, in view of the constraint (
10.2
), there exists some integer
r\geq 0
such that
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\infty}
begins with

(10.14)

\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}.

Suppose, by way of contradiction, that
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is a proper prefix of this last word, so

\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{p}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}\qquad\mbox{for some $0\leq p\leq r$ and $1\leq m<j_{k}$}.

(Note
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}
cannot end in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-}
because
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is Lyndon.) Then it is easy to see that
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}
does not contain the word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
(this follows from Lemma
10.10
). However,
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\infty}
does contain
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
because it begins with (
10.14
). Therefore, it must be that
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-}
contains
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
, and this word must begin inside the block
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
. So let
i<m
be such that
\alpha_{i+1}\dots\alpha_{m}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-}
begins with
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
. Then necessarily
j_{k}-(m-i)<|\mathbf{v}_{k}|=n_{k}
in view of Lemma
10.10
. But then we have

v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,j_{k}-(m-i)}=\alpha_{m-i+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}},

so again using Lemma
10.10
it follows that

v_{k,j_{k}-(m-i)+1}\dots v_{k,n_{k}}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{j_{k}+1}\dots\alpha_{m-i+n_{k}}=v_{k,1}\dots v_{k,n_{k}+m-i-j_{k}},

contradicting that
\mathbf{v}_{k}
is Lyndon. Therefore,
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}
begins with
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
.

Case 2.

\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
. Let
\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}
be the largest integer such that
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}=\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}
.
Then
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}\neq\mathbf{v}_{\ell-1}
,
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k}
since
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k}
, and of course
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
. By Lemma
10.12
,

\mathbf{v}_{\ell}=\mathbf{u}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}}

for some word
\mathbf{u}
not ending in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}}^{-}
and some
r\geq 0
. If
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}
, then clearly
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}
extends
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
. So assume
\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}
. Then by Lemma
10.12
there are two possibilities:

(a)
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}_{\ell-1}^{-}
. Then
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{*}=\mathbf{v}_{\ell}^{*}=\mathbf{v}_{\ell-1}^{*}
, so
(\mathbf{v}_{\ell-1}^{*})^{\infty}\succ\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty}
by (
10.13
). This means
\overline{t}_{\ell-1}>\underline{t}_{k}
, so by the induction hypothesis,
\mathbf{v}_{\ell-1}
begins with
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{n}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
for some
n\geq 0
, since
\mathbf{v}_{\ell-1}=\mathbf{u}^{+}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k}
. Now either
\mathbf{v}_{\ell-1}=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{n}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
, in which case

\mathbf{v}_{\ell}=\mathbf{v}_{\ell-1}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}}=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{n+1}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}},

and so
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}
begins with
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{n+1}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
because
j_{\ell}>j_{k}
; or
\mathbf{v}_{\ell-1}
properly extends
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{n}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
, in which case
\mathbf{u}
, and hence
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}
, begins with
(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{n}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
.

(b)
\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{v}_{\ell-1}^{-}
. Then
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{*}=\mathbf{v}_{\ell}^{*}=\mathbf{u}^{+}
, and hence,

(\mathbf{u}^{+})^{\infty}\succ\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty}.

This implies that
(\mathbf{u}^{+})^{\infty}
begins with
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
for some
r\geq 0
. The same argument as in Case 1 then shows that
\mathbf{u}^{+}
must extend
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
. If
\mathbf{u}^{+}
properly extends this word, then so does
\mathbf{u}
and hence so does
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}
.

Otherwise,
\mathbf{u}^{+}=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
, and so

\mathbf{v}_{\ell}=\mathbf{u}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}}=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r+1}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}},

so that
\mathbf{v}_{\ell}
extends
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r+1}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
, because
j_{\ell}>j_{k}
.

In both case (a) and case (b) we have shown that
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}=\mathbf{v}_{\ell}
extends
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{n}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
for some
n\geq 0
. This completes the proof.
∎

11.
Proof of Theorem
10.3

We are now ready to prove Theorem
10.3
. The theorem involves three separate statements, which we prove in Propositions
11.3
,
11.5
and
11.6
, respectively. Our notation and assumptions are the same as in the previous section.

Lemma 11.1
.

For each
k\geq 1
,
(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*})^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
.

Proof.

First, take
k=1
. If
\mathbf{v}_{1}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, then
(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*})^{\infty}=\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{-}1^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
, using (
10.5
) and the fact that
{\mathbf{S}}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1}
are both Lyndon. If
\mathbf{v}_{1}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
, then
\mathbf{v}_{1}=\mathbf{u}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}
for some word
\mathbf{u}
and integer
r\geq 0
by Lemma
10.12
, and
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}=\mathbf{u}^{+}
. This implies that
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}\neq{\mathbf{S}}
, for else we would have

\mathbf{v}_{1}=\mathbf{u}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}={\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{r}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+},

contradicting (
10.5
). But
(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*})^{\infty}\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
, because
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
,
{\mathbf{S}}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, and
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}
is the smallest
\beta
-Lyndon word greater than or equal to
\mathbf{v}_{1}
. Therefore,
(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*})^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
.

For
k\geq 2
, the statement now follows inductively by Lemma
10.8
.
∎

Definition 11.2
.

We call an interval
I_{k}\in\mathcal{I}
a
non-transitivity window
if it is a maximal element of
\mathcal{I}
with respect to set inclusion; that is, if
I_{k}
is not contained in any other interval
I_{\ell}\in\mathcal{I}
. We denote by
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
the collection of all non-transitivity windows.

By Proposition
10.13
, the collection
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is pairwise disjoint. The name “non-transitivity window” is made clear by the next proposition.

Proposition 11.3
.

Let
I_{k}\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
be a non-transitivity window. Then for any
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]
with
t_{R}\in I_{k}
, the subshift
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is not transitive.

Proof.

Suppose
t_{R}\in I_{k}
. We first assume
I_{k}
is open on the right, so

(11.1)

\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty}=b(\underline{t}_{k},\beta)\preccurlyeq b(t_{R},\beta)\prec b(\overline{t}_{k},\beta)=(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*})^{\infty}.

Observe that, by definition of
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}
, there are no
\beta
-Lyndon words
\mathbf{w}
such that
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*})^{\infty}
, and so we actually have
b(t_{R},\beta)\prec\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}
. (Strict inequality, because if
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}\prec(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*})^{\infty}
, then
\mathbf{v}_{k}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
.) But then, since
\mathbf{v}_{k}\in L^{*}
,
b(t_{R},\beta)
must begin with
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}
.

We claim that
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))
. To see this, consider two cases:

(i)

If
t_{R}>\underline{t}_{k}
, then
b(t_{R},\beta)
begins with
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
for some
r\geq 0
, and since
b(t_{R},\beta)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
, this implies that
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))
.

(ii)

If
t_{R}=\underline{t}_{k}
, then
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty}
, and in this case the sequence

\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty}

lies in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
, so again
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))
.

From the construction of
\mathbf{v}_{k}
, (
11.1
) and the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
, it follows that any sequence in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
beginning with
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
must be of the form

(11.2)

\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{p_{1}}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{q_{1}}\,\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{p_{2}}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{q_{2}}\dots,

where
0\leq p_{i},q_{i}\leq\infty
for all
i
. If
k>1
, then no such sequence ends in
{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
, because
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
by Lemma
11.1
and
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-}
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}
. Since
{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
is always a valid sequence in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
, we see that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is not transitive in this case.

Suppose now that
k=1
. Then we have to be a bit more careful since
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-}=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})
. If
t_{R}>\underline{t}_{1}
, then we cannot have
q_{i}=\infty
for any
i
in (
11.2
), so again the legal sequences of the form (
11.2
) cannot end in
{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is not transitive. Suppose
t_{R}=\underline{t}_{1}
, so
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{\infty}
. Since
b(t_{R},\beta)
is purely periodic, this implies that either
\mathbf{v}_{1}=\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}
for some
m<j_{1}
, or else

\mathbf{v}_{1}=\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}

for some
m<j_{1}
and
r\geq 0
. However, it can be seen from the inequalities in (
10.6
) that neither of these is possible. Thus, we must have
t_{R}>\underline{t}_{1}
, and we are done.

Finally, we consider the case when
I_{k}
is closed. Recall that this means that
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},\dots,I_{k}\}
is finite,
\mathbf{v}_{k}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, and
\sigma^{j_{k}}(\alpha(\beta))=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}
. If
t_{R}<\overline{t}_{k}
, the proof goes as before, so assume
t_{R}=\overline{t}_{k}
. Then
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}
. Now
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is not transitive because
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
is a legal word that can only be followed by the sequence
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}
.
∎

Remark 11.4
.

In the last case in the above proof, when
I_{k}
is closed and
t=\overline{t}_{k}
, we showed that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
is not transitive. However, in this special case the set

\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t)=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:b(t,\beta)\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\prec\alpha(\beta)\ \forall\,n\geq 0\}

is in fact itself a transitive subshift, as is easy to see. So the non-transitivity of
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
in this case is somewhat artificial, caused by enlarging the set
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t)
more than what is needed to obtain a subshift.

Proposition 11.5
.

Assume
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{s}}\in\Lambda_{1}=F^{*}
. Let
t_{R}\in(0,\tau(\beta))
be a right endpoint of a
\beta
-Lyndon interval, and suppose
t_{R}
does not lie in any non-transitivity window. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive.

(The statement of this proposition fails when
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda_{k}
with
k\geq 2
, as we will see in Section
12
.)

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{w}
be the
\beta
-Lyndon word such that
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
.
We enumerate
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
as
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\{I_{1}=I_{k_{1}},I_{k_{2}},\dots\}
, where
k_{1}<k_{2}<\dots
, or as
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\{I_{1}=I_{k_{1}},I_{k_{2}},\dots,I_{k_{N}}\}
, where
k_{1}<k_{2}<\dots<k_{N}
, depending on whether
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is infinite or finite. Recall that the intervals
I_{k_{1}},I_{k_{2}},\dots
proceed from right to left. There are three cases to consider:

•

t_{R}\geq\overline{t}_{1}
;

•

\overline{t}_{k_{i+1}}\leq t_{R}<\underline{t}_{k_{i}}
for some
i
;

•

t_{R}<\underline{t}_{k}
for all
k
.

The argument differs somewhat for each of the three cases.

Case 1.

t_{R}\geq\overline{t}_{1}
. Then
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succcurlyeq(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*})^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\alpha_{j_{1}+1}\alpha_{j_{1}+2}\dots
.
In fact, we can show that

(11.3)

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succ\alpha_{j_{1}+1}\alpha_{j_{1}+2}\dots,

by considering the following cases:

•

If
t_{R}>\overline{t}_{1}
, then
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succ(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*})^{\infty}
;

•

If
t_{R}=\overline{t}_{1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1}
is
not

\beta
-Lyndon, then
(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*})^{\infty}\succ\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\alpha_{j_{1}+1}\alpha_{j_{1}+2}\dots
by the definition of
\mathbf{v}_{1}
;

•

If
t_{R}=\overline{t}_{1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1}

is

\beta
-Lyndon, then, since we assumed that
t_{R}\not\in I_{1}
, the interval
I_{1}
was taken to be half open and therefore
\sigma^{j_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))\neq\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty}
, which implies
(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*})^{\infty}=\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty}\succ\alpha_{j_{1}+1}\alpha_{j_{1}+2}\dots
.

By (
11.3
), we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})=\{\mathbf{z}:\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{\infty}\ \forall\,n\geq 0\}.

Thus, the word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}
cannot occur in any sequence
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
; in particular, it cannot occur in
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
.

Let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))
and
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
be given. By Lemma
6.3
, we can extend
\mathbf{z}
to the left to a sequence
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
beginning with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
. Thus, without loss of generality, we will assume that
\mathbf{z}
itself begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
.

If
\mathbf{u}
does not end in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
for any
m
, set
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{u}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
. Otherwise, let
m
be the largest integer such that
\mathbf{u}
ends in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
, say
\mathbf{u}=u_{1}\dots u_{l}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
. Then
m<j_{1}
, and we set
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}:=u_{1}\dots u_{l}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-}=u_{1}\dots u_{l}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
.

Observe that
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}
, so there exists
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{N_{1}}0^{\infty}
. Furthermore,
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}
for all
n\geq 0
, and in fact the inequality is strict because
\mathbf{w}
and
{\mathbf{s}}
are both Lyndon, and
\mathbf{w}\neq{\mathbf{s}}
. Thus, since
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
is periodic, there exists
N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{N_{2}}0^{\infty}\qquad\forall\,n\geq 0.

Now let
N:=\max\{N_{1},N_{2}\}
, and set
\mathbf{v}:=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{N}
. Then
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\mathbf{v}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. Therefore,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive.

Case 2.

\overline{t}_{k_{i+1}}\leq t_{R}<\underline{t}_{k_{i}}
for some
i
. For ease of notation, put
k:=k_{i}
and
\ell:=k_{i+1}
, so we have

\overline{t}_{\ell}\leq t_{R}<\underline{t}_{\nu}\qquad\forall\,1\leq\nu<\ell,

since
\underline{t}_{\nu}\geq\underline{t}_{k}
for
k\leq\nu<\ell
. By the same reasoning as in Case 1, we have that

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succ\alpha_{j_{\ell}+1}\alpha_{j_{\ell}+2}\dots,

and so

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})=\{\mathbf{z}:\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}}^{-})^{\infty}\ \forall\,n\geq 0\}.

Thus, the word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}}
never occurs in any sequence in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. On the other hand, for each
1\leq\nu<\ell
,

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}=b(t_{R},\beta)\prec b(\underline{t}_{\nu},\beta)=\mathbf{v}_{\nu}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\nu}}^{-})^{\infty},

so there is an integer
r_{\nu}\geq 0
such that

(11.4)

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{v}_{\nu}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\nu}}^{-})^{r_{\nu}}0^{\infty}.

Let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))
and
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
be given. As in Case 1, we may assume that
\mathbf{z}
begins with the word
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
. If no suffix of
\mathbf{u}
is a prefix of
\alpha(\beta)
, set
m=0
. Otherwise, let
m
be the largest integer such that
\mathbf{u}
ends in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
. In either case
m<j_{\ell}
, and we define

\mathbf{u}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{u}\alpha_{m+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell}}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\ell-1}}^{-})^{r_{\ell-1}}\dots(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{r_{1}}.

It is not difficult to check using (
11.4
) that
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. (The idea is that
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\nu}}^{-}
ends in
\mathbf{v}_{\nu-1}^{-}
. So we append a high enough power of
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{\nu-1}}^{-}
to it to ensure that
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\mathbf{z})\succcurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
for all
n
, and repeat until we reach
\nu=1
.) Hence,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive.

Case 3.

t_{R}<\underline{t}_{k}
for all
k
. This case is the most involved.

(a) Assume first that
\mathcal{I}
is infinite. Then as in Case 2 there exist integers
r_{1},r_{2},\dots
such that

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r_{k}}0^{\infty},\qquad k=1,2,\dots.

Let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))
and
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
be given.
If
\mathbf{u}
does not end in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
for any
m
, set
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{u}
. Otherwise, let
m
be the largest integer such that
\mathbf{u}
ends in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
. Here we do not have an upper bound for
m
, however there is an index
k
such that
j_{k-1}\leq m<j_{k}
, since
j_{k}\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
. We can thus extend
\mathbf{u}
to a word
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))
ending in the block

\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k-1}}^{-})^{r_{k-1}}\dots(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{r_{1}}.

Similarly, as before, we may assume
\mathbf{z}
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
. Then
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
just in Case 2.

(b) Assume next that
\mathcal{I}
is finite:
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{k_{0}}\}
, where
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
. This case is more difficult because
\mathbf{u}
can end in an arbitrarily long prefix of
\alpha(\beta)
, but there are only finitely many
j_{k}
’s, so the construction of case (a) above needs to be modified. Set
n_{0}:=|\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\mathbf{v}_{2}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}|=j_{k_{0}}+|\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}|
.

(i) Suppose first that
\alpha(\beta)
is not periodic. Note that
\sigma^{n_{0}}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{\infty}
, so
\sigma^{n_{0}}(\alpha(\beta))
begins with
0
. Since
\sigma^{n_{0}}(\alpha(\beta))\neq 0^{\infty}
, there is an index
n_{1}>n_{0}
such that
\alpha_{n_{0}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{1}}
is Lyndon. Then
\sigma^{n_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))\succ(\alpha_{n_{0}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{1}})^{\infty}
, or else the word
\alpha_{n_{0}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{1}}
would have become
\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}+1}
and generated an additional interval
I_{k_{0}+1}
in
\mathcal{I}
. Hence, there is an index
n_{2}>n_{1}
such that
\alpha_{n_{0}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{2}}
is also Lyndon. Repeating this argument yields an infinite, strictly increasing sequence
(n_{i})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\alpha_{n_{0}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{i}}
is Lyndon for each
i
, and

\sigma^{n_{i}}(\alpha(\beta))\succ(\alpha_{n_{0}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{i}})^{\infty}\qquad\forall\,i\in\mathbb{N}.

Note also that the tail
\sigma^{n_{0}}(\alpha(\beta))
cannot be periodic. If now
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succ\sigma^{n_{0}}(\alpha(\beta))
, then

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})=\{\mathbf{z}:\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}^{-})^{\infty}\ \forall\,n\geq 0\},

and the proof proceeds essentially as in Case 2. Otherwise, since
\sigma^{n_{0}}(\alpha(\beta))
is not periodic, there is a sufficiently large index
i_{0}
such that

(11.5)

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec\alpha_{n_{0}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{i_{0}}}^{-}0^{\infty}.

Claim:
The tail
\sigma^{n_{0}}(\alpha(\beta))
does not contain the word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}
.

To see this, write
(c_{i}):=\sigma^{n_{0}}(\alpha(\beta))
and suppose by way of contradiction that
c_{m+1}\dots c_{m+n_{0}}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}
, where we can choose
m
to be minimal, i.e.
c_{l+1}\dots c_{l+n_{0}}\neq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}
for all
0\leq l<m
. We first show that
c_{1}\dots c_{m+n_{0}}
is Lyndon. This means verifying that

(11.6)

c_{l+1}\dots c_{m+n_{0}}\succ c_{1}\dots c_{m+n_{0}-l}\qquad\forall\,1\leq l<m+n_{0}.

Recall that

c_{m+1}\dots c_{m+n_{0}}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}.

By the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
10.8
,
c_{l+1}\dots c_{m+n_{0}}
does not extend
\mathbf{v}_{k}
for any
k\leq k_{0}
. Since in addition each
\mathbf{v}_{k}
is Lyndon, this implies (
11.6
) for
m+j_{1}\leq l<m+n_{0}
.

Furthermore, for
m\leq l<m+j_{1}
we have

\displaystyle c_{l+1}\dots c_{m+j_{1}}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{l-m+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}\succ s_{1}\dots s_{j_{1}-(l-m)}

\displaystyle\succcurlyeq(\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{\infty})_{1:j_{1}-(l-m)}\succcurlyeq c_{1}\dots c_{j_{1}-(l-m)},

where the first inequality follows since
\alpha_{l-m+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}
is a suffix of
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}
, the second inequality follows by Lemma
11.1
, and the last inequality follows from the definition of
\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}
.

(Note: If the collection
\mathcal{I}
is empty, then
n_{0}=j_{1}
and we obtain directly the inequality
s_{1}\dots s_{j_{1}-(l-m)}\succcurlyeq c_{1}\dots c_{j_{1}-(l-m)}
because
\sigma^{j_{1}}(\alpha(\beta))\prec{\mathbf{s}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{\infty}
.)

Finally, for
1\leq l<m
we have
c_{l+1}\dots c_{m}\succcurlyeq c_{1}\dots c_{m-l}
because
c_{1}\dots c_{m-l}
is a prefix of the Lyndon word
\alpha_{n_{0}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{i}}
for a sufficiently large
i
; and
c_{m+1}\dots c_{m+n_{0}}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}\succ c_{m-l+1}\dots c_{m-l+n_{0}}
by the minimality of
m
. This gives (
11.6
) for
1\leq l<m
as well.

Having verified (
11.6
), we conclude that
c_{1}\dots c_{m+n_{0}}
is Lyndon. But then
\sigma^{2n_{0}+m}(\alpha(\beta))\succ(c_{1}\dots c_{n_{0}+m})^{\infty}
, which implies
\sigma^{n_{0}+m}(\alpha(\beta))\succ\alpha(\beta)
, a contradiction. Therefore, our assumption that
c_{m+1}\dots c_{m+n_{0}}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}
was invalid, and the claim is proven.

From the Claim, it follows that

(11.7)

\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}^{-})^{\infty}\qquad\forall\,n\geq n_{0}.

Now let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))
and
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
be given. As in Case 2, we can extend
\mathbf{z}
to a sequence
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
beginning with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
. We extend
\mathbf{u}
first to a sequence
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
ending in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{i_{0}}}^{-}
, where
i_{0}
was chosen to satisfy (
11.5
), in the usual way by pasting
\mathbf{u}
and
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{i_{0}}}^{-}
together along their longest overlap. If
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
does not end in a prefix of
\mathbf{w}
, then
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
and we are done.

Otherwise, let
m
be the largest integer such that
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
ends in
w_{1}\dots w_{m}
, where we write
(w_{i}):=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
. Then by (
11.5
) and the Lyndon property of
\alpha_{n_{0}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{i_{0}}}
,

w_{1}\dots w_{m}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{n_{0}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}+m}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{n_{i_{0}}-m+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{i_{0}}}^{-}=w_{1}\dots w_{m},

hence
w_{1}\dots w_{m}=\alpha_{n_{0}+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}+m}
. By (
11.5
) and (
11.7
), it follows that

w_{m+1}w_{m+2}\dots\prec\alpha_{n_{0}+m+1}\dots\alpha_{n_{i_{0}}}^{-}0^{\infty}\prec(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}^{-})^{\infty},

so there is some integer
r_{*}\geq 1
such that
w_{m+1}w_{m+2}\dots\prec(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}^{-})^{r_{*}}0^{\infty}
.
Recall again that
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}^{-}=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{-}
. As in Case 2 there exist integers
r_{1},\dots,r_{k_{0}}
such that

(11.8)

\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r_{k}}0^{\infty},\qquad k=1,\dots,k_{0}.

We now extend
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
further by setting

\mathbf{u}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}^{-})^{r_{*}}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k_{0}}}^{-})^{r_{k_{0}}}\dots(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{r_{1}}.

Then
\mathbf{u}^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. Hence,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive.

(ii) Suppose next that
\alpha(\beta)
is periodic. Then

\alpha(\beta)=(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}})^{\infty}=(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{-})^{\infty}.

Let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))
. If
\mathbf{u}
does not end in a prefix of
\alpha(\beta)
, we set
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{u}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}}
. Otherwise, there is some
r\geq 0
and
0\leq m<n_{0}
such that
\mathbf{u}
ends in
(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
. In that case we extend
\mathbf{u}
slightly further to a word
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
ending in
(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n_{0}})^{r+1}
. Observe that
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}
then ends in
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{-}
. From here we proceed as above: We choose exponents
r_{1},\dots,r_{k_{0}}
satisfying (
11.8
), and set

\mathbf{u}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k_{0}}}^{-})^{r_{k_{0}}}\dots(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{r_{1}}.

This word
\mathbf{u}^{\prime\prime}
can then be connected directly to any sequence
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
beginning with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{s}})
.
∎

Proposition 11.6
.

Let
I_{k}=[\underline{t}_{k},\overline{t}_{k})\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
be a non-transitivity window. Then
h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
is constant on
I_{k}
.

Proof.

This will follow from Lemmas
14.4
and
14.5
in Section
14
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
10.3
.

The theorem follows from Propositions
11.3
,
11.5
and
11.6
.
∎

The work we did in this section and the last, also has an important consequence for the bifurcation sets
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
and
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
:

Theorem 11.7
.

Let
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}})
, where
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
. If
I_{k}
is a non-transitivity window, then
\dim_{H}(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap I_{k})>0
. In particular, if the collection
\mathcal{I}
is nonempty, then
\dim_{H}(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta})>0
.

Proof.

Let
I_{k}=[\underline{t}_{k},\overline{t}_{k})
be a non-transitivity window, so

b(\underline{t}_{k},\beta)=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty},\qquad b(\overline{t}_{k},\beta)=(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*})^{\infty}.

Writing
B_{k}:=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
for brevity, consider points
t
with greedy expansion

b(t,\beta)=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(B_{k}^{-})^{r_{1}}B_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(B_{k}^{-})^{r_{2}}B_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(B_{k}^{-})^{r_{3}}B_{k}\dots.

Note that this expression is indeed a greedy
\beta
-expansion since

B_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}\prec B_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}\alpha_{j_{k}+1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}+n_{k}}.

Furthermore, observe that
\sigma^{n}(b(t,\beta))\succcurlyeq b(t,\beta)
for all
n\geq 0
if and only if
(r_{1},r_{2},\dots)\succcurlyeq(r_{n},r_{n+1},\dots)
for all
n\geq 1
. Taking
r_{1}=3
and
r_{i}\in\{1,2\}
arbitrarily for
i\geq 2
we see that there are uncountably many points of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
in
I_{k}
. In fact, the set

C:=\{\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(B_{k}^{-})^{3}B_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(B_{k}^{-})^{r_{2}}B_{k}\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(B_{k}^{-})^{r_{3}}B_{k}\dots:r_{i}\in\{1,2\}\ \forall\,i\geq 2\}

has positive entropy, hence positive Hausdorff dimension in
\Sigma_{\beta}
. Therefore, by (
4.1
),

\dim_{H}(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap I_{k})\geq\dim_{H}\pi_{\beta}(C)>0.

The second statement follows from the first and Proposition
11.6
, which implies that there are no points of
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
in
I_{k}
.
∎

12.
Higher order basic intervals

In this section we generalize Theorem
10.3
to the higher order basic interval
[\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{r}^{{\mathbf{S}}}]
, where
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
. We do this by combining the construction from Section
10
with the renormalization method of Section
7
.

Recall that
\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)
is the set of all right endpoints of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
. The following theorem established the final case of Theorem
4.4
.

Theorem 12.1
.

Let
[\beta_{\ell},\beta_{*}]
be a basic interval generated by a word
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{n}\in\Lambda
, where
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}\in F^{*}
for each
i
, and let
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell},\beta_{*})
. Then there is a (finite or infinite, possibly empty) collection
\mathcal{I}
of intervals such that

(i)

For any
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive if and only if
b(t_{R},\beta)\prec{\mathbf{r}}_{1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{1})^{\infty}
and
t_{R}\not\in\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I
;

(ii)

For any
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)\backslash\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
has a transitive subshift
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
of full entropy and full Hausdorff dimension that contains the sequence
b(t_{R},\beta)
;

(iii)

The collection

\{\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}):t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)\backslash\textstyle{\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I}\}

is a strictly descending collection of subshifts;

(iv)

The entropy function
t\mapsto h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
is constant throughout each interval
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

We begin by proving an extension of Lemma
6.3
.

Lemma 12.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{n}
, where
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}\in F^{*}
for each
i
, and let
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}}]
. Let
[t_{L},t_{R}]
be a
\beta
-Lyndon interval such that
b(t_{R},\beta)\prec{\mathbf{r}}_{1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{1})^{\infty}
. If
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
, then
\mathbf{z}
can be extended to the left to a sequence
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
beginning with
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}
, and also to a sequence
\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
beginning with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{1})
.

The proof goes exactly like that of Lemma
6.3
, replacing
{\mathbf{s}}
with
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}
and observing that
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}
, which in turn begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{1})^{+}
, followed by either
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}
or
{\mathbf{r}}_{1}^{-}
.

Now let
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{n}\in\Lambda
with
n\geq 2
, and consider
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}})
.
We follow the same construction as in Section
10
. Thus,
j_{1}=|{\mathbf{S}}|
, the first special word
\mathbf{v}_{1}
begins with
\alpha_{j_{1}+1}
, etc. The procedure from Section
10
generates a (finite or infinite) collection
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots\}
of intervals.

Recall that the transitivity result of Proposition
11.5
required that
{\mathbf{S}}\in F^{*}
. For general
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, we have instead the following fact:

Proposition 12.3
.

With
\beta
as above, let
[t_{L},t_{R}]
be a
\beta
-Lyndon interval. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive if and only if
b(t_{R},\beta)\prec{\mathbf{r}}_{1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{1})^{\infty}
and
t_{R}\not\in\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I
.

Proof.

Write
{\mathbf{r}}:={\mathbf{r}}_{1}
for brevity. If
t_{R}\in\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is not transitive by Proposition
11.3
. We now show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is not transitive when
b(t_{R},\beta)\succcurlyeq{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
. This follows essentially the same way as in the proof of Proposition
7.6
. Let
\mathbf{w}
be the
\beta
-Lyndon word such that
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
. Observe that for any sequence
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
, we have

\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(0^{\infty})={\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}{\mathbf{r}}^{\infty}=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(1^{\infty}).

Thus, if
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
begins with
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
or
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}
, then
\mathbf{z}=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
for some sequence
\hat{\mathbf{z}}
by Lemma
7.2
. Furthermore, since
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}
and
{\mathbf{S}}^{-}
begins with the word
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
,
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
also begins with
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
. The rest of the proof follows that of Proposition
7.6
.

Next, we show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive when
b(t_{R},\beta)\prec{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
and
t_{R}\not\in\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I
. This proceeds almost exactly as in the proof of Proposition
11.5
, with one essential difference: Since
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
, we can find an integer
M
such that
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{M}0^{\infty}
. In each of the three cases in the proof of Proposition
11.5
, we can assume that
\mathbf{z}
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})
by Lemma
12.2
. We now extend
\mathbf{z}
further by preceding it with the factor
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{M}
, calling the new sequence again
\mathbf{z}
. The rest of the proof is now the same as before (changing
{\mathbf{s}}
to
{\mathbf{S}}
throughout).
∎

Recall that
X({\mathbf{s}})=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}\big(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\big)
and
X^{*}({\mathbf{s}})=\Phi_{\mathbf{s}}\big(\{0,1\}^{*}\big)
for any Lyndon word
{\mathbf{s}}
.

Lemma 12.4
.

Suppose
(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*})^{\infty}\succ{\mathbf{r}}_{1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{1})^{\infty}
. Then
\mathbf{v}_{k}\in X^{*}({\mathbf{r}}_{1})
, and
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty}\succcurlyeq{\mathbf{r}}_{1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{1})^{\infty}
.

Proof.

We prove this by induction on
k
. Take first
k=1
, and assume
(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*})^{\infty}\succ{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
, where we write
{\mathbf{r}}:={\mathbf{r}}_{1}
. If
\mathbf{v}_{1}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, then
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty}\succ{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
, so
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty}
begins with
{\mathbf{r}}^{-}
, and then
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{\infty}\in X({\mathbf{r}})
by Lemma
7.2
. But then
\mathbf{v}_{1}\in X^{*}({\mathbf{r}})
because
\mathbf{v}_{1}
is Lyndon. Moreover, since
\mathbf{v}_{1}
ends in 1, the last block of
\mathbf{v}_{1}
is
{\mathbf{r}}
or
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{+}
, and so
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-}\in X^{*}({\mathbf{r}})
as well.

If
\mathbf{v}_{1}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
, then
\mathbf{v}_{1}=\mathbf{u}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}
for some word
\mathbf{u}
and integer
r\geq 0
, and
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}=\mathbf{u}^{+}
by Lemma
10.12
. Here
\mathbf{u}^{+}\in L^{*}(\beta)
and
(\mathbf{u}^{+})^{\infty}\succ{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
, so
\mathbf{u}\in X^{*}({\mathbf{r}})
by the same argument as above. Observing that

\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-}=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})=\Phi_{\mathbf{r}}(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{2}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{n}))\in X^{*}({\mathbf{r}}),

it follows that
\mathbf{v}_{1}\in X^{*}({\mathbf{r}})
as well, and then also
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{\infty}\in X^{*}({\mathbf{r}})
. This implies
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{1}}^{-})^{\infty}\succcurlyeq{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
.

Next, let
k\geq 2
and assume the lemma holds for the words
\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
. Suppose
(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*})^{\infty}\succ{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
. The same argument we used for
\mathbf{v}_{1}
shows that
\mathbf{v}_{k}\in X^{*}({\mathbf{r}})
. Now since
\mathbf{v}_{l}^{\infty}\succ\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}\succ{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
for all
l<k
, the induction hypothesis implies that
\mathbf{v}_{l}\in X^{*}({\mathbf{r}})
for all
l<k
, and thus,
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k-1}\in X^{*}({\mathbf{r}})
. It follows that
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty}\in X^{*}({\mathbf{r}})
, and hence,
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty}\succcurlyeq{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
12.1
.

By Theorem
10.3
, the theorem holds for the case
n=1
, i.e.
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{r}}\in F^{*}
; we simply set
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}):=\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
for
t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)\backslash\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I
.

Now let
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{m}
, where
m\geq 2
and
{\mathbf{r}}_{i}\in F^{*}
for each
i
. Let
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}})
, and construct the Lyndon words
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\dots
and collection of intervals
\mathcal{I}=\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots\}
as outlined above. Then statement (i) follows from Proposition
12.3
.

We define a new base
\beta^{\prime}\in[\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}})
as follows. Set
{\mathbf{r}}:={\mathbf{r}}_{1}
. If
\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\succcurlyeq{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
for all
n\geq j_{1}
(and hence for all
n\geq 0
), we set
\beta^{\prime}:=\beta
and
n_{0}:=\infty
. Otherwise, let

n_{0}:=\min\{n\geq j_{1}:\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\prec{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}\}.

Since the words
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\dots
are Lyndon, there is an integer
k_{0}\geq 0
