such that
n_{0}=j_{k_{0}+1}
. We let
\beta^{\prime}
be the base given by

\alpha(\beta^{\prime})=(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k_{0}+1}}^{-})^{\infty}=\big(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{-}\big)^{\infty},

where we interpret the last expression as
\alpha(\beta^{\prime})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}
if
k_{0}=0
. Let
t_{*}
be the point given by
b(t_{*},\beta)={\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
. By Lemma
12.4
, any interval
I_{k}\in\mathcal{I}
lies either completely in
(0,t_{*})
or in
[t_{*},\tau(\beta)]
. By definition of
n_{0}
, the intervals
I_{1},\dots,I_{k_{0}}
lie to the right of the critical point
t_{*}
, and
I_{k_{0}+1},I_{k_{0}+2},\dots
lie to the left of
t_{*}
. (If
n_{0}=\infty
, then each
I_{k}
lies to the right of
t_{*}
; in this case, we set
k_{0}:=\infty
for convenience.)

Due to the special way in which we constructed the intervals
I_{1},I_{2},\dots
for the case of periodic
\alpha(\beta)
in Section
10
, the non-transitivity windows for
\beta^{\prime}
are exactly the intervals
I_{1}^{\prime}=[\underline{t}_{1}^{\prime},\overline{t}_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,I_{k_{0}}^{\prime}=[\underline{t}_{k_{0}}^{\prime},\overline{t}_{k_{0}}^{\prime})
given by

b(\underline{t}_{k}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{\infty},\qquad b(\overline{t}_{k}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*})^{\infty},\qquad k=1,2,\dots,k_{0}.

Let
\mathbf{w}
be a
\beta
-Lyndon word such that
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succ{\mathbf{r}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}})^{\infty}
and
\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\not\in\bigcup_{k=1}^{k_{0}}I_{k}
, and let
t_{R}
be the point such that
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
.
We first claim that
\mathbf{w}
is also
\beta^{\prime}
-Lyndon. This is obvious if
\beta^{\prime}=\beta
, so assume
\beta^{\prime}\neq\beta
so that
n_{0}<\infty
. Suppose
\sigma^{l}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\succcurlyeq\alpha(\beta^{\prime})=\big(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{-}\big)^{\infty}
for some
l
. Since
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon and the words
\mathbf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}
decrease lexicograpically, this can only happen if
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}-1}=\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{-}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k_{0}}}
. But then

b(\underline{t}_{k_{0}},\beta)=\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k_{0}}}^{-})^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec(\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{*})^{\infty}=b(\overline{t}_{k_{0}},\beta),

so
t_{R}=\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})\in I_{k_{0}}
, a contradiction. Hence,
\mathbf{w}
is
\beta^{\prime}
-Lyndon, and there is a point
t_{R}^{\prime}
such that
b(t_{R}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
.

Furthermore,
\sigma^{n_{0}}(\alpha(\beta))\prec\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
if
n_{0}
is finite, and then

\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})=\{\mathbf{z}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k_{0}}}^{-})^{\infty}\ \forall\,n\geq 0\}.

It follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})=\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(t_{R}^{\prime})
.

Now define
{\mathbf{S}}_{i}:={\mathbf{r}}_{1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{i}
for
i=1,2,\dots,m
, so in particular,
{\mathbf{S}}_{m}={\mathbf{S}}
. Since
\tau(\beta)={\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}={\mathbf{S}}_{m}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{m})^{\infty}
, there is a unique
i\in\{1,2,\dots,m-1\}
such that

{\mathbf{S}}_{i}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{i})^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}_{i+1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{i+1})^{\infty}.

Set
\mathbf{R}:={\mathbf{S}}_{i}
and
\mathbf{R}^{\prime}:={\mathbf{r}}_{i+1}\bullet\dots\bullet{\mathbf{r}}_{m}
. It follows (by Lemma
7.2
if
n_{0}=\infty
; by Lemma
12.4
if
n_{0}<\infty
) that
\alpha(\beta^{\prime})\in X(\mathbf{R})
, and hence
\alpha(\beta^{\prime})=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
for some base
\hat{\beta}
. Note that
{\mathbf{S}}=\mathbf{R}\bullet\mathbf{R}^{\prime}
. Since
\beta^{\prime}\in[\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}})
, we have
\hat{\beta}\in[\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}})
. We make the following observations.

•

If
n_{0}=\infty
and
\mathcal{I}
is infinite, then
\alpha(\beta^{\prime})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\mathbf{v}_{2}\mathbf{v}_{3}\dots
and
\alpha(\hat{\beta})=\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})^{+}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{2}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{3}\dots
, where for each
k
,
\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{k}
is a Lyndon word such that
\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{k})=\mathbf{v}_{k}
.

•

If
n_{0}=\infty
and
\mathcal{I}
is finite, then for some
k\geq 0
,
\alpha(\beta^{\prime})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k}\mathbf{y}
and
\alpha(\hat{\beta})=\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})^{+}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1}\dots\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{k}\hat{\mathbf{y}}
, where
\mathbf{y}
and
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
are sequences satisfying
\sigma^{j}(\mathbf{y})\succ\mathbf{y}
for all
j\geq 0
,
\mathbf{y}=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})
, and hence also
\sigma^{j}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})\succ\hat{\mathbf{y}}
for all
j\geq 0
.

•

If
n_{0}<\infty
, then
\alpha(\beta^{\prime})=\big(\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}\mathbf{v}_{1}\dots\mathbf{v}_{k_{0}}^{-}\big)^{\infty}
and
\alpha(\hat{\beta})=\big(\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})^{+}\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1}\dots\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{k_{0}}^{-}\big)^{\infty}
, where again,
\mathbf{v}_{k}=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{k})
for
k=1,2,\dots,k_{0}
. (If
k_{0}=0
, we have
\alpha(\beta^{\prime})=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}
and
\alpha(\hat{\beta})=\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})^{\infty}
.)

The words
\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{1},\hat{\mathbf{v}}_{2},\dots
determine a (finite or infinite) collection
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}=\{\hat{I}_{1},\hat{I}_{2},\dots\}
of intervals in the same way that
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\dots
determine
I_{1},I_{2},\dots
. In fact, if
\hat{I}_{k}=[\underline{\hat{t}}_{k},\overline{\hat{t}}_{k})
, then

(12.1)

b(\underline{t}_{k},\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}\big(b(\underline{\hat{t}}_{k},\hat{\beta})\big),\qquad b(\overline{t}_{k},\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}\big(b(\overline{\hat{t}}_{k},\hat{\beta})\big).

Since
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\succ\mathbf{R}^{-}\mathbb{L}(\mathbf{R})^{\infty}
, we have
\mathbf{w}=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}})
for some
\hat{\mathbf{w}}\in L^{*}(\hat{\beta})
, as in the proof of Proposition
7.8
, and so there is a point
\hat{t}_{R}
such that
b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta})=\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty}
. Therefore, since
t_{R}^{\prime}\not\in\bigcup_{k=1}^{k_{0}}I_{k}^{\prime}
and
b(t_{R}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(\hat{\mathbf{w}}^{\infty})=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}(b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta}))
, it follows using (
12.1
) that
\hat{t}_{R}\not\in\bigcup_{k=1}^{k_{0}}\hat{I}_{k}
. Furthermore, since
b(t_{R}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}_{i+1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{i+1})^{\infty}
, it follows that

b(\hat{t}_{R},\hat{\beta})=\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}\big(b(t_{R}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\big)\prec\Phi_{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}\big({\mathbf{S}}_{i+1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}}_{i+1})^{\infty}\big)={\mathbf{r}}_{i+1}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{r}}_{i})^{\infty}.

Hence, by Proposition
12.3
applied to
\hat{\beta}
in place of
\beta
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R})
is transitive. We now define a subshift
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
by

\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}):=\{\sigma^{n}(\Phi_{{\mathbf{r}}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})):\hat{\mathbf{z}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\hat{\beta}}(\hat{t}_{R}),\ n\geq 0\}.

As in the proof of Proposition
7.8
,
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
is transitive and of full entropy and full Hausdorff dimension in
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(t_{R}^{\prime})=\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
, and contains the sequence
b(t_{R},\beta)=b(t_{R}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
. Moreover, the collection

\{\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R}):t_{R}\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(\beta)\backslash\textstyle{\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I}\}

is a strictly descending collection of subshifts.

Finally, Proposition
11.6
implies that
h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
is constant on each interval in
\mathcal{I}
.
∎

13.
Gaps between
\beta
-Lyndon intervals

We saw in Subection
5.1
that when
\beta\in\overline{E}
, the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
. However, Example
5.10
showed that this need not be the case when
\beta
lies in the interior of a basic interval. In this section we show that having gaps between
\beta
-Lyndon intervals is in fact typical.

Below we fix a basic interval
[\beta_{\ell},\beta_{*}]=[\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}}]
generated by a Lyndon word
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
.

Definition 13.1
.

We say an open interval
(t_{1},t_{2})
is a
\beta
-Lyndon gap
if

(i)

(t_{1},t_{2})
does not intersect any
\beta
-Lyndon intervals; and

(ii)

any open interval
(u_{1},u_{2})
properly containing
(t_{1},t_{2})
intersects at least one
\beta
-Lyndon interval.

Note that this definition is indifferent about whether the endpoints
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
belong to
\beta
-Lyndon intervals.

We first show that
\beta
-Lyndon gaps always exist when
\beta
lies in a basic interval.
Most importantly, we show that the entropy
h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
stays constant across each
\beta
-Lyndon gap.

Proposition 13.2
.

Let
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}})
, where
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
. Then there are infinitely many
\beta
-Lyndon gaps, and the left endpoint of each of these lies in
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
.

Proof.

Write
{\mathbf{S}}=s_{1}\dots s_{m}
and
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})=c_{1}\dots c_{m}
, and let
j
be the integer such that
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})=s_{j+1}\dots s_{m}s_{1}\dots s_{j}
. Set
\mathbf{u}:=s_{j+1}\dots s_{m}
. Since
\beta<\beta_{*}
, we have
\sigma^{m}(\alpha(\beta))\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}
, so there is an integer
N
such that
\sigma^{m}(\alpha(\beta))\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{N}0^{\infty}
. Choose
M\geq N
, and let
t_{M}
be the point given by

b(t_{M},\beta)={\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{M}\mathbf{u}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}.

This is a valid greedy
\beta
-expansion, because it does not contain the word
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
, where
(\alpha_{i}):=\alpha(\beta)
. Also, since
\mathbf{u}^{-}\prec\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})
, it is clear that
b(t_{M},\beta)\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}=b(\tau(\beta),\beta)
, so
t_{M}<\tau(\beta)
. Furthermore,
b(t_{M},\beta)\succ\sigma^{m}(\alpha(\beta))
by the choice of
M
and
N
. Finally, for
j\leq i<m
, the properties of Lyndon words yield

s_{i+1}\dots s_{m}^{-}c_{1}\dots c_{i}\succcurlyeq s_{1}\dots s_{m-i}c_{1}\dots c_{i}\succ s_{1}\dots s_{m-i}s_{m-i+1}\dots s_{m}^{-}={\mathbf{s}}^{-},

so that

\sigma^{(M+1)m+i-j}(b(t_{M},\beta))=s_{i+1}\dots s_{m}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\succ b(t_{M},\beta),

and also,

\sigma^{(M+1)m-j}(b(t_{M},\beta))=s_{1}\dots s_{j}\mathbf{u}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}={\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\succ b(t_{M},\beta).

It follows that
\sigma^{n}(b(t_{M},\beta))\succcurlyeq b(t_{M},\beta)
for all
n\geq 0
; in other words,
t_{M}\in\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
. Hence, by Lemma
4.5
(and its proof),
t_{M}
is the limit of an increasing sequence of endpoints of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals.

On the other hand, if
k>(M+1)|{\mathbf{S}}|+|\mathbf{u}|
, it is easy to see that
b_{1}\dots b_{k}^{+}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
(where we write
b(t_{M},\beta)=b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}\dots
): If it were, then
b_{1}\dots b_{k}^{+}
would have to end in
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}
, but
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}{\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{M}\mathbf{u}^{-}0^{\infty}\succ\alpha(\beta)
by the choice of
M
. Thus,
t_{M}
is not a decreasing limit of endpoints of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals. Finally,
t_{M}
is not itself a right endpoint of a
\beta
-Lyndon interval because
b(t_{M},\beta)
is aperiodic. Hence,
t_{M}
is a left endpoint of a
\beta
-Lyndon gap.

Since different choices of
M
give rise to different sequences
b(t_{M},\beta)
, there are infinitely many
\beta
-Lyndon gaps.
∎

Proposition 13.3
.

Let
(t_{1},t_{2})
be a
\beta
-Lyndon gap. Then
h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{1}))=h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{2}))
.

Proof.

By Lemmas
4.5
and
3.1
,
(t_{1},t_{2}]\cap\mathscr{E}_{\beta}=\emptyset
. Thus, if
t\in(t_{1},t_{2})
then
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t)=\mathcal{K}_{\beta}(t_{2})
, and so
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{2})
is at most countable. Hence,
h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))=h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{2}))
. Letting
t\searrow t_{1}
and using the continuity of
t\mapsto h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
, the proposition follows.
∎

Corollary 13.4
.

If
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}})
for some
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, then
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
is infinite.

Proof.

This follows directly from Propositions
13.2
and
13.3
, since the latter implies that the left endpoint of a
\beta
-Lyndon gap does not lie in
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
.
∎

Corollary 13.5
.

Let
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{{\mathbf{S}}},\beta_{*}^{{\mathbf{S}}})
for some
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, and suppose
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic. If the collection
\mathcal{I}
of non-transitivity windows is empty, then
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
is countably infinite.

Proof.

By the assumption and Proposition
11.5
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is transitive for every
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]
. Furthermore, since
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic, the subshift
\Sigma_{\beta}
is sofic and hence
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is sofic as well. Thus, if
[t_{L},t_{R}]
and
[t_{L}^{\prime},t_{R}^{\prime}]
are two
\beta
-Lyndon intervals with
t_{R}<t_{R}^{\prime}
, then
h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))>h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}^{\prime}))
by
[
27
, Corollary 4.4.9]
. So the only intervals on which
\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
is constant are the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals, which contain no points of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
except the right endpoints, and
\beta
-Lyndon gaps, which contain no points of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
except possibly the left endpoints. In other words, each interval on which
\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
is constant contains at most one point from
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
. Hence,
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
is countable, and by Corollary
13.4
, it is infinite.
∎

Example
10.4
(c) presents a
\beta
satisfying the hypotheses of Corollary
13.5
.

14.
Properties of extended
\beta
-Lyndon intervals

Recall the definition of an extended
\beta
-Lyndon interval (EBLI) from Section
1
.

Lemma 14.1
.

Let
[t_{L}^{*},t_{R}]
be an EBLI that is not a
\beta
-Lyndon interval. Then
t_{L}^{*}\in\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
.

Proof.

We have to check that

(14.1)

\sigma^{n}(b(t_{L}^{*},\beta))\succcurlyeq b(t_{L}^{*},\beta)\qquad\forall\,n\geq 0.

Let
\mathbf{w}
be the
\beta
-Lyndon word generating the EBLI, so
b(t_{L}^{*},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}
and
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
, where
m:=\min\{n:\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\}
.
Write
\mathbf{w}=w_{1}\dots w_{l}
. We first establish that

(14.2)

w_{i+1}\dots w_{l}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}\preccurlyeq(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}\qquad\forall\,0\leq i<l.

If
l-i\leq m
, then
w_{i+1}\dots w_{l}^{-}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{l-i}^{-}
since
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, which yields (
14.2
) for this case.

If
l-i>m
, then we claim that
w_{i+1}\dots w_{i+m}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-}
. Suppose to the contrary that
w_{i+1}\dots w_{i+m}\succcurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
. We have
\sigma^{m}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{i+m}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})
since
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon, and hence it follows that

\alpha(\beta)=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}\sigma^{m}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq w_{i+1}\dots w_{i+m}\sigma^{i+m}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})=\sigma^{i}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty}),

contradicting that
\mathbf{w}\in L^{*}(\beta)
. Hence,
w_{i+1}\dots w_{i+m}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-}
. We can then cancel the first
m
digits on both sides of (
14.2
) and continue inductively until we are left with a word of length
\leq m
in front of
(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}
in the left hand side, at which point we know the inequality to be true from the first case above.

For
1\leq j<l
we have
w_{j+1}\dots w_{l}^{-}\succcurlyeq w_{1}\dots w_{l-j}
(since
\mathbf{w}
is Lyndon), and so (
14.2
) with
i=l-j
gives

\sigma^{j}(b(t_{L}^{*},\beta))=w_{j+1}\dots w_{l}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}=b(t_{L}^{*},\beta).

It remains to check that

(14.3)

\alpha_{j+1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}\succcurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}\qquad\forall\,0\leq j<m.

This is obvious for
j=0
, so assume
1\leq j<m
. If
l\leq m-j
, then
\alpha_{j+1}\dots\alpha_{j+l}\succcurlyeq\mathbf{w}
since
\sigma^{j}(\alpha(\beta))\succ\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
, hence (
14.3
) holds for this case.

So suppose
l>m-j
. Then
\sigma^{j}(\alpha(\beta))\succ\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
implies
w_{1}\dots w_{m-j}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{j+1}\dots\alpha_{m}
. If we had equality here then by the Lyndon property of
\mathbf{w}
we would get

\sigma^{j}(\alpha(\beta))=w_{1}\dots w_{m-j}\sigma^{m}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq w_{1}\dots w_{m-j}\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty},

contradicting the minimality of
m
. Thus,
w_{1}\dots w_{m-j}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{j+1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-}
. Together with (
14.2
) (taking
i=m-j
), this gives (
14.3
).
∎

Lemma 14.2
.

Any two EBLIs are either non-overlapping or else one contains the other.

Proof.

Any two
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are disjoint, and a
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]
and an EBLI
[u_{L}^{*},u_{R}]
cannot overlap because the left endpoint
u_{L}^{*}
lies in
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
by Lemma
14.1
, and therefore does not lie in
[t_{L},t_{R}]
.

It remains to consider the case of two EBLIs
I_{\mathbf{v}}=[\mathbf{v}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{v})}^{-})^{\infty},\mathbf{v}^{\infty}]
and
I_{\mathbf{w}}=[\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{w})}^{-})^{\infty},\mathbf{w}^{\infty}]
; suppose they overlap without one containing the other. Without loss of generality,

(14.4)

\mathbf{v}^{-}\big(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{v})}^{-}\big)^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{w}^{-}\big(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{w})}^{-}\big)^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{v}0^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{v}^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{w}^{\infty},

where we have the second inequality because
\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{w})}^{-})^{\infty}
does not lie in the
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[\mathbf{v}0^{\infty},\mathbf{v}^{\infty}]
, as observed at the beginning of the proof.
Since
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
are Lyndon,
\mathbf{v}^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
implies
\mathbf{v}^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}
. So
\mathbf{v}^{\infty}
begins with
\mathbf{w}^{-}
, and hence
m(\mathbf{v})\geq m(\mathbf{w})
. In fact
\mathbf{v}
extends
\mathbf{w}^{-}
: It cannot be a prefix of
\mathbf{w}^{-}
because
\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{w})}^{-})^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{v}0^{\infty}
. Say
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{w}^{-}u_{1}\dots u_{l}
. Then from (
14.4
),

(14.5)

u_{1}\dots u_{l}^{-}\big(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{v})}^{-}\big)^{\infty}\prec\big(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{w})}^{-}\big)^{\infty}\prec u_{1}\dots u_{l}0^{\infty}.

Hence the sequence
(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{w})}^{-})^{\infty}
begins with
u_{1}\dots u_{l}^{-}
, and the first inequality in (
14.5
) implies

\big(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{v})}^{-}\big)^{\infty}\prec\sigma^{l}\big(\big(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{w})}^{-}\big)^{\infty}\big).

But this is impossible, because
m(\mathbf{v})\geq m(\mathbf{w})
and
(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{w})}^{-})^{\infty}=\alpha(\beta^{\prime})
for some base
\beta^{\prime}
, hence by Lemma
2.1
,

\sigma^{l}\big(\big(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{w})}^{-}\big)^{\infty}\big)\preccurlyeq\big(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{w})}^{-}\big)^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\big(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m(\mathbf{v})}^{-}\big)^{\infty}.

This contradiction completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 14.3
.

The union of all EBLIs is dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
.

Proof.

We know from Proposition
5.9
and Theorems
7.1
and
9.1
that the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
when
\beta\in\overline{E}\cup\bigcup_{{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda}\overline{E^{\mathbf{S}}}\cup E_{\infty}
. So it remains to show that the EBLIs are dense for
\beta
in the interior of a basic interval, i.e.
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}})
with
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
.

Let
t\in[0,\tau(\beta))
with greedy
\beta
-expansion
b(t,\beta)=b_{1}b_{2}\dots
. Let
\mathcal{N}
denote the set of all indices
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that the word
b_{1}\dots b_{n}
is Lyndon. Since
b_{1}=0
, the set
\mathcal{N}
is not empty. There are now three possibilities:

(1)

\mathcal{N}
is finite and
b_{1}\dots b_{n}\in L^{*}(\beta)
for each
n\in\mathcal{N}
;

(2)

\mathcal{N}
is infinite and
b_{1}\dots b_{n}\in L^{*}(\beta)
for each
n\in\mathcal{N}
; or

(3)

b_{1}\dots b_{n}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
for some
n\in\mathcal{N}
.

In the first case, letting
n_{\max}:=\max\mathcal{N}
, we have
\sigma^{n_{\max}}(b(t,\beta))\preccurlyeq b(t,\beta)
and so
t
lies in the
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[b_{1}\dots b_{n_{\max}},(b_{1}\dots b_{n_{\max}})^{\infty}]
.

In the second case,
t
does not lie in any
\beta
-Lyndon interval but is the limit of left endoints of
\beta
-Lyndon intervals
[b_{1}\dots b_{n},(b_{1}\dots b_{n})^{\infty}],n\in\mathcal{N}
.

So it remains to deal with the third case. We show that in this case
t
lies in some
EBLI
. Let
N
be the smallest
n\in\mathcal{N}
for which
b_{1},\dots b_{n}
is
not

\beta
-Lyndon, and put
\mathbf{w}:=b_{1}\dots b_{N}
. Note that
\mathbf{w}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
; we first aim to determine
\mathbf{w}^{*}
. Observe that, since
t<\tau(\beta)=\pi_{\beta}({\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty})
, there is at least one
\beta
-Lyndon word, namely
{\mathbf{S}}
, that is greater than
\mathbf{w}
; hence
\mathbf{w}^{*}
is well defined, and we can write
\mathbf{w}^{*}=b_{1}\dots b_{l}^{+}
for some
l<N
.

Since
\mathbf{w}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
, there is an index
k<N
such that
b_{k+1}\dots b_{N}b_{1}\dots b_{k}\succcurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{N}
. Let
k_{1}
be the smallest such
k
. In particular
b_{k_{1}+1}\dots b_{N}\succcurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{N-k_{1}}
. But, since
b(t,\beta)
is a greedy
\beta
-expansion, we also have the reverse inequality. Hence,

(14.6)

b_{k_{1}+1}\dots b_{N}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{N-k_{1}},\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}\succcurlyeq\alpha_{N-k_{1}+1}\dots\alpha_{N}.

Clearly, then,
l\leq k_{1}
as otherwise
b_{1}\dots b_{l}^{+}
would include the forbidden word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{l-k_{1}}^{+}
.

Suppose
b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}^{+}\in L^{*}(\beta)
. Then
\mathbf{w}^{*}=b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}^{+}
, and
m:=\min\{n\geq 0:\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq(\mathbf{w}^{*})^{\infty}\}\leq N-k_{1}
, because
\alpha_{N-k_{1}+1}\dots\alpha_{N}\preccurlyeq b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}\prec\mathbf{w}^{*}
. So in this case, we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathbf{w}^{*})^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}

\displaystyle=b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}\preccurlyeq b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{N-k_{1}}^{-})^{\infty}

\displaystyle=b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}(b_{k_{1}+1}\dots b_{N}^{-})^{\infty}\prec b(t,\beta),

and hence,
t
lies in the EBLI generated by
\mathbf{w}^{*}
.

If
b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}^{+}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, we repeat the process with
k_{1}
in place of
N
. Precisely, there is a smallest integer
k_{2}<k_{1}
such that
b_{k_{2}+1}\dots b_{k_{1}}^{+}b_{1}\dots b_{k_{2}}\succcurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k_{1}}
. We claim that in fact

(14.7)

b_{k_{2}+1}\dots b_{k_{1}}^{+}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k_{1}-k_{2}},

and hence
b_{1}\dots b_{k_{2}}\succcurlyeq\alpha_{k_{1}-k_{2}+1}\dots\alpha_{k_{1}}
. To see the claim, suppose by way of contradiction that
b_{k_{2}+1}\dots b_{k_{1}}\succcurlyeq\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k_{1}-k_{2}}
. Then this must hold with equality since
b_{k_{2}+1}\dots b_{k_{1}}
is part of
b(t,\beta)
. By the Lyndon property of
b_{1}\dots b_{N}
, it then follows that
b_{1}\dots b_{N}
ends in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k_{1}-k_{2}}
. Thus,
\alpha_{k_{1}-k_{2}}=b_{N}=1
, whereas
b_{k_{1}}=0
by the minimality of
k_{1}
. This contradiction proves the claim.

Suppose we had
k_{1}-k_{2}>N-k_{1}
. Then (
14.7
) implies
b_{k_{2}+1}\dots b_{k_{2}+N-k_{1}}=\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{N-k_{1}}
, and so by (
14.6
),

b_{k_{2}+N-k_{1}+1}\dots b_{N}\preccurlyeq\alpha_{N-k_{1}+1}\dots\alpha_{N-k_{2}}\preccurlyeq b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}-k_{2}},

contradicting that
b_{1}\dots b_{N}
is Lyndon. Thus,
k_{1}-k_{2}\leq N-k_{1}
.

Suppose now that
b_{1}\dots b_{k_{2}}^{+}\in L^{*}(\beta)
. Since
\alpha_{k_{1}-k_{2}+1}\dots\alpha_{k_{1}}\preccurlyeq b_{1}\dots b_{k_{2}}
, we have

\sigma^{k_{1}-k_{2}}(\alpha(\beta))=\alpha_{k_{1}-k_{2}+1}\alpha_{k_{1}-k_{2}+2}\dots\prec(b_{1}\dots b_{k_{2}}^{+})^{\infty},

so
m=\min\{n\geq 0:\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq(\mathbf{w}^{*})^{\infty}\}\leq k_{1}-k_{2}
. Hence,

\displaystyle(\mathbf{w}^{*})^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq b_{1}\dots b_{k_{2}}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k_{1}-k_{2}}^{-})^{\infty}=b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{k_{1}-k_{2}}^{-})^{\infty}

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{N-k_{1}}^{-})^{\infty}\prec b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{N-k_{1}}0^{\infty}

\displaystyle=b_{1}\dots b_{N}0^{\infty}\preccurlyeq b(t,\beta),

where the first equality uses (
14.7
), and the second inequality uses that
k_{1}-k_{2}\leq N-k_{1}
. So,
t
lies in the EBLI generated by
\mathbf{w}^{*}
.

If
b_{1}\dots b_{k_{2}}^{+}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
, we repeat the above process with
k_{2}
replacing
k_{1}
and the Lyndon word
b_{1}\dots b_{k_{1}}^{+}
replacing the Lyndon word
b_{1}\dots b_{N}
. Continuing this way, we obtain a sequence
N>k_{1}>k_{2}>\dots
. The process must stop after a finite number of steps, say
p
, and then
\mathbf{w}^{*}=b_{1}\dots b_{k_{p}}^{+}
and
t
lies in the EBLI generated by
\mathbf{w}^{*}
.
∎

Lemma 14.4
.

Assume
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}})
for some
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
. Then the entropy function
t\mapsto h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
is constant in each EBLI that lies fully in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
.

Proof.

The entropy is obviously constant on each
\beta
-Lyndon interval, so consider an EBLI
I_{\mathbf{w}}=[\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty},\mathbf{w}^{\infty}]
inside
[0,\tau(\beta)]
. Since
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
for
\beta\in\overline{E}\cup\bigcup_{{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda}\overline{E^{\mathbf{S}}}\cup E_{\infty}
and EBLIs are non-overlapping by Lemma
14.2
, it must be the case that
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}})
for some
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
. Moreover,
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\preccurlyeq b(\tau(\beta),\beta)={\mathbf{S}}^{-}\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
. So
\mathbf{w}
and
{\mathbf{S}}
are two distinct
\beta
-Lyndon words, and
m=\min\{n:\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\leq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\}\geq|{\mathbf{S}}|
, because
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}
.

Let
t_{L}^{*}
and
t_{R}
be the left and right endpoints of the projection
\pi_{\beta}(I_{\mathbf{w}})
. It suffices to show that

h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{L}^{*}))=h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})).

Suppose
\mathbf{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{L}^{*})\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. Then

(14.8)

b(t_{L}^{*},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)\qquad\forall\,n\geq 0,

and there is some integer
n_{0}
such that
\sigma^{n_{0}}(\mathbf{z})\prec b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
. We claim that
\mathbf{z}
must end in a sequence of the form

(14.9)

\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{p_{1}}\,\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}\mathbf{w}^{q_{1}}\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{p_{2}}\,\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}\mathbf{w}^{q_{2}}\dots,

where
0\leq p_{i},q_{i}\leq\infty
for all
i
. Since
\sigma^{n_{0}}(\mathbf{z})\prec\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
, there is some
n_{1}\geq n_{0}
such that
\sigma^{n_{1}}(\mathbf{z})
begins with
\mathbf{w}^{-}
. By (
14.8
), it then follows that
\sigma^{n_{1}}(\mathbf{z})
begins with
\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{p_{1}}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
for some
p_{1}\leq\infty
. But now recall that
\sigma^{m}(\alpha(\beta))\preccurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
. Thus, if
p_{1}<\infty
, then again using (
14.8
), the word
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
must be followed by
\mathbf{w}^{q_{1}}\mathbf{w}^{-}
for some
q_{1}\leq\infty
. Repeating this argument, we see that
\mathbf{z}
ends in a sequence of the form (
14.9
).

Now let
\mathcal{X}
denote the set of all sequences of the form (
14.9
), together with their shifts. Then
\mathcal{X}
is a subshift of
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{L}^{*})
. Also, let
\mathcal{Y}
denote the set of all infinite concatenations of the words
{\mathbf{S}}
and
\mathbf{w}
and their shifts, that is,

\mathcal{Y}:=\left\{\sigma^{n}\big({\mathbf{S}}^{p_{1}}\mathbf{w}^{q_{1}}{\mathbf{S}}^{p_{2}}\mathbf{w}^{q_{2}}\dots\big):\ n\geq 0,\ 0\leq p_{i},q_{i}\leq\infty\ \forall\,i\right\}.

If
\mathbf{z}\in\mathcal{Y}
, then
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\succcurlyeq\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
for all
n\geq 0
since
\mathbf{w}
and
{\mathbf{S}}
are both Lyndon and
\mathbf{w}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
. Furthermore,
\sigma^{n}(\mathbf{z})\preccurlyeq\alpha(\beta)
for all
n\geq 0
since
{\mathbf{S}}
and
\mathbf{w}
are both
\beta
-Lyndon. Hence,
\mathcal{Y}
is a subshift of
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
. Since neither
\mathbf{w}
nor
{\mathbf{S}}
is a power of the other (else one of them wouldn’t be Lyndon) and
|{\mathbf{S}}|\leq m
, a comparison of the definitions of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
shows that
h(\mathcal{Y})\geq h(\mathcal{X})
. As a result,

h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{L}^{*}))\leq\max\{h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})),h(\mathcal{X})\}\leq\max\{h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})),h(\mathcal{Y})\}=h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})).

The reverse inequality is obvious. Therefore, the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 14.5
.

The closure of every non-transitivity window (that is, every interval in the collection
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
) is an EBLI.

Proof.

Let
I_{k}=[\underline{t}_{k},\overline{t}_{k})\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
be a non-transitivity window for
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}})
with
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
. Set
m:=j_{k}
. If
\mathbf{v}_{k}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, then
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}=\mathbf{v}_{k}
, and so we have
b(\underline{t}_{k},\beta)=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}
and
b(\overline{t}_{k},\beta)=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}
. By Lemma
11.1
,
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}\prec{\mathbf{S}}^{\infty}
, and since
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}
, this implies that
\sigma^{n}(\alpha(\beta))\succ\mathbf{v}_{k}^{\infty}
for all
n<m
. Therefore,
\overline{I_{k}}
is an EBLI.

Suppose now that
\mathbf{v}_{k}\not\in L^{*}(\beta)
. Since
I_{k}
is a non-transitivity window,
I_{k}\not\subseteq I_{k-1}
and so
\mathbf{v}_{k}\neq\mathbf{v}_{k-1}
. Thus, by Lemma
10.12
, there exist a nonnegative integer
r
and a word
\mathbf{u}
not ending in
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-}
such that
\mathbf{v}_{k}=\mathbf{u}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}^{-})^{r}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k}}
, and

\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{*}&\mbox{if $k\geq 2$ and $\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{-}$},\\
\mathbf{u}^{+}&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

If
k\geq 2
and
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{-}
, then, since
j_{k}>j_{k-1}
, it follows that
\mathbf{v}_{k}
begins with
\mathbf{v}_{k-1}^{-}\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{j_{k-1}}
and so
I_{k}\subseteq I_{k-1}
by Proposition
10.13
. This contradicts our assumption that
I_{k}
is a non-transitivity window. Hence, this case cannot happen and
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}=\mathbf{u}^{+}
. Now observe that

\mathbf{v}_{k}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}=\mathbf{u}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{r+1}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}=(\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*})^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}.

Hence,
\overline{I_{k}}
is the EBLI generated by
\mathbf{v}_{k}^{*}
.
∎

Lemma 14.6
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{R}^{\prime}
denote the set of all right endpoints of maximal EBLIs in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
. Then for each
t\in\mathcal{T}_{R}^{\prime}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t)
has a transitive subshift
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t)
of full entropy containing the sequence
b(t,\beta)
. Moreover, these subshifts can be chosen so that
\{\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t):t\in\mathcal{T}_{R}^{\prime}\}
is a strictly descending collection of subshifts, and they are sofic if
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic.

Proof.

A maximal EBLI either contains a non-transitivity window or else does not intersect any non-transitivity windows, in view of Lemmas
14.2
and
14.5
. Thus, the result follows from Proposition
11.5
and Theorems
7.1
,
9.1
and
12.1
.
∎

Corollary 14.7
.

Assume
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic. Then the entropy function
t\mapsto h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
takes distinct values on distinct maximal EBLIs in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
.

Proof.

Immediate from Lemma
14.6
and
[
27
, Corollary 4.4.9]
.
∎

Corollary 14.8
.

Assume
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic. Then an EBLI is maximal if and only if it is not properly contained in the closure of a non-transitivity window.

Proof.

The forward direction is immediate from Lemma
14.5
. For the converse, assume an EBLI
I_{\mathbf{w}}=[t_{L}^{*},t_{R}]
is not properly contained in the closure of a non-transitivity window. Suppose there is another EBLI, say
I_{\mathbf{v}}=[u_{L}^{*},u_{R}]
that properly contains
I_{\mathbf{w}}
. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(u_{R})
) has a transitive sofic subshift
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
(resp.
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(u_{R})
) of full entropy that contains the sequence
b(t_{R},\beta)
(resp.
b(u_{R},\beta)
), because neither
t_{R}
nor
u_{R}
lies in a non-transitivity window. Since
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(u_{R})
is a proper subshift of
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
, this implies
h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R}))>h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(u_{R}))
, contradicting that the entropy is constant on
[u_{L}^{*},u_{R}]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Recall the decomposition (
2.12
).
Assume
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic. Then
\beta\not\in E
by Proposition
2.9
. Likewise,
\beta\not\in E^{\mathbf{S}}
for any
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
because if
\beta\in E^{\mathbf{S}}
, then
\alpha(\beta)=\Phi_{\mathbf{S}}(\alpha(\hat{\beta}))
for some
\hat{\beta}\in E
, and
\alpha(\hat{\beta})
is not eventually periodic, so
\alpha(\beta)
isn’t either. Furthermore,
\beta\not\in E_{\infty}
(see the remark following Proposition
9.2
). Thus, by (
2.12
),
\beta\in I^{\mathbf{S}}=[\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}]
for some
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
.

If
\beta\in\{\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}\}
, then the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals are dense in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
by Proposition
5.9
and Theorem
7.1
, so the EBLIs in
[0,\tau(\beta)]
are just the
\beta
-Lyndon intervals and the conclusion follows from Theorem
8.1
.

Finally, if
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}})
, the theorem follows from Proposition
14.3
, Lemma
14.4
and Corollary
14.7
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.5
.

We first show that
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
is well defined. First, suppose
\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{b}})\succ{\mathbf{b}}
for some
n
, and take
n
minimal with this property. Then
b_{n}=0
, and setting
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}:=(b_{1}\dots b_{n})^{\infty}
we see that
0^{\infty}\prec\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
for all
n\geq 0
, and it is easy to see that no sequence between
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{b}}
satisfies these inequalities. Furthermore,
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}=\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}
for all
\mathbf{a}
. Second, suppose
\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{b}})\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{b}}
for all
n\geq 0
, but
{\mathbf{b}}=b_{1}\dots b_{n}0^{\infty}
with
b_{n}=1
. Setting
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}:=(b_{1}\dots b_{n}^{-})^{\infty}
we see that
0^{\infty}\prec\sigma^{n}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})\preccurlyeq{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
for all
n\geq 0
by the assumption
{\mathbf{b}}\succ 10^{\infty}
. Again, no sequence between
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbf{b}}
satisfies these inequalities. Moreover,
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}\backslash\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}}
is at most countable for each
\mathbf{a}
.
Thus, in both cases,
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
is well defined,
h(\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}})=h(\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}})
for all
\mathbf{a}
, and
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}=\alpha(\beta)
for some
\beta\in(1,2]
by Lemma
2.1
.

Now assume
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
is eventually periodic. For simplicity we relabel
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
as
{\mathbf{b}}
in the rest of the proof.
If
[\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}),\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})]
is a plateau of
t\mapsto h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
, then
[\mathbf{w}^{-}{\mathbf{b}},\mathbf{w}^{\infty}]
is a plateau of
\mathbf{a}\mapsto h(\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}})
since
\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}}=\Sigma_{\mathbf{w}0^{\infty},{\mathbf{b}}}
for all
\mathbf{a}\in(\mathbf{w}^{-}{\mathbf{b}},\mathbf{w}0^{\infty}]
. Similarly, if
[\pi_{\beta}\big(\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty}\big),\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{w}^{\infty})]
is a plateau of
t\mapsto h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
, then
[\mathbf{w}^{-}(\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}^{-})^{\infty},\mathbf{w}^{\infty}]
is a plateau of
\mathbf{a}\mapsto h(\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}})
.

It remains to show that no interval of the form
[\mathbf{u}^{-}{\mathbf{b}},\mathbf{u}0^{\infty}]
, with
\mathbf{u}
an allowed word in
\Sigma_{\beta}
ending in
1
, is a plateau of
\mathbf{a}\mapsto h(\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}})
. (Since
\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{u}^{-}{\mathbf{b}})=\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{u}0^{\infty})
, the interval
[\mathbf{u}^{-}{\mathbf{b}},\mathbf{u}0^{\infty}]
collapses to a point when projecting under
\pi_{\beta}
, so it is at least conceivable that
[\mathbf{u}^{-}{\mathbf{b}},\mathbf{u}0^{\infty}]
is a plateau.) This is equivalent to showing that the point
t_{0}:=\pi_{\beta}(\mathbf{u}0^{\infty})
already lies in a plateau of
t\mapsto h(\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t))
, i.e. in an EBLI.

If
\mathbf{u}
is not Lyndon, then
t_{0}
lies inside some
\beta
-Lyndon interval, hence in an EBLI. If
\mathbf{u}\in L^{*}(\beta)
, then
t_{0}
is the left endpoint of a
\beta
-Lyndon interval, hence is contained in an EBLI. Finally, suppose
\mathbf{u}
is Lyndon but not
\beta
-Lyndon. Then the proof of Proposition
14.3
shows that
t_{0}
lies in an EBLI. Hence, in all cases, the interval
[\mathbf{u}^{-}{\mathbf{b}},\mathbf{u}0^{\infty}]
already lies in one of the plateaus
\tilde{I}_{\mathbf{w}}
.
∎

Remark 14.9
.

The
0
-plateau of
\mathbf{a}\mapsto h(\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}})
is
[b(\tau(\beta),\beta),1^{\infty}]
as long as
b(\tau(\beta),\beta)
does not end in
0^{\infty}
. The only exceptional case is when
\beta=\beta_{r}^{\mathbf{S}}
for
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, in which case
b(\tau(\beta),\beta)={\mathbf{S}}0^{\infty}
and the
0
-plateau of
\mathbf{a}\mapsto h(\Sigma_{\mathbf{a},{\mathbf{b}}})
is
[{\mathbf{S}}^{-}{\mathbf{b}},1^{\infty}]
. (An equivalent result was proved also by Labarca and Moreira
[
25
]
.)

15.
Proofs of the other main theorems

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

Recall the decomposition (
2.12
).
For
{\mathbf{S}}\in F^{*}
, the left endpoint
\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}}
of
I^{{\mathbf{S}}}
lies in
E_{L}
; for
{\mathbf{S}}={\mathbf{s}}\bullet{\mathbf{r}}\in\Lambda
with
{\mathbf{s}}\in F^{*}
and
{\mathbf{r}}\in\Lambda
,
\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}}
lies in
\overline{E^{\mathbf{s}}}
; and for all
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, the right endpoint
\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}
of
I^{{\mathbf{S}}}
lies in
\overline{E^{\mathbf{S}}}
.
Thus, the theorem follows from Theorems
5.1
,
6.1
,
7.1
,
9.1
and
12.1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

We first show that
\dim_{H}(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta})>0
for almost all
\beta\in(1,2]
. Fix for the moment a basic interval
I^{\mathbf{S}}=[\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}}]
where
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
, and take
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}})
. Then
\alpha(\beta)
begins with
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}
. Let
k
be the integer such that
\sigma^{|{\mathbf{S}}|}(\alpha(\beta))
begins with
0^{k}1
. If there is a string of more than
k
consecutive zeros in
\alpha(\beta)
beyond
\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}0^{k}1
, then it follows from the construction in Section
10
that the collection
\mathcal{I}
of non-transitivity windows is non-empty. It is well known (see
[
31
, p. 678]
) that for Lebesgue-almost all
\beta\in(1,2]
the sequence
\alpha(\beta)
contains arbitrarily long strings of consecutive zeros. Hence for almost all
\beta\in I^{\mathbf{S}}
there is at least one non-transitivity window, and for such
\beta
,
\dim_{H}(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta})>0
by Theorem
11.7
. Since the basic intervals
I^{\mathbf{S}}
,
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
cover the interval
(1,2]
up to a set of Lebesgue measure zero (see
[
4
]
), we conclude that
\dim_{H}(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta})>0
for almost all
\beta\in(1,2]
.

(In fact the above argument can easily be extended to show that for almost all
\beta\in(1,2]
, there are infinitely many non-transitivity windows.)

Next, we show that for each
k\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}\cup\{\aleph_{0}\}
there are infinitely many
\beta
’s such that
|\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}|=k
. For finite
k
this is an immediate consequence of Theorem
8.1
, because for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
the set
\Lambda_{k}
is infinite. For
k=\aleph_{0}
it follows from Corollary
13.5
, since for each
{\mathbf{S}}\in\Lambda
we can find a base
\beta\in(\beta_{\ell}^{\mathbf{S}},\beta_{*}^{\mathbf{S}})
such that
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
and
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic. (For instance, let
m:=|{\mathbf{S}}|
and take
\alpha(\beta)=\mathbb{L}({\mathbf{S}})^{+}0^{m+1}(10)^{\infty}
.)

Finally, we show that, if
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
is uncountable, then its Hausdorff dimension is strictly positive. The intersection of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
with at least one complementary interval of
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
, say
[t_{1},t_{2})
, must be uncountable. Since the intersection of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
with each
\beta
-Lyndon interval or
\beta
-Lyndon gap contains at most one point, the interval
[t_{1},t_{2})
must contain infinitely many
\beta
-Lyndon intervals. Let
[t_{L},t_{R}]
and
[u_{L},u_{R}]
be two of them, ordered so that
t_{R}<u_{R}
. Let
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
be the
\beta
-Lyndon words such that
b(t_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{v}^{\infty}
and
b(u_{R},\beta)=\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
. Now consider all sequences of the form

\mathbf{v}^{3}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{v}^{k_{1}}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{v}^{k_{2}}\mathbf{w}\mathbf{v}^{k_{3}}\mathbf{w}\dots,\qquad k_{i}\in\{1,2\}\ \forall\,i.

Since
\mathbf{v}^{\infty}\prec\mathbf{w}^{\infty}
, it is easy to see that each such sequence is the
\beta
-greedy expansion of some point
t\in(t_{R},u_{R})\cap\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
. Hence, as in the proof of Theorem
11.7
, it follows that

\dim_{H}(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta})\geq\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t_{1},t_{2})\big)\geq\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap(t_{R},u_{R})\big)>0.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

(i) Let
\beta\in(1,2]
and
t\in\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Then by Theorem
1.1
,

\displaystyle\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,1]\big)=\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t),

\displaystyle\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t+\varepsilon,1]\big)=\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t+\varepsilon).

Since
t\in\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
,
\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t+\varepsilon)<\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
. Hence, using the stability of Hausdorff dimension, it follows that
\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,t+\varepsilon)\big)=\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,1]\big)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)

\displaystyle=\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,1]\big)=\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,t+\varepsilon)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap(t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon)\big)\leq\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t-\varepsilon,1]\big)

\displaystyle=\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t-\varepsilon),

so letting
\varepsilon\searrow 0
and using the continuity of
t\mapsto\dim_{H}K_{\beta}(t)
gives (i).

(ii) Assume
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic; then
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
(or
\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{\prime}(t_{R})
, as appropriate) is sofic for every
\beta
-Lyndon interval
[t_{L},t_{R}]
. Take
t\in\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
. Since
\mathscr{B}_{\beta}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\beta}
, we have

(15.1)

\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{B}_{\beta}\cap(t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon)\big)\leq\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap(t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon)\big).

On the other hand, it follows from our results on
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
and the argument in the proof of Proposition
4.6
that the only intervals with which
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
can have an uncountable intersection are the non-transitivity windows, if they are present. Since
\alpha(\beta)
is eventually periodic, the construction in Section
10
implies that there are at most finitely many non-transitivity windows. Furthermore, since
t\in\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
,
t
cannot be a left endpoint of a non-transitivity window by Proposition
11.6
. Thus, for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, the interval
(t,t+\varepsilon)
does not intersect any non-transitivity window. Hence it contains at most countably many points of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
, and so

(15.2)

\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{B}_{\beta}\cap[t,t+\varepsilon)\big)\geq\dim_{H}\big(\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\cap[t,t+\varepsilon)\big).

From (
15.1
), (
15.2
) and part (i) of the theorem, (ii) follows.
∎

Remark 15.1
.

The reason why we cannot extend Theorem
1.3
(ii) to all
\beta\in(1,2]
has to do with a technical limitation. All our upper bounds for the size of
\mathscr{E}_{\beta}\backslash\mathscr{B}_{\beta}
depend on
[
27
, Corollary 4.4.9]
, which states that, if
\mathcal{X}
is a transitive sofic subshift and
\mathcal{Y}
is a proper subshift of
\mathcal{X}
, then
h(\mathcal{X})>h(\mathcal{Y})
. Without the assumption of
\mathcal{X}
being sofic, this conclusion may fail. When
\alpha(\beta)
is not eventually periodic, the subshift
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\beta}(t_{R})
is not necessarily sofic, and therefore the argument in the proof of Proposition
4.6
does not work. We suspect that nonetheless, Theorem
1.3
(ii) holds for all
\beta\in(1,2]
.
